Two-step nilpotent monodromy of local systems on special varieties
Junyan Cao, Ya Deng, Christopher D. Hacon, Mihai Paun

Abstract
Résumé Let X X be a smooth complex quasi-projective variety that is special in the sense of Campana. We prove that the monodromy group of any complex local system on X X is virtually nilpotent of class at most 2 2 . This result sharply refines a theorem of Cadorel, Yamanoi, and the second author. To establish this result, we develop a deformation theory for certain local systems on quasi-compact Kähler manifolds by constructing universal deformations for such local systems. As a byproduct of our argument, we also show that a general fiber of the quasi-Albanese map of X X is special, extending a result of Campana and Claudon from the projective to the quasi-projective setting.

1
Introduction

1.1
Main theorems

The main goal of this paper is to prove the following.

Theorem A

(=
6.2
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth complex quasi-projective variety. Suppose that
X
is special in the sense of Campana (for example, if its logarithmic Kodaira dimension
\bar{\kappa}(X)=0
). Then for any linear representation
\varrho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
, the image
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
is virtually nilpotent of class at most
2
.

We recall the following definition of nilpotent group.

Definition 1.1

(Nilpotent group)
.

A group
G
is
nilpotent
if it possesses a central series of finite length. This is defined as a series of normal subgroups

\{1\}=G_{0}\trianglelefteq G_{1}\trianglelefteq\cdots\trianglelefteq G_{n}=G

such that
G_{i+1}/G_{i}\leq Z(G/G_{i})
for all
0\leq i<n
. For a nilpotent group
G
, the minimal integer
n
for which a central series of length
n
exists is called the
nilpotency class
of
G
. Such a group is said to be
nilpotent of class
n
. We say that
G
is
n
-step nilpotent
if it is nilpotent of class less than or equal to
n
. In particular, 1-step nilpotent groups are abelian.

Previously, in
[
CDY25c
]
, Cadorel, Yamanoi, and the second author proved that for any
\varrho
as in
A
, the image
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
is virtually nilpotent. Moreover, they constructed examples of special varieties whose fundamental groups are
2
-step nilpotent but not virtually abelian.
Later, in
[
DY25
]
, Yamanoi and the second author proved that for any representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(K)
where
K
is a field of positive characteristic, the image
\tau(\pi_{1}(X))
is virtually abelian. With these results in place,
A
completes the picture for the monodromies of local systems of arbitrary characteristic over special quasi-projective varieties.

One motivation for this paper is the following fundamental question
concerning fundamental groups of complex algebraic varieties.

Question 1.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth complex quasi-projective variety.
Assume that its fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
is nilpotent.
Is
\pi_{1}(X)
necessarily
2
-step nilpotent ?

Since every finitely generated nilpotent group is linear,
A
thus yields a positive answer to
1.2
for the class of special varieties.

We remark that
1.2
was originally asked by Campana
[
Cam95
, Remarque 1.5]

for compact Kähler manifolds, and only recently raised in its present form for quasi-projective varieties in his joint work with Aguilar
[
AAC25
, Question 25]
. Very recently, there have been some interesting works regarding
1.2
by Shimoji
[
Shi25
]
, who provided a positive answer when the rank of
\pi_{1}(X)
is at most seven. In
[
Rog25
]
, Rogov proposed a strategy to study
1.2
using o-minimality, combined with Hain’s higher Albanese maps
[
Hai87
]
and the unipotent variations of mixed Hodge structures of Hain–Zucker
[
HZ87
]
.

In the present paper, our approach to proving
A
is essentially self-contained and does not rely on any of these preceding strategies or tools. A key novelty of our work is the establishment of a
2
-step phenomenon in non-abelian Hodge theory
. We begin by clarifying this terminology with the following definition.

Definition 1.3

(Quasi-compact Kähler)
.

A complex manifold
X
is called
quasi-compact Kähler
if it is a Zariski open subset of a compact Kähler manifold
\overline{X}
, which we refer to as a
compactification
of
X
. A holomorphic map between two quasi-compact Kähler manifolds is called
compactifiable
if it extends to a meromorphic map between their compactifications.

The following theorem is a simplified version of the so-called
2-step phenomenon
. For a more general statement, see
5.25
.

Theorem B

(=
6.1
)
.

Let
X
,
Y
, and
Z
be quasi-compact Kähler manifolds. Let
f\colon Y\to X
and
g\colon Z\to Y
be compactifiable holomorphic maps. Assume that the images of these maps under the respective quasi-Albanese maps are points ; that is,

\operatorname{alb}_{X}(f(Y))=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
and
\operatorname{alb}_{Y}(g(Z))=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
,
where
\operatorname{alb}_{X}\colon X\to\operatorname{Alb}(X)
and
\operatorname{alb}_{Y}\colon Y\to\operatorname{Alb}(Y)
denote the quasi-Albanese maps of
X
and
Y
, respectively.

Then, for any
N\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and any representation
\tau\colon\pi_{1}(X,x)\to\operatorname{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
belonging to the irreducible component of the representation variety
\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{1}(X,x),\operatorname{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C}))
that contains the trivial representation, the pullback representation
(f\circ g)^{*}\tau:\pi_{1}(Z)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is trivial. In particular, for any unipotent representation
\tau
, the pullback
(f\circ g)^{*}\tau
is trivial.

A more conceptual formulation of
B
is given in
C
. We refer the reader to
[
Fuj24
]
for the precise definition and properties of quasi-Albanese maps.

To prove
B
, we develop a deformation theory for certain local systems on quasi-compact Kähler manifolds
X
. More precisely, inspired by the celebrated work of Eyssidieux–Simpson
[
ES11
]
, we construct a
canonical and universal
connection for extendable unitary local systems on
X
. Since the precise statements are somewhat technical, we only state a special case here and refer the reader to
5.21
and
5.23
for the general formulations.

Theorem C

(
\subset
5.21
,
5.23
and
5.25
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
be a simple normal crossing divisor on
\overline{X}
. Set
X:=\overline{X}\setminus D
and fix a base point
x\in X
. Let
(V,\nabla)
be a trivial flat bundle of rank
N
on
\overline{X}
and denote the associated endomorphism bundle by
(E,\nabla):=(\End(V),\nabla)
.

Let
Z
be the affine subvariety of the affine space underlying the complex vector space
H^{1}(\overline{X},E)\times H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
defined by the following quasi-homogeneous equations for
(\alpha,\beta)\in H^{1}(\overline{X},E)\times H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
of type
(1,2)
:

\displaystyle g(\beta)+\frac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]

\displaystyle=0\in H^{2}(\overline{X},E),

\displaystyle[\iota^{*}\alpha,\beta]

\displaystyle=0\in H^{1}(D^{(1)},E),

\displaystyle\bigl[\iota_{ij}^{*}(\beta|_{D_{i}}),\iota_{ji}^{*}(\beta|_{D_{j}})\bigr]

\displaystyle=0\in H^{0}(D_{i}\cap D_{j},E)\quad\text{for any }1\leq i<j\leq m,

where
D^{(1)}:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
, and
\iota\colon D^{(1)}\to\overline{X}
is the natural morphism. Here,
g\colon H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)\to H^{2}(X,E)
denotes the Gysin morphism (cf.
5.3
), and
\iota_{ij}\colon D_{i}\cap D_{j}\to D_{i}
is the inclusion map.

Then there exists an open ball
B\subset H^{1}(\overline{X},E)\times H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
centered at the origin
O
such that, over the analytic open subset
\mathcal{T}:=Z\cap B
, there exists a
canonical
and
analytic
family of logarithmic connections
\nabla_{(\alpha,\beta)}
on
V
, parametrized by
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathcal{T}
. These connections are flat over
X_{0}
and satisfy the following property :
The natural map defined by the monodromy representations of the flat connections
(V|_{X},\nabla_{(\alpha,\beta)})

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}

\displaystyle\to\Hom(\pi_{1}(X,x),\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}))

\displaystyle(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{Mon}(\nabla_{(\alpha,\beta)})

is a biholomorphism onto an open neighborhood of the trivial representation in the representation variety

\Hom(\pi_{1}(X,x),\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}))
.

Furthermore, we can regard
\nabla_{(\alpha,\beta)}
as defining a representation

\varrho_{\mathcal{T}}\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},O}),

where
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},O}
is the local ring of
\mathcal{T}
at the origin
O
. Let
\mathcal{V}
be the local system defined by the composition

\pi_{1}(X)\xrightarrow{\varrho_{\mathcal{T}}}\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},O})\to\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},O}/\mathfrak{m}^{3}),

where
\mathfrak{m}
denotes the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},O}
. This local system has 2-step nilpotent monodromy. Moreover, it satisfies the following property : if
f\colon Y\to X
is a compactifiable holomorphic map from a quasi-Kähler manifold
Y
such that the pullback
f^{*}\mathcal{V}
is trivial, then the pullback representation
f^{*}\tau\colon\pi_{1}(Y)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is also trivial for any representation
\tau\colon\pi_{1}(X,x)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
belonging to the irreducible component of the representation variety
\Hom(\pi_{1}(X,x),\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}))
that contains the trivial representation.

We refer the reader to
5.21
for the precise formula for
\nabla_{(\alpha,\beta)}
. Notably, when
X
is compact, the above quasi-homogeneous equations reduce to the homogeneous quadratic equation
[\alpha,\alpha]=0
.
C
provides an analytic viewpoint and a concrete construction of the equivalence of analytic germs between the representation variety
\Hom(\pi_{1}(X,x),\GL_{N})
and the quadratic cone in
H^{1}(X,E)
defined by
[\alpha,\alpha]=0
, a result originally established by Goldman and Millson
[
GM88
]
and Eyssidieux and Simpson
[
ES11
]
. The compact case of
C
is covered by the more general framework in
4.4
. We refer the reader to
5.21
for the precise formula for
\nabla_{(\alpha,\beta)}
.
Subsequently, we establish the
1-step phenomenon
for local systems on
compact Kähler
manifolds in
4.5
. Together, these phenomena provide a conceptually unifying framework that allows us to reprove several known results in a uniform way :

(1)

the theorems of Katzarkov
[
Kat97
]
and Leroy
[
Ler99
]
establishing the Shafarevich conjecture for compact Kähler manifolds with nilpotent fundamental groups (see
4.10
) ;

(2)

more generally, the theorems of Green–Griffiths–Katzarkov
[
GGK24
]
and Aguilar–Campana
[
AAC25
]
on the holomorphic convexity of the universal covers of quasi-compact Kähler manifolds with nilpotent fundamental groups and
proper
quasi-Albanese maps (see
5.26
) ;

(3)

Campana’s theorem
[
Cam04
]
on the virtual abelianity of the monodromy of local systems over special smooth projective varieties (see
4.11
) ; and

(4)

a structure theorem for the linear Shafarevich morphism (see
4.12
), established implicitly in the work of Eyssidieux, Katzarkov, Pantev, and Ramachandran on the linear Shafarevich conjecture
[
EKPR12
]
, and later applied by Wang and the second author
[
DW24
]
in their proof of the linear Kollár conjecture on the positivity of the holomorphic Euler characteristic for varieties with big fundamental groups.

In the course of proving
C
, we derive several ancillary results, including the construction of explicit zigzags of
1
-quasi-isomorphisms
between the differential graded Lie algebras (dglas) associated with
E
-valued logarithmic complexes and the cohomology complexes. We present this construction here due to its independent interest.

Theorem D

(=
5.19
)
.

Let
\overline{X}
be a compact Kähler
n
-fold and let
D=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
be a simple normal crossing divisor on
\overline{X}
. Let
(V,\nabla)
be a unitary flat vector bundle on
\overline{X}
and denote the associated endomorphism bundle by
(E,\nabla):=(\End(V),\nabla)
. Then for the following maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{0}(\overline{X},D,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{2n}(\overline{X},D,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{0}(\overline{X},D,E)\cap\ker\nabla^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{2n}(\overline{X},D,E)\cap\ker\nabla^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(D^{(0)},E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus_{p+q=2n}H^{q}(D^{(p)},E)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

the vertical morphisms
\phi
and
\psi
are both
1
-equivalences (see
3.2
) connecting the three horizontal dgla complexes.
Here :

—

\phi
denotes the inclusion map ;

—

D^{(k)}
denotes the disjoint union of all
k
-fold intersections

D_{I}:=D_{i_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap D_{i_{k}},

where
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
is a multi-index satisfying
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq m
, with the convention
D^{(0)}:=\overline{X}
;

—

the differential
d
in the bottom chain complex is given by the Gysin morphism with suitable signs (see (
5.15.1
)), and the Lie brackets are defined in (
) ;

—

for any
\eta\in A^{k}(\overline{X},D,E)
, the map
\psi(\eta)
is defined by

\psi(\eta):=\left((-1)^{|I|}\{\mathcal{H}(\Res_{D_{I}}\eta)\}\right)_{I}\in H^{k-|I|}(D_{I},E),

where
I
ranges over all ordered multi-indices contained in
\{1,\ldots,m\}
, and
\mathcal{H}
denotes the
harmonic projection
of the residue
\Res_{D_{I}}\eta\in A^{k-|I|}(D_{I},\sum_{j\notin I}D_{j}\cap D_{I},E)
.

In particular, this refines previous results by Morgan
[
Mor78
]
, Pridham
[
Pri16
]
, and Lefèvre
[
Lef19
]
, while avoiding the use of either Sullivan’s minimal models or
L_{\infty}
-algebras. To the best of our knowledge, this provides the first explicit construction of zigzags connecting the dglas of
E
-valued logarithmic complexes to their cohomology complexes, establishing a 1-quasi-equivalence.

In a forthcoming paper
[
CDHP26
]
, we will extend
C
to a more general setting, with
(V,\nabla)
be replaced by a semisimple flat bundle defined over
X
.

In the course of the proof of
A
, we need to establish a result in
birational geometry within Campana’s theory of special varieties and orbifolds. We state it here because of its independent interest ; its
proof is independent of the other results stated above.

Theorem E

(=
7.15
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety that is special. Then a general fiber of its quasi-Albanese map (which is dominant with connected general fibers) is also special.

Note that Campana–Claudon
[
CC16
]
proved
E
in the case
where
X
is projective.

Finally, we state a structure theorem for quasi-projective special varieties
admitting a big local system, which is a consequence of the proof of

A
.

Corollary F

(=
6.3
)
.

Let
X
be a special smooth quasi-projective variety admitting a
big
representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
(cf.
4.8
).
Then, after replacing
X
by a suitable finite étale cover, for a very general fiber
Y
of the quasi-Albanese map
\mathrm{alb}_{X}:X\to\mathrm{Alb}(X)
,
its quasi-Albanese map
\mathrm{alb}_{Y}:Y\to\mathrm{Alb}(Y)
is birational.

The logical dependencies among the main theorems are summarized in
Figure

1
.

5.14

[
CDY25c
]

Logical dependencies between the main theorems

1.2
Idea of the proof of
A

In this subsection, we outline the main ideas of the proof of
A
, utilizing the deformation theory of local systems on quasi-compact Kähler manifolds presented above.

By
[
CDY25c
]
, after replacing
X
by a finite étale cover, the Zariski closure of the image
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
decomposes as a direct product
U\times T
, where
U
is a unipotent algebraic group and
T
is an algebraic torus (see
2.11
). Hence, it suffices to prove that the image of any unipotent representation
\sigma:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is 2-step nilpotent.

According to
[
CDY25c
]
(see
proposition

2.13
), the quasi-Albanese map
a:X\to A
is dominant with connected general fibers and is
\pi_{1}
-exact ; that is, we have a short exact sequence

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(A)\to 0,

(1.3.1)

where
Y
is a general fiber of
a
. By
E
,
Y
is also special, and by
proposition

2.13
again, the quasi-Albanese map
a_{Y}:Y\to A_{Y}
of
Y
is also
\pi_{1}
-exact. Let
F
be a general fiber of
a_{Y}
.

By
B
, we have

\sigma\left([\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(X)]\right)=0.

The
\pi_{1}
-exactness of
a_{Y}
yields the short exact sequence

\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(A_{Y})\to 0.

It follows that the representation

\iota^{*}\sigma:\pi_{1}(Y)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})

factors through
\pi_{1}(A_{Y})
, thus has an abelian image. Here
\iota:Y\hookrightarrow X
denotes the inclusion. Finally, by the
\pi_{1}
-exactness of
a
in (
1.3.1
), one can see that
\sigma(\pi_{1}(X))
is 2-step nilpotent.

Notations and conventions

Throughout this paper, unless otherwise stated, we assume that
(X,\omega)
is a compact Kähler manifold of dimension
n
and let
D=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
be a simple normal crossing divisor on
X
. We set
X_{0}:=X\setminus D
.

Throughout this paper, we adopt the convention that any multi-index
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
consists of
distinct entries
in
\{1,\ldots,m\}
.
We say that
I
is
ordered
if
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
. For any
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
, denote by
z_{I}:=z_{i_{1}}\cdots z_{i_{k}}
. For such an index, we define the intersection

D_{I}:=D_{i_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap D_{i_{k}}.

For any integer
k\geq 1
, let
D^{(k)}
denote the disjoint union of all intersections indexed by ordered multi-indices of length
k
:

D^{(k)}:=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}|I|=k\\
I\text{ ordered}\end{subarray}}D_{I}.

In particular,
D^{(1)}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
.

—

Let
(V,\nabla)
be a flat bundle of rank
N
on
X
. Unless otherwise mentioned, we denote the associated endomorphism bundle by
(E,\nabla):=(\End(V),\nabla)
, and we write
H^{k}(X,E)
for the de Rham cohomology of
E
with respect to
\nabla
. Throughout this paper, except in
section

4
, we assume that the flat bundle
(V,\nabla)
is unitary.

—

For any unitary flat bundle
(V,\nabla)
on
X
, we write

\nabla=\nabla^{\prime}+\nabla^{\prime\prime}

for the decomposition of
\nabla
into its
(1,0)
- and
(0,1)
-parts. We endow
E=\End(V)
with the holomorphic vector bundle structure induced by the
(0,1)
-component
\nabla^{\prime\prime}
.

—

A local system
\mathcal{L}
on
X\backslash D
is called
extendable
if it extends to a local system on
{X}
.

—

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)
be the sheaf of
E
-valued smooth differential forms
with at most logarithmic poles along
D
. We write
A^{k}(X,E)
for the space of smooth

E
-valued
k
-forms,
A^{k}(X,D,E)
for those with at most logarithmic poles along
D
,

\mathscr{D}^{k}(X,E)
for the space of
E
-valued currents of degree
k
,

A^{k}_{\mathrm{cts}}(X,E)
for the space of continuous
E
-valued differential forms of degree
k
, and

\mathcal{H}^{k}(X,E)
for the space of
E
-valued harmonic
k
-forms.

—

For any
\eta\in A^{k}(X,D,V)
, we denote by
\bm{\nabla}\eta
(resp.
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\eta
and
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\eta
, noting the bold font) its covariant derivative in the sense of currents. In general,
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\eta
may contain nontrivial residue terms supported on
D
, whereas
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\eta=\nabla^{\prime}\eta
.

—

Any element
\eta\in A^{k}(X,D,E)
is called
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact if
\eta=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\sigma
for some
\sigma\in\mathscr{D}^{k-1}(X,E)
.

—

For any closed form
\eta\in\mathscr{D}^{k}(X,E)
with
\bm{\nabla}\eta=0
, we let
\mathcal{H}(\eta)
denote its harmonic projection, which is the unique harmonic representative of the class
\{\eta\}\in H^{k}(X,E)
. By abuse of notation, if
\alpha\in H^{k}(X,E)
is a cohomology class, we also write
\mathcal{H}(\alpha)
for its unique harmonic representative.

—

Let
W_{\bullet}\mathcal{A}^{k}(X,D,E)\subset\mathcal{A}^{k}(X,D,E)
be the increasing filtration induced by the number
of pure logarithmic factors, and let
W_{\bullet}A^{k}(X,D,E)
denote its space of global
sections on
X
.

—

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}_{X,D}(E)
be the kernel of the residue morphism

\Res_{D}\colon W_{1}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\bullet-1}(D^{(1)},E).

—

Fix a base point
x\in X_{0}
. We denote the topological fundamental group of
X_{0}
by
\pi_{1}(X_{0},x)
. By abuse of notation, we usually omit the base point when the context is clear. Furthermore, we let
\mathfrak{g}:=E_{x}
denote the fiber of
E
at
x
, which carries a natural Lie algebra structure.

—

Let
R(X_{0},N)\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}(\pi_{1}(X_{0}),\operatorname{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C}))
denote the variety of representations of the fundamental group of
X_{0}
into
\operatorname{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.

—

A group
G
is said to be virtually
P
if it contains a subgroup of finite index
that has property
P
.

—

For brevity, we write dgla (resp. dglas) for differential graded Lie algebra(s).

—

We denote by
\Art
the category of Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebras, and
\Grp
the category of groupoids. For any Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
A
, denote by
\mathfrak{m}
its maximal ideal.

—

For any
\varepsilon>0
, let
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}^{p+q}
denote the ball of radius
\varepsilon
centered at the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{p+q}
.

—

An
algebraic fiber space

f\colon X\to Y
between smooth projective varieties is a surjective morphism with connected fibers. For a divisor
E
on
Y
, we let
f^{*}E
denote the pullback divisor and
f^{-1}(E):=(f^{*}E)_{\mathrm{red}}
denote its reduced support.

—

For any quasi-compact Kähler manifold
Y
, we denote by
\mathrm{alb}_{Y}:Y\to\mathrm{Alb}(Y)
its quasi-Albanese map.

—

A birational morphism
f:X\to Y
between smooth quasi-projective varieties is said to be
proper over a big open subset
if there exists an open subset
U\subset Y
with
\operatorname{codim}_{Y}(Y\setminus U)\geq 2
such that the restriction
f:f^{-1}(U)\to U
is a proper morphism.

2
Technical Preliminaries

2.1
Special varieties and orbifolds

Special varieties are introduced by Campana
[
Cam04
,
Cam11
]
in his remarkable program of classification of geometric orbifolds. We collect some facts about orbifolds from
[
Cam04
,
Cam11
]
.

Recall that a (geometric) orbifold
(X,D)
is a pair consisting of a variety
X
and a
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
D=\sum c_{i}P_{i}
, where the
P_{i}
are prime divisors and the coefficients
0\leq c_{i}\leq 1
are of the form
c_{i}=1-\frac{1}{m_{i}}
for some
m_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{+\infty\}
. We will say that
(X,D)
is
log smooth
(or just
smooth
) if
X
is non-singular and the support of
D
has simple normal crossings. The multiplicities of
D
along
P_{i}
are
m_{i}=m_{P_{i}}(D)=\frac{1}{1-c_{i}}
so that

D=\sum c_{i}P_{i}=\sum(1-\frac{1}{m_{i}})P_{i}.

Note that
m_{i}\in[1,+\infty]
and
m_{i}=+\infty
coincides with the case
c_{i}=1
whilst
m_{i}=1
when
c_{i}=0
.

Definition 2.1

(Multiplicity and Orbifold base)
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a surjective morphism of normal varieties with connected fibers. Let
P
be a prime divisor on
X
. We say
P
is
f
-exceptional
if
\operatorname{codim}_{Y}f(P)\geq 2
. If
P
dominates
Y
, it is called
horizontal
; otherwise, it is
vertical
. If
P
is an
f
-vertical divisor that is not
f
-exceptional, then its image
f(P)=Q
is a prime divisor on
Y
. Restricting to the regular loci
X^{0}\subset X
and
Y^{0}\subset Y
, we can write the pullback of
Q
as

(f^{0})^{*}Q=m(f,P)P+F

where
F
is an effective divisor whose support does not contain
P
. We define the integer
m(f,P)
to be the
multiplicity of
f
along
P
.

Suppose now that
Y
is smooth, and
(X,D)
is a smooth orbifold, where the boundary divisor is written as
D=\sum(1-\frac{1}{m_{P}(D)})P
with multiplicities
m_{P}(D)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
. We define the
orbifold base
(or
orbifold divisor of
f
) as the
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor on
Y
given by (cf.
[
CC16
, Définition A.3]
) :

\Delta(f,D)=\sum_{Q}\left(1-\frac{1}{m_{f}(Q)}\right)Q

where the sum runs over all prime divisors
Q\subset Y
, and the
multiplicity
of
\Delta(f,D)
along
Q
is defined as :

m_{Q}\left(\Delta(f,D)\right)=m_{f}(Q):=\inf_{f(P)=Q}\big\{m(f,P)\cdot m_{P}(D)\big\}.

Definition 2.2

(Orbifold morphism)
.

If
(X,D_{X})
and
(Y,D_{Y})
are log smooth orbifolds and
f:X\to Y
is a morphism such that
m_{i}m_{P_{i}}(D_{X})\geq m_{Q}(D_{Y})
for every divisor
Q
on
Y
and
f^{*}(Q)=\sum m_{i}P_{i}
then we say that
f:(X,D_{X})\to(Y,D_{Y})
is an
orbifold morphism
. If
f
is birational and
f_{*}D_{X}=D_{Y}
, then we say that
f
is
birational morphism
between orbifolds. A birational morphism that is also an orbifold morphism is called an
elementary birational orbifold morphism
.

Note that if
D_{Y}=\sum Q_{i}
is a reduced divisor and
f:(X,D_{X})\to(Y,D_{Y})
is an orbifold morphism, then
n_{i}=\infty
and so
m_{P_{j}}=\infty
for any
P_{j}
in the support of
f^{*}(Q_{i})
, i.e.
\lfloor D_{X}\rfloor\geq{\rm Supp}(f^{*}D_{Y})
.
Note that it is not always the case that
f:(X,D)\to(Y,\Delta(f,D))
is an orbifold morphism.

Definition 2.3

(
[
Cam11
, Définition 4.10]
)
.

Let
g:(Z,\Delta)\rightarrow Y
be a fibration, with
(Z,\Delta)
and
Y
smooth. We say that
g
is
neat
(relative to
w
) if there exists a diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z,\Delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

in which :

—

w
is an orbifold morphism,
v
and
w
are bimeromorphic,
Z^{\prime},Y,Y^{\prime}
are smooth, and
w_{*}(\Delta)=\Delta^{\prime}
;

—

every
g
-exceptional divisor of
Z
is
w
-exceptional.

We say that
g
is
neat
if it is neat relative to a fibration
g^{\prime}
as above.

We say that
g
is
neat and high
if it is neat, and if
g:(Z,\Delta)\rightarrow(Y,\Delta(g,\Delta))
is an orbifold morphism.

We say that
g
is
strictly neat
if it is neat, and if, moreover, its orbifold base is smooth.

Definition 2.4

(
[
Cam11
, Définition 5.7]
)
.

We say that
f
and
f^{\prime}
are
birationally elementarily equivalent
if there exists a commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

in which
u,v
are bimeromorphic,
u,v
are holomorphic, and
v
is an an elementary birational orbifold morphism.

More generally,
f
and
f^{\prime}
are equivalent (we then denote
f\sim f^{\prime}
) if they belong to the equivalence relation generated by such diagrams.

Proposition 2.5

(
[
Cam11
, Proposition 5.9]
)
.

Consider the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y,D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

in which
u
and
v
are birational, and
v
is an orbifold morphism such that
v_{*}(D^{\prime})=D
. Then we have

(i)

u_{*}\left(\Delta(f^{\prime},D^{\prime})\right)=\Delta(f,D)
;

(ii)

\kappa\left(X,\Delta(f,D)\right)\geqslant\kappa\left(X^{\prime},\Delta(f^{\prime},D^{\prime})\right)
. ∎

Note that the strict inequality in
2.5.(ii)
can indeed occur ; thus, the Kodaira dimension of the orbifold base of a fibration is not, in general, a birational invariant. We therefore introduce the following definition (cf.
[
Cam11
, Définitions 5.10 & 5.16]
) :

Definition 2.6

(Canonical dimension)
.

Let
f:(Y,D)\to X
be a fibration where both the orbifold pair
(Y,D)
and the base
X
are smooth. We define the
canonical dimension
of the orbifold fibration
f
as :

\kappa(f,D):=\inf_{f^{\prime}\sim f}\{\kappa(X^{\prime},\Delta(f^{\prime},D^{\prime}))\}

where
f^{\prime}:(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})\to X^{\prime}
ranges over all models that are birationally equivalent to
f
, as in
2.4
. By definition,
\kappa(f,D)
is a birational invariant of the fibration. If
\kappa(f,D)=\dim(X)
, we say that
f
is a
fibration of general type
.

By
2.5.(ii)
, we generally have the inequality :

\kappa(f,D)\leq\kappa(X,\Delta(f,D)).

Equality is achieved when the fibration is neat :

Lemma 2.7

(
[
Cam11
, Corollaire 5.11.(3)]
)
.

If the fibration
f
is neat, then :

\kappa(f,D)=\kappa(X,\Delta(f,D)).

Proposition 2.8

(
[
Cam11
, Proposition 4.10]
)
.

Let
g^{\prime}:\left(Z^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime}\right)\rightarrow Y^{\prime}
be a morphism from a projective orbifold to a projective normal variety. Then there exists a commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z,\Delta)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Z^{\prime},\Delta^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}

such that
w
and
v
are both birational,
(Z,\Delta)
and
Y
are both smooth, and
g
is strictly neat and high.∎

Definition 2.9

(Special variety)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a smooth projective orbifold, then
(X,D)
is
special
if, for every elementary birational orbifold morphism
u:(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow(X,D)
and every neat fibration
f:(X^{\prime},D^{\prime})\longrightarrow Y
with
\dim(Y)>0
, the following inequality holds :

\kappa\left(Y,K_{Y}+\Delta(f,D^{\prime})\right)<\dim(Y).

In other words,
(X,D)
is special if it does not admit any fibration of general type in the sense of
2.6
.

We will need the following result of Campana
[
Cam11
, Théorème 10.1]
.

Theorem 2.10

(Relative core map)
.

Let
(X,D)
be a smooth projective orbifold, and let
f\colon X\to S
be an algebraic fiber space over a smooth projective variety
S
. Then there exists an almost holomorphic fibration
c\colon X\dasharrow C
over
S
such that :

(i)

for the orbifold base
(C,D_{C})
induced by
c\colon(X,D)\dasharrow C
(after possibly replacing
c
by a birational model as in
2.4
), the general fibers
(C_{s},D_{C_{s}})
of the induced map
(C,D_{C})\to S
are of general type ; and

(ii)

the general fibers
(X_{c},D_{c})
of
c\colon(X,D)\dasharrow C
are special.

The map
c\colon X\dasharrow C=:C(X,D/S)
is called the relative core of
(X,D)
over
S
. Such a relative core map is unique up to birational equivalence.

Démonstration.

This follows easily from
[
Cam11
, Théorème 10.1]
, which establishes the absolute case where
\dim(S)=0
. We include the details for the reader.

By
proposition

2.8
, we may replace
f\colon X\to S
by a neat model. Let
H
be a sufficiently ample divisor on
S
in general position. Then
f
is clearly a fibration of general type for the pair
(X,D+f^{*}H)
. It follows that if
c\colon X\dasharrow C
is the absolute core of
(X,D+f^{*}H)
, then
f
factors through
c
(see
[
Cam11
, Corollaire 9.13]
). Up to replacing
c
with a birationally equivalent model as in
2.4
, we may assume that it is a neat morphism. This yields the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

The general fibers of
c\colon(X,D)\to C
agree with the general fibers of
c\colon(X,D+f^{*}H)\to C
. Since
c
is the absolute core of
(X,D+f^{*}H)
, these general fibers are special by
[
Cam11
, Théorème 10.1]
. This proves Item (ii).

For the orbifold base
\Delta(c,D+f^{*}H)
of
c\colon(X,D+f^{*}H)\to C
, we note that the difference

\Delta(c,D+f^{*}H)-\Delta(c,D)

is an effective divisor entirely supported on
g^{-1}(H)
. Consequently, the general fibers of the induced fibrations
(C,\Delta(c,D+f^{*}H))\to S
and
(C,\Delta(c,D))\to S
coincide.

Since
c
is a neat morphism and is the absolute core of
(X,D+f^{*}H)
, its orbifold base
(C,\Delta(c,D+f^{*}H))
is of general type. It is a standard property that the general fibers of an orbifold of general type over any base are again of general type. Hence, the general fibers of
(C,\Delta(c,D+f^{*}H))\to S
, and thus the general fibers of
(C,\Delta(c,D))\to S
, are of general type. This proves Item (i).

Finally, the uniqueness of
c
up to birational equivalence follows directly from
[
Cam11
, Corollaire 9.13]
. The theorem is proved.∎

In
[
CDY25c
]
, the authors conjectured that smooth special quasi-projective varieties have virtually nilpotent fundamental groups, and prove the following :

Theorem 2.11

(
[
CDY25c
, Theorem A]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety that is special and let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a representation. Then after replacing
X
by a finite étale cover, the Zariski closure of the image
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
decomposes as a direct product
U\times T
, where
U
is a unipotent algebraic group, and
T
is an algebraic torus. In particular,
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
is a nilpotent group. ∎

In this paper, we propose a refined version of the above nilpotency conjecture.

Conjecture 2.12

(2-step nilpotency conjecture)
.

Let
X
be a smooth special quasi-projective variety. Then
\pi_{1}(X)
is virtually nilpotent of class at most
2
.

We will need the following result on the
\pi_{1}
-exactness
of quasi-Albanese maps of special varieties in the proof of
A
.

Proposition 2.13

(
[
CDY25c
, Proposition 4.13]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety that is special. Then its quasi-Albanese map
a:X\to A
is
\pi_{1}
-exact, i.e.,
a
is a dominant morphism with connected general fibers, such that we have the following short exact sequence

\pi_{1}(F,x)\to\pi_{1}(X,x)\to\pi_{1}(A,a(x))\to 0,

where
F
is a general smooth fiber, and
x\in F
is a base point. ∎

2.2
Hodge decomposition for currents and
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-fold, and let
(E,\nabla)
be a semisimple flat bundle on
X
. By Simpson
[
Sim92
]
, there exists a harmonic metric
h
on
E
. This metric induces a decomposition
\nabla=\nabla^{\prime}+\nabla^{\prime\prime}
such that the following identities hold :

\Delta=2\Delta^{\prime}=2\Delta^{\prime\prime}.

Here, the total Laplacian is defined as
\Delta:=\nabla^{*}\nabla+\nabla\nabla^{*}
, and the partial Laplacians
\Delta^{\prime}
and
\Delta^{\prime\prime}
are defined analogously using
\nabla^{\prime}
and
\nabla^{\prime\prime}
respectively. Note that the splitting
\nabla=\nabla^{\prime}+\nabla^{\prime\prime}
generally differs from the decomposition of
\nabla
into its standard
(1,0)
- and
(0,1)
-parts, unless
(E,\nabla)
is unitary.

Definition 2.14
.

We denote by
\mathscr{D}^{k}(X,E)
the space of
E
-valued currents of degree
k
on
X
. It is the topological dual of the space of smooth global forms of degree
2n-k
with values in the dual bundle
E^{*}
:

\mathscr{D}^{k}(X,E):=\big({A}^{2n-k}(X,E^{*})\big)^{\prime}.

The space of test forms
{A}^{2n-k}(X,E^{*})
is equipped with the natural Fréchet topology, i.e. the topology of uniform convergence of all derivatives.

Note that if
D
is a simple normal crossing divisor on
X
, then we have the inclusion

A^{k}(X,D,E)\subset\mathscr{D}^{k}(X,E).

Let
G
denote the Green operator associated with
\Delta^{\prime}
. We can extend the operators

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime},\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime},\Delta^{\prime}
, and
G
to act on the space of
E
-valued currents of degree
k
. Since
X
is compact Kähler, we have the following identities :

\displaystyle[\bm{\nabla}^{\prime},G]=0,\quad

\displaystyle[\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime},G]=0,

(2.14.1)

\displaystyle[\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime},(\bm{\nabla}^{\prime})^{*}]=0,\quad

\displaystyle[\bm{\nabla}^{\prime},(\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime})^{*}]=0,

\displaystyle\alpha=\mathcal{H}\alpha+\Delta^{\prime}G\alpha,

where
\mathcal{H}
is the harmonic projection.

Theorem 2.15

(De Rham-Kodaira decomposition for currents)
.

Given a current
T\in\mathscr{D}^{k}(X,E)
, we have the following Hodge decomposition

T=\mathcal{H}(T)+\Delta^{\prime}G(T),

(2.15.1)

where
G
is the Green operator with respect to the Laplacian
\Delta^{\prime}:=[\bm{\nabla}^{\prime},\bm{\nabla}^{\prime*}]
.

Démonstration.

For the proof, we refer to
[
dRK50
]
or
[
CP23
, Section 3]
.
∎

Lemma 2.16

(
\partial\bar{\partial}
-lemma)
.

Let
u\in\mathscr{D}^{k}(X,E)
such that
\bm{\nabla}u=0
as currents on
X
.
If
u=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v
, or
u=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}v
on
X
for some current
v\in\mathscr{D}^{k-1}(X,E)
, then we have

u=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\sigma

for some
\sigma\in\mathscr{D}^{k-2}(X,E)
. In particular, if
k=1
, then
u=0
.

Démonstration.

We suppose that
u=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v
for some current
v
. By applying Theorem
2.15
to
v
, we have

v=v_{0}+\bm{\nabla}v_{1}+\bm{\nabla}^{*}v_{2},

where
v_{0}
is harmonic and
v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathscr{D}^{k-2}(X,E)
.
Then we have

u=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}v_{1}+\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{*}v_{2}.

Then

\bm{\nabla}^{*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{2}=u+\bm{\nabla}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{1}\in\ker\bm{\nabla}.

Therefore

\Delta(\bm{\nabla}^{*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{2})=\bm{\nabla}^{*}\bm{\nabla}(\bm{\nabla}^{*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{2})+\bm{\nabla}\bm{\nabla}^{*}(\bm{\nabla}^{*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{2})=0.

Then by the elliptic regulairty,
\bm{\nabla}^{*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{2}
is smooth and harmonic. Then we have

\langle\bm{\nabla}^{*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{2},\bm{\nabla}^{*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{2}\rangle=\langle\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{2},\bm{\nabla}\bm{\nabla}^{*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{2}\rangle=0.

Then
\bm{\nabla}^{*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}v_{2}=0
and

u=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}v_{1}.

The lemma is thus proved.
∎

3
A quick tour of Goldman-Millson theory

In this section, we briefly recall some essential ingredients of (Deligne–)Goldman–Millson theory used in the proof of
B
and
C
.

3.1
Transformation groupoids

Recall that a (transformation)
groupoid
is a small category in which all morphisms are isomorphisms. Let
({X},{G})
be a transformation groupoid and let
Y
be a set upon which
G
acts. Then the transformation groupoid
({X}\times{Y},{G})
, where
G
acts on
{X}\times{Y}
by the diagonal action is a new groupoid which we denote by
({X},{G})\bowtie{Y}
. If
\varphi:({X}^{\prime},{G}^{\prime})\to({X},{G})
is a morphism of transformation groupoids and Y is a
G
-set, then there is a corresponding morphism of transformation groupoids

\varphi\bowtie{Y}:({X}^{\prime},{G}^{\prime})\bowtie{Y}\to({X},{G})\bowtie{Y}

where the
{G}^{\prime}
-action on
Y
is induced from the
G
-action on
Y
by the homomorphism
\varphi:{G}^{\prime}\to{G}
.

Lemma 3.1

(
[
GM88
, Lemma 3.8]
)
.

If
\varphi:({X}^{\prime},{G}^{\prime})\to({X},{G})
is an equivalence of groupoids, then
\varphi\bowtie{Y}:({X}^{\prime},{G}^{\prime})\bowtie{Y}\to({X},{G})\bowtie{Y}
is also an equivalence of groupoids. ∎

Let us recall the definition of equivalence of categories
[
GM88
, p. 52]
. If
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{B}
are categories, then a functor
F:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
is an
equivalence
if it satisfies three basic properties :

(a)

F
is surjective on isomorphism classes, i.e. the induced map
F_{*}:\mathrm{Iso}\,\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathrm{Iso}\,\mathscr{B}
is surjective ;

(b)

F
is full, i.e. for any two objects
x,y\in\mathrm{Obj}\,\mathscr{A}
, the map

F(x,y):\operatorname{Hom}(x,y)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(F(x),{F}(y))

is surjective ;

(c)

F
is faithful, i.e. for any two objects
x,y\in\mathrm{Obj}\,\mathscr{A}
, the map

F(x,y):\operatorname{Hom}(x,y)\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(F(x),F(y))

is injective.

In particular, an equivalence of categories
F:\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{B}
induces an bijection of sets

F_{*}:\text{ Iso }\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\text{ Iso }\mathscr{B}.

3.2
DGLA

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra. A
\mathfrak{g}
-augmented differential graded Lie algebra is a triple
(L^{\bullet},d,\varepsilon)
where
(L^{\bullet},d)
is a differential graded Lie algebra (dgla for short) and
\varepsilon:L^{0}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
is a homomorphism of Lie algebras. A homomorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
-augmented differential graded Lie algebras
(L^{\bullet},d,\varepsilon)\rightarrow(\overline{L^{\bullet}},\bar{d},\bar{\varepsilon})
is a differential graded Lie algebra homomorphism
\varphi:(L^{\bullet},d)\rightarrow(\overline{L^{\bullet}},\bar{d})
such that
\varepsilon\circ\varphi=\bar{\varepsilon}
. If
(L^{\bullet},d,\varepsilon)
is a
\mathfrak{g}
-augmented differential graded Lie algebra, then the augmentation extends trivially to a differential graded Lie algebra homomorphism
\varepsilon:L^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathrm{g}
where
\mathfrak{g}
is given the dgla structure with no nonzero elements of positive degree.

Definition 3.2

(1-equivalence)
.

A morphism of dglas

f:(L^{\bullet}_{0},d_{0})\to(L^{\bullet}_{1},d_{1})
is called
1-equivalent

if the induced map on cohomology is an isomorphism in
degrees 0 and 1, and a monomorphism in degree 2.

Definition 3.3

((1-)quasi-isomorphism)
.

Two
\mathfrak{g}
-augmented differential graded Lie algebras

(L^{\bullet},d,\varepsilon)
and
(\overline{L^{\bullet}},\bar{d},\bar{\varepsilon})

are called
quasi-isomorphic
(resp.
1-quasi-isomorphic
)
if there exists a zigzag of
\mathfrak{g}
-augmented dgla homomorphisms

L^{\bullet}=L_{0}\longrightarrow L_{1}\longleftarrow L_{2}\longrightarrow\dots\longleftarrow L_{m-1}\longrightarrow L_{m}=\overline{L^{\bullet}}

such that each homomorphism induces an isomorphism in cohomology
(resp. is 1-equivalent).

A
\mathfrak{g}
-augmented differential graded Lie algebra is formal if it is quasi-isomorphic to its cohomology. Note that in this paper, the dgla constructed to study the deformation of local systems on quasi-compact Kähler manifolds is, in general, not formal. This makes the present work significantly more involved than
[
GM88
,
ES11
,
EKPR12
]
, particularly regarding the analytic aspects of Hodge theory ; consequently, we must establish new techniques in Hodge theory to study these dglas.

3.3
DGLA and deformation functor

Given a dgla
C^{\bullet}
, there is a functor from Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebras to groupoids

\displaystyle{\rm Def}(C^{\bullet}):\Art\rightarrow{\rm Grp}

as follows. Let
A
be an Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebra and
\mathfrak{m}
be its maximal ideal.
Then the objects of
{\rm Def}(C^{\bullet},A)
are defined by

{\rm Obj}\ {\rm Def}(C^{\bullet},A)=\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A):=\{\alpha\in C^{1}\otimes\mathfrak{m}\mid d\alpha+\frac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]=0\},

and the morphisms are

\operatorname{Hom}(\alpha,\beta)=\exp(C^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}):=\{\lambda\in C^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}\mid\exp(\lambda)\alpha=\beta\}

where
\exp(\lambda)\alpha
denotes the gauge transformation
e^{\lambda}\circ\alpha\circ e^{-\lambda}-d(e^{\lambda})\circ e^{-\lambda}
.
We often write
[\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A)/\exp(C^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]
for this transformation groupoids.

We shall encounter augmented ones. Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a Lie algebra over
\mathbb{C}
, viewed as a dgla in degree 0, and let
\varepsilon:L^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathfrak{g}
be an augmentation of the dgla
L^{\bullet}
. Let
(A,\mathfrak{m})
be an Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebra. Then one defines the small groupoid
\Def(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)
by :

\displaystyle\obj\Def\left(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A\right)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A):=\left\{\left(\alpha,e^{r}\right)\in L^{1}\otimes\mathfrak{m}\times\exp(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{m})\left\lvert\,d\alpha+\frac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]=0\right.\right\},

(3.3.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}\left(\left(\alpha,e^{r}\right),\left(\beta,e^{s}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\lambda\in L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}\mid\exp(\lambda)\alpha=\beta,\exp(\varepsilon(\lambda))\cdot e^{r}=e^{s}\right\}.

In the preceding notation, one has

\displaystyle\Def\left(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A\right)=\Def\left(L^{\bullet},A\right)\bowtie\exp(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{m})_{\varepsilon},

(3.3.2)

where the
\varepsilon
subscript meaning that the gauge group acts via
\varepsilon
.
This gives a covariant functor
\Def\left(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon,-\right)
on the category Art of
\mathbb{C}
-Artin local rings with values in the category of small groupoids.

A main result by Goldman-Millson in
[
GM88
]
is the following :

Theorem 3.4

(
[
GM88
, Theorems 2.4 & 3.8]
)
.

If
(C_{1}^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{1})\rightarrow(C_{2}^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{2})
is a 1-quasi-isomorphism of
\mathfrak{g}
-augmented dgla, then it induces an equivalence of groupoids
\Def(C_{1}^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{1},A)\to\Def(C_{2}^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{2},A)
for any local Artin
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
A
. ∎

An analytic germ
(S,s)
defines a (covariant) functor of Artin rings

\displaystyle F_{S,s}:{\rm Art}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\text{ Set }

(3.4.1)

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\longmapsto\operatorname{Hom}(\widehat{O}_{S,s},A),

where the
\Hom
denotes the set of
\mathbb{C}
-algebra homomorphisms.
Such a functor is called
pro-representable
.

Theorem 3.5

(
[
GM88
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Two analytic germs
(S_{1},s_{1})
and
(S_{2},s_{2})
are analytically isomorphic if and only if the associated pro-representable functors
F_{S_{1},s_{1}},F_{S_{2},s_{2}}
are isomorphic. ∎

3.4
Application to the variety of representations

Let
X
be a differential manifold. For any
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
, we define a functor by

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\varrho}:{\rm Art}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\rm Grp},

whose objects are

\displaystyle\obj\mathscr{R}_{\varrho}(A)=R_{\varrho}(A):=\{\tilde{\rho}\in\Hom(\pi_{1}(X),\GL_{N}(A))\mid\tilde{\rho}=\rho\mbox{ mod }\mathfrak{m}\}

(3.5.1)

and the morphisms

G_{A}^{0}:=\exp(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{m})
, where
\mathfrak{g}:={\rm Lie}\,\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
. Here,
\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{m}
is a nilpotent Lie algebra, the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff series define a nilpotent Lie group structure on the space
G_{A}^{0}
. If we consider the morphism
q:\GL_{N}(A)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
induced by
A\to A/\mathfrak{m}\simeq\mathbb{C}
, then its kernel is
G_{A}^{0}
. Note that there exists a natural map

q_{*}:\Hom(\pi_{1}(X),\GL_{N}(A))\to\Hom(\pi_{1}(X),\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}))

whose fibers are objects
\mathscr{R}_{\varrho}(A)
. Since
G_{A}^{0}
preserves
R_{\varrho}(A)
, they defines a groupoid

[R_{\varrho}(A)/G_{A}^{0}]=:\mathscr{R}_{\varrho}(A).

We now consider a flat bundle
(V,\nabla)
and let
(E,\nabla):=(\End(V),\nabla)
be the associated endomorphism bundle. We consider a dgla

(L^{\bullet},d):=(A^{\bullet}(X,E),\nabla).

Fix a base point
x\in X
and an isomorphism
V_{x}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Let

\varrho:\pi_{1}(X,x)\to\GL(V_{x})\simeq\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})

be the holonomy representation of
\nabla
. In
[
GM88
, Theorems 6.3 & 6.6]
, the following result is proved.

Lemma 3.6
.

There is a natural equivalence of groupoids

\displaystyle h:[\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},A)/\exp(L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]

\displaystyle\to[R_{\varrho}(A)/G_{A}^{0}]

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle\mapsto{\rm hol}_{x}(\nabla+\alpha).

∎

By eliminating the superfluous isotropy, we have the following isomorphism between groupoids

\displaystyle[R_{\varrho}(A)/\{{\rm Id}\}]

\displaystyle\to[R_{\varrho}(A)/G_{A}^{0}]\bowtie G_{A}^{0}

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\tilde{\rho},1)

Consider the functor

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{\varrho}^{\prime}:{\rm Art}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\rm Grp},

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto[R_{\varrho}(A)/\{{\rm Id}\}]

where

\obj\mathscr{R}^{\prime}_{\varrho}(A)=R_{\varrho}(A)

and the only morphisms in
\mathscr{R}^{\prime}_{\varrho}(A)
are
the identity morphisms.
Then we have

Lemma 3.7
.

The functor
A\mapsto\mathscr{R}^{\prime}_{\varrho}(A)
it is prorepresented by the analytic germ
(R(X,N),\varrho)
. ∎

Therefore, by
3.1
and
3.6
, the morphism

\displaystyle h\bowtie G_{A}^{0}:[\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},A)/\exp(L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]\bowtie G_{A}^{0}\to[R_{\varrho}(A)/\{{\rm Id}\}]

(3.7.1)

is an equivalence of groupoids.

Let
\varepsilon_{x}:L^{\bullet}\to\End(V_{x})=E_{x}
defined by the evaluation

A^{0}(X,E)\to E_{x}
. It follows from (
3.3.2
) that there exists a natural equivalence

[\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},A)/\exp(L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]\bowtie G_{A}^{0}\simeq\Def(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{x},A).

Lemma 3.8
.

The map

\displaystyle h:[\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{x},A)/\exp(L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]

\displaystyle\to[R_{\varrho}(A)/{\rm Id}]

\displaystyle(\alpha,e^{r})

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(-r){\rm hol}_{x}(\nabla+\alpha)\exp(r)

is well-defined, and is an equivalence of groupoids.

Démonstration.

Recall that

\operatorname{Hom}\left(\left(\alpha,e^{r}\right),\left(\beta,e^{s}\right)\right)=\left\{\lambda\in L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}\mid\exp(\lambda)\alpha=\beta,\exp(\varepsilon_{x}(\lambda))\cdot e^{r}=e^{s}\right\}.

Note that we have

\nabla+\exp(\lambda)\alpha=\exp(\lambda)\circ(\nabla+\alpha)\circ\exp(-\lambda).

This implies that

{\rm hol}_{x}(\nabla+\exp(\lambda)\alpha)=\exp(\varepsilon_{x}(\lambda)){\rm hol}_{x}(\nabla+\alpha)\exp(\varepsilon_{x}(-\lambda)).

Therefore, we have

h(\beta,e^{s})=\exp(-s)\exp(\varepsilon_{x}(\lambda)){\rm hol}_{x}(\nabla+\alpha)\exp(\varepsilon_{x}(-\lambda))\exp(s)=h(\alpha,e^{r}).

Hence
h
is well-defined. By the above arguments, it is an equivalence of groupoids.
∎

3.5
An extension to quasi-compact Kähler manifolds

Let
X
be a compact Kähler
n
-fold and let
D
be a simple normal crossing divisor on
X
. Set
X_{0}:=X\setminus D
. Let
(V,\nabla)
be a semisimple flat bundle on
X
, and we denote the associated endomorphism bundle by
(E,\nabla):=(\End(V),\nabla)
.

Consider a twisted logarithmic De Rham complex

(T^{\bullet},d):=(A^{\bullet}(X,D,E),\nabla)=\left(A^{0}(X,D,E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\to}}A^{2n}(X,D,E)\right).

which constitutes a dgla on
X
. Let

(L^{\bullet},d):=(A^{0}(X_{0},E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\to}}A^{2\dim X}(X_{0},E))

be the standard de Rham complex on
X_{0}
. Fix a base point
x\in X_{0}
and let
\varepsilon_{x}:A^{0}(X_{0},E)\to E_{x}
be the augmentation map. The restriction of
(A^{\bullet}(X,D,E),\nabla)
to
X_{0}
induces a natural morphism of augmented dglas

a:(T^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{x},d)\to(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{x},d).

Let
\mathcal{V}
be the local system associated to
(V,\nabla)|_{X_{0}}
. By
[
Del70
, II, 6.10]
, there is a natural isomorphism between the logarithmic de Rham cohomology and the singular cohomology with coefficients in
\mathcal{V}
:

H^{i}(T^{\bullet},d)\simeq H_{\rm sing}^{i}(X_{0},\mathcal{V}).

This isomorphism factors through the map

H^{i}(T^{\bullet},d)\to H^{i}(L^{\bullet},d)

induced by the restriction morphism
a:T^{\bullet}|_{X_{0}}\to L^{\bullet}
.
This implies that, the dgla morphism of
a
is a quasi-isomorphism. Hence,
by
3.4
and
3.8
, we have

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\varrho
be the monodromy representation of
(V,\nabla)|_{X_{0}}
. We define

R_{\varrho}(A):=\{\tilde{\rho}\in\Hom(\pi_{1}(X_{0},x),\GL_{N}(A))\mid\tilde{\rho}\equiv\varrho\pmod{\mathfrak{m}}\}

The holonomy map
h :

\displaystyle h:[\mathscr{C}(T^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{x},A)/\exp(T^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]

\displaystyle\to[R_{\varrho}(A)/{\rm Id}]

\displaystyle(\alpha,e^{r})

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(-r){\rm hol}_{x}(\nabla+\alpha)\exp(r)

is well-defined and induces an equivalence of groupoids.∎

4
New perspectives on deformations of local systems on compact Kähler manifolds

In this section, we offer new perspectives on several foundational results established by Goldman–Millson
[
GM88
]
, Eyssidieux–Simpson
[
ES11
]
, and Eyssidieux et al.
[
EKPR12
]
. While this section is logically independent of the rest of the paper, it serves as a warm-up for understanding the quasi-compact case. Readers familiar with the standard compact theory may skip this section without loss of continuity.

4.1
Universal connection : analytic construction

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
(V,\nabla)
be a semisimple flat bundle on
X
, and we denote the associated endomorphism bundle by
(E,\nabla):=(\End(V),\nabla)
. Fix a basis
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{p}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,E)
.
For any multi-index
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
with
|I|\geq 2
, we define inductively

\displaystyle\gamma_{I}:

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=(I_{1},I_{2});\\
1\leq|I_{1}|<|I_{2}|\end{subarray}}[\eta_{I_{1}},\eta_{I_{2}}]+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=(I_{1},I_{2});\\
|I_{1}|=|I_{2}|\end{subarray}}[\eta_{I_{1}},\eta_{I_{2}}]

(4.0.1)

\displaystyle\eta_{I}:

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{\prime\prime*}G\gamma_{I}\in A^{1}(X,E),

(4.0.2)

where
G
is Green operator with respect to the Laplacian
\Delta^{\prime}
. Note that the decomposition
\nabla=\nabla^{\prime}+\nabla^{\prime\prime}
does not correspond to the standard splitting into
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
parts, unless
(V,\nabla)
is unitary. Instead, this decomposition arises from the harmonic bundle (see
[
Sim92
]
).

Lemma 4.1
.

For any
|I|\geq 2
, we have

\eta_{I}\in\operatorname{Im}\nabla^{\prime}
, and any
|I|\geq 3
,
\gamma_{I}\in\operatorname{Im}\nabla^{\prime}
.

Démonstration.

We prove by induction on
|I|
, and assume that it holds for any
|I|\in\{2,\ldots,k-1\}
. Fix some
I
with
|I|=k
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime}\nabla^{\prime*}G\eta_{I}

\displaystyle=(1-\mathcal{H})\eta_{I}-\nabla^{\prime*}G\nabla^{\prime}\eta_{I}

\displaystyle=\eta_{I}+\nabla^{\prime\prime*}G\nabla^{\prime}\gamma_{I}=\eta_{I}.

Together with (
4.0.1
), it follows that
\gamma_{I}
is also
\nabla^{\prime}
-exact when
|I|\geq 3
.
The lemma is proved.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Set

\displaystyle\sigma_{k}:=-(\sum_{|I|=k}z_{I}\eta_{I}).

(4.2.1)

Let
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
be the quadratic cone in
\mathbb{C}^{p}
defined by

\{[\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i},\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i}]\}=0\in H^{2}(X,E),

Then for any
z\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla\sigma_{k}=\nabla^{\prime\prime}\sigma_{k}=-\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\sigma_{i}\wedge\sigma_{k-i}.

(4.2.2)

Démonstration.

Set
\sigma_{1}(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{m}z_{i}\eta_{i}
.
Note that

\nabla\sigma_{2}(z):=-\nabla\nabla^{\prime\prime*}G\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{1}]=-(1-\mathcal{H})\frac{1}{2}[\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i},\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i}].

By the assumption, for any
z\in\mathscr{Q}
,
[\sigma_{1},\sigma_{1}]
is cohomologous to zero. Hence we have

\nabla\sigma_{2}(z)+\frac{1}{2}[\sigma_{1}(z),\sigma_{1}(z)]=0

for any
z\in\mathscr{Q}
.

We now make the induction for
\ell\geq 3
. Assume that (
4.2.2
) holds for
k=2,\ldots,\ell-1
.
Then
we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime\prime}(\sum_{|I|=\ell}z_{I}\gamma_{I})=\nabla^{\prime\prime}(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}\sigma_{i}\wedge\sigma_{\ell-i})=0

by the induction for (
4.2.2
) and the Jacobian identity. Thus,

\displaystyle\nabla\sigma_{\ell}(z)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{|I|=\ell}z_{I}\nabla\nabla^{\prime\prime*}G\gamma_{I}

\displaystyle=-(1-\mathcal{H})(\sum_{|I|=\ell}z_{I}\gamma_{I})+\sum_{|I|=\ell}z_{I}\nabla^{\prime\prime*}G\nabla^{\prime\prime}(\gamma_{I})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{|I|=\ell}z_{I}\gamma_{I}

where the last equality follows from
4.1
.
Hence the induction is proved for
\ell
. The lemma is proved.
∎

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}^{p}
,
we define a connection

\displaystyle\nabla_{z}:=\nabla+\sum_{|I|\geq 1}z_{I}\eta_{I}.

(4.2.3)

for
V
. In general, this connection is neither flat nor smooth. However, by applying elliptic estimates for the Laplacian, there exists an
\varepsilon>0
such that on the intersection
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}^{p}
, the operator
\nabla_{z}
forms an analytic family of flat connections for
V
. We omit the proof here, as we will treat the more general quasi-projective setting later.

4.2
Universal connection : universal property

Consider a dgla defined by

(L^{\bullet},d):=(A^{\bullet}(X,E),\nabla).

Set

(C^{\bullet},d):=(\ker\nabla^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime\prime}),\quad(D^{\bullet},d):=(H^{i}(L^{\bullet},d),0),

both of which are naturally endowed with dgla structures.
Then by the formality in
[
Sim92
]
, we have the quasi-isomorphisms

\displaystyle L^{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\leftarrow}}C^{\bullet}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi}}{{\rightarrow}}D^{\bullet}.

(4.2.4)

wehre
\phi
is the inclusion, and
\psi(\eta):=\{\mathcal{H}(\eta)\}
.
Fix a base point
x\in X
. Let
\varepsilon:L^{0}\to E_{x}
,
\varepsilon:C^{0}\to E_{x}
, and
\varepsilon:D^{0}\to E_{x}
be the evaluation maps which define the augmentations.
By
3.4
, for any Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
A
, the natural maps

\displaystyle[\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)/\exp(L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]\leftarrow[\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)/\exp(C^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]\to[\mathscr{C}(D^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)/\exp(D^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]

(4.2.5)

are equivalences of groupoids.

Let
\mathscr{Q}_{0}
be the quadratic cone in
D^{1}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}_{0}:=\left\{u\in D^{1}\otimes\mathfrak{m}\mid[u,u]=0\right\}.

(4.2.6)

Denote by

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}:=\mathscr{Q}_{0}\times\mathfrak{g}/\varepsilon(D^{0}).

(4.2.7)

The following lemma was proven in
[
GM88
, Lemma 3.10]
. Here, we refine the result by providing an explicit natural transformation between the functors.

Lemma 4.3
.

The analytic germ
(\mathscr{Q},0)
(non-canonically) prorepresents the functor

\displaystyle{\rm Art}

\displaystyle\to{\rm Grp}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto\Def(D^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)

where
\Def(D^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)
is the small groupoids defined in (
3.3.1
).

Démonstration.

Let
\ell:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{g}/\varepsilon(C^{0}))
. We choose a subset
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
the images of
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}\}
form a basis for the quotient space
\mathfrak{g}/\varepsilon(C^{0})
.
Then the natural linear map
W\to\mathfrak{g}/\varepsilon(D^{0})
is an isomorphism. Since
\varepsilon:D^{0}\to E_{x}

defined by the evaluation is injective, it follows that
\exp\left(\varepsilon(D^{0})\otimes\mathfrak{m}\right)
acts freely on
G_{A}^{0}:=\exp(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{m})
by left-multiplication. Hence the following (non-canonical) maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{g}/\varepsilon(D_{0})\otimes\mathfrak{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W\otimes\mathfrak{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\frac{\exp(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{m})}{\exp(\varepsilon(D^{0})\otimes\mathfrak{m})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p(r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp(\varepsilon(D^{0})\otimes\mathfrak{m})\cdot\exp(r)}

are both bijective. Here
\exp(\varepsilon(D^{0})\otimes\mathfrak{m})\cdot\exp(r)
is the right coset of
\exp(r)
.
Hence we can define an equivalence

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(D^{\bullet},A)\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle\to\frac{\mathscr{C}(D^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)}{\exp(D^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})}

(4.3.1)

\displaystyle(\alpha,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto[(\alpha,\exp(r))].

Note that
C^{0}=D^{0}
, the map

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A)\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle\to\frac{\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)}{\exp(C^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})}

(4.3.2)

\displaystyle(\alpha,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto[(\alpha,\exp(r))].

We consider
D^{1}\times W
as an affine
\mathbb{C}
-space. Let
\mathcal{I}
be the ideal of
\mathbb{C}[D^{1}\times W]
generated by the quadratic equations

[q(u),q(u)]=0,

where
q:D^{1}\times W\to D^{1}
denotes the projection map. It follows that

\mathbb{C}[D^{1}\times W]/\mathcal{I}=\textrm{Spec}\,(\mathscr{Q}).

Then for any element
\alpha\in D^{1}\otimes\mathfrak{m}
and
r\in W\otimes\mathfrak{m}
, it can be written as

\displaystyle\alpha=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\{\eta_{i}\}\otimes f_{i},\quad r=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}v_{i}\otimes g_{i},

(4.3.3)

where
f_{i},g_{j}\in\mathfrak{m}
. This gives rise to a morphism

\displaystyle\Hom(\mathbb{C}[D^{1}\times W],A)

\displaystyle(\{\eta_{i}\}^{*},(v_{j})^{*})\mapsto f_{i}+g_{j}

Here we consider
\{\eta_{i}\}^{*}\in(D^{1})^{*}
and
(v_{j})^{*}\in W^{*}
.
Note that the above morphism factors through

\displaystyle\Hom(\mathbb{C}[D^{1}\times W]/\mathcal{I},A)

if and only if
\alpha
satisfies

\frac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]=d(\alpha)+\frac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]=0,

where
d:D^{1}\to D^{2}
denotes the differential of the dgla
(D^{\bullet},d)
, that is zero.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{1}
be the ideal of
\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]
generated by the image of

(D^{1}\times W)^{*}\subset\mathbb{C}[D^{1}\times W]
under the natural projection

\mathbb{C}[D^{1}\times W]\to\mathbb{C}[D^{1}\times W]/\mathcal{I}
.
Let
\widehat{\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}
denote the
\mathcal{I}_{1}
-adic completion of
\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]
.
Since
A
is an Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebra, it follows that

\displaystyle\Hom(\mathbb{C}[D^{1}\times W]/\mathcal{I},A)\simeq\Hom(\widehat{\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}},A).

By standard properties of completion of quotient rings, there is a natural isomorphism

\widehat{\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\;\simeq\;\frac{\mathbb{C}[[D^{1}\times W]]}{\mathcal{I}},

where, by abuse of notation, we continue to denote by
\mathcal{I}
the ideal

\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathbb{C}[[D^{1}\times W]]
.

On the other hand,
\mathcal{I}_{1}
-adic completion commutes with localization at the
maximal ideal
\mathcal{I}_{1}
of
\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]
.
Therefore we have a natural isomorphism

\widehat{\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}}.

Therefore, the following map

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(D^{\bullet},A)\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}\to\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}},A)

(4.3.4)

\displaystyle(\alpha,r)\mapsto\left((\{\eta_{i}\}^{*},v_{j}^{*})\mapsto f_{i}+g_{j}\right)

is an equivalence, where
(\alpha,r)
is written as in (
4.3.3
), and
\{\eta_{i}\}^{*}
and
v_{j}^{*}
are understood as the images of
\{\eta_{i}\}^{*}
,
v_{j}^{*}
under the composite morphisms

\mathbb{C}[D^{1}\times W]\to\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}\to\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0}.

We remark that
\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}}
is generated by

\{\eta_{i}\}^{*}
and
v_{j}^{*}
.
Therefore, for any morphism in

\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}},A)
, the images of these generators
completely determine the morphism.
Combining (
4.3.4
) with (
4.3.1
) yields the lemma.
∎

Fix a positive integer
k\geq 2
. We set

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{C}^{\ell}.

Let
\mathfrak{m}
be the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}
and we consider
A_{k}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
, that is an Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebra. Write
\mathfrak{m}_{k}:=\mathfrak{m}A_{k}
, that is the maximal ideal for
A_{k}
. We now regard
z_{I}
and
x_{j}
as the image of
z_{I}
and
x_{j}
under the morphism

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}^{p+\ell},0}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}=A_{k}.

Then
z_{I}\in\mathfrak{m}_{k}
for each
I
with
|I|\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
.
For any
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, let

\sigma_{i}=\sum_{|I|=i}z_{I}\eta_{I}

be defined in (
4.2.1
).
By
4.1
, we have

\eta_{I}\in\operatorname{Im}\nabla^{\prime}
for
|I|\geq 2
, and for any
z\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
, we have

\nabla^{\prime}\sigma_{i}(z)=0

for any
i\geq 1
. This implies that

\displaystyle\psi(\sigma_{i})=0\in D^{1}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k},\quad\mbox{ if }i\geq 2,

(4.3.5)

and

\delta_{k}:=\sum_{1\leq|I|\leq k}z_{I}\eta_{I}\in C^{1}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}.

On the other hand, by
4.2
, we have

\delta_{k}:=\sum_{1\leq|I|\leq k}z_{I}\eta_{I}\in\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A_{k}).

Together with (
4.3.5
), this yields

\psi(\delta_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\{\eta_{i}\}\in\mathscr{C}(D^{\bullet},A_{k}).

By (
4.2.4
) and
3.4
, we have the equivalences

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A)\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi\times\mathrm{Id}}}{{\to}}\mathscr{C}(D^{\bullet},A)\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}{\to}\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},A_{k}),

(4.3.6)

with

\Phi(\delta_{k},\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}w_{i}v_{i})=\left((\{\eta_{i}\}^{*},v_{j}^{*})\mapsto z_{i}+w_{j}\right).

As remarked above, since
\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}}
is generated by

\{\eta_{i}\}^{*}
and
v_{j}^{*}
, any morphism in

\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}},A_{k})
is determined by the images of these generators.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
\nabla_{z}
denote the analytic family of flat connections on
V
,
parametrized by
z\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
, as defined in (
4.2.3
). Let
\mathrm{ev}_{x}:H^{0}(X,E)\to E_{x}
be the evaluation map, and let
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}\}
be a subset of
E_{x}
such that
the images of
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}\}
form a basis for the quotient space
\mathfrak{g}/\mathrm{ev}_{x}H^{0}(X,E)
.
Let
{\rm Mon}(\nabla_{z})
be the monodromy representation for any
z\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
. Then the natural map between analytic germs

\displaystyle f:(\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\times\mathbb{C}^{\ell},0)

\displaystyle\to\left(R(X,N),\varrho\right)

(4.4.1)

\displaystyle(z,w)

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}w_{i}v_{i}){\rm Mon}(\nabla_{z})\exp(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}w_{i}v_{i})

is an analytic isomorphism.

Démonstration.

We recall that we have morphisms of groupoids

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom\!\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},A_{k}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[R_{\varrho}(A_{k})/{\rm Id}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A_{k})\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(D^{\bullet},A_{k})\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A_{k})/\exp(L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k})]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A_{k})/\exp(C^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k})]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathscr{C}(D^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A_{k})/\exp(D^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k})]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\times\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

(4.4.2)

in which each of them is an isomorphism by (
4.3.1
), (
4.3.2
), (
4.2.4
) and
3.4
. Here

\displaystyle h:[\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{x},A_{k})/\exp(L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]

\displaystyle\to[R_{\varrho}(A_{k})/{\rm Id}]

\displaystyle(\alpha,e^{r})

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(-r){\rm hol}_{x}(\nabla+\alpha)\exp(r)

is defined in
3.8
.
We denote by

\Upsilon:\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A_{k})\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}\to[R_{\varrho}(A_{k})/{\rm Id}]

the induced equivalence of groupoids in (
). It follows that

\Upsilon(\delta_{k},\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}w_{i}v_{i})=\exp(-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}w_{i}v_{i}){\rm Mon}(\nabla_{z})\exp(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}w_{i}v_{i})\in R_{\varrho}(A_{k})

corresponds to the morphism

f_{k}:\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{R(X,N),\varrho}\to A_{k}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}

induced by
f
defined in (
4.4.1
).

Let us define a morphism

g_{k}\in\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},A_{k})

by setting

\displaystyle g_{k}(\{\eta_{i}\}^{*})=z_{i},\quad g_{k}(v_{j}^{*})=w_{j}.

(4.4.3)

for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
j\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}
. By (
4.3.6
), we have

\Phi(\delta_{k},\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}w_{i}v_{i})=g_{k}.

One can see the compatibility of
{g}_{k}
by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

In conclusion, there exists natural maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom\!\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},A_{k}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom\!\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},A_{k-1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A_{k})\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A_{k-1})\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{{R(X,N)},\varrho},A_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{{R(X,N)},\varrho},A_{k-1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon}

such that all vertical maps are isomorphisms. We have the inverse limit

\varprojlim_{k}g_{k}\in\Hom\!\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},\varprojlim_{k}A_{k}\right)=\Hom\!\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}\right)

and by (
4.4.3
), it is an isomorphism.
By (
), it follows that

f^{*}=\varprojlim_{k}f_{k}:\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{R(X,N),\varrho}\to\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0},

is also an isomorphism.
By
3.5
, this implies that

f:(\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\times W,0)\to(R(X,N),\varrho)
is a
biholomorphism
between analytic germs.
The theorem is therefore proved.
∎

4.3
1-step phenomenon

We are now in a position to prove the result on the so-called
1-step phenomenon
for semisimple systems on compact Kähler manifolds.

Theorem 4.5

(1-step phenomenon)
.

Let
h:Y\to X
be a holomorphic map between compact Kähler manifolds.
Let
(V,\nabla)
be a semisimple flat bundle on
X
with monodromy
representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X,x)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
, and set
y=h(x)
.
Denote by

h^{*}:R(X,N)\longrightarrow R(Y,N)

the induced morphism of representation varieties, given by

\tau\mapsto h^{*}\tau
for any
\tau\in R(X,N)
.

Assume that :

(a)

the pullback representation
h^{*}\varrho
has image in the center

Z(\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}))
, that is,
h^{*}\varrho
factors through a representation

\tau_{0}:\pi_{1}(Y,y)\to Z(\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}))
, where

Z(\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}))=\{\lambda\,\mathrm{Id}_{N}\mid\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}\};

(b)

for every
\eta\in H^{1}(X,E)
, one has
h^{*}\eta=0
in

H^{1}(Y,h^{*}E)
.

Let
R
be any (geometrically) irreducible component of
R(X,N)

containing
\varrho
.
Then the image of
R
under
h^{*}
consists of the single point

h^{*}(R)=\{\tau_{0}\}.

Démonstration.

We use the notations introduced in this subsection.
Let
\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{m}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,E)
be a basis, and let

\eta_{I}
be defined as in (
4.0.2
).
By assumption,
h^{*}\eta_{i}=0
for each
i
.
By the construction of
\eta_{I}
in (
4.0.2
), this yields

h^{*}\eta_{I}=0\qquad\text{for all }I\text{ with }|I|\geq 1.

This implies that

h^{*}\nabla_{z}=h^{*}\nabla\qquad\text{for every }z\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{r}.

On the other hand, by
Item

(a)
, for any
g\in\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
we have

g^{-1}\tau_{0}g=\tau_{0}
; hence the representation
\tau_{0}
is fixed under
conjugation.
Therefore, for the map
f:\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\times W\to R(X,N)
constructed in
4.4
, the composition

h^{*}\circ f:\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\times W\longrightarrow R(Y,N).

is constant.
By
4.4
, this implies that
h^{*}
is constant on some neighborhood of
\varrho
in
R
.
Since
h^{*}
is an algebraic morphism, it follows that

h^{*}(R)=\{h^{*}\varrho\}=\{\tau_{0}\}.

The theorem follows.
∎

4.4
Geometric applications I : Katzarkov’s and Campana’s theorems

Definition 4.6

(Shafarevich morphism)
.

Let
X
be a quasi-compact Kähler manifold, and let

\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a linear representation.
Assume that there exists a
compactifiable
dominant holomorphic map

{\rm sh}_{\varrho}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)

onto a complex normal variety
{\rm Sh}_{\varrho}(X)
with connected general fibers,
such that for every compactifiable holomorphic map
f:Y\to X
, the following properties are equivalent :

(a)

{\rm sh}_{\varrho}\circ f(Y)
is a point ;

(b)

f^{*}\varrho(\pi_{1}(Y))
is finite ;

Then such
{\rm sh}_{\varrho}
is called the
Shafarevich morphism
of
\varrho
.

The following lemma allows us to pass to a finite étale cover when constructing the Shafarevich morphism.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
\varrho\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\operatorname{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a linear representation. Assume there exists a finite étale Galois cover
\mu\colon\widehat{X}\to X
such that the Shafarevich morphism
\operatorname{sh}_{\mu^{*}\varrho}\colon\widehat{X}\to\operatorname{Sh}_{\mu^{*}\varrho}(\widehat{X})
associated to the pullback
\mu^{*}\varrho
exists. Then the Shafarevich morphism of
\varrho
exists.

Démonstration.

Let
G\coloneqq\operatorname{Aut}(\widehat{X}/X)
be the Galois group of the cover.
Let
F
be a fiber of
\operatorname{sh}_{\mu^{*}\varrho}
. Note that, for any
\gamma\in G
, we have

\mu^{*}\varrho\bigl(\operatorname{Im}[\pi_{1}(\gamma\cdot F)\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{X})]\bigr)=\mu^{*}\varrho\bigl(\operatorname{Im}[\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(\widehat{X})]\bigr),

which implies that the image of the fundamental group of
\gamma\cdot F
is also finite. By
4.6
, it follows that
\operatorname{sh}_{\mu^{*}\varrho}
must contract
\gamma\cdot F
to a point ; in other words,
\gamma\cdot F
is contained in a fiber of
\operatorname{sh}_{\mu^{*}\varrho}
.

Consequently, the action of
G
on
\widehat{X}
descends to an action on
\operatorname{Sh}_{\mu^{*}\varrho}(\widehat{X})
, making
\operatorname{sh}_{\mu^{*}\varrho}
equivariant with respect to this action. Therefore, the quotient
X\to\operatorname{Sh}_{\mu^{*}\varrho}(\widehat{X})/G
is proper, and one can check that it satisfies the properties required to be the Shafarevich morphism of
\varrho
.
∎

Definition 4.8

(Big representation)
.

Let
X
be a smooth quasi-projective variety.
A linear representation

\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})

is called
big
if, for any closed subvariety

Z\subset X
passing through a very general point,
the group

\varrho\!\left(\mathrm{Im}\bigl[\pi_{1}(Z^{\mathrm{norm}})\to\pi_{1}(X)\bigr]\right)

is infinite, where
Z^{\mathrm{norm}}
denotes the normalization of
Z
.

In
[
Kat97
]
, Katzarkov proved that for a smooth projective variety with linear nilpotent fundamental groups, its Shafarevich morphism is the Stein factorization of its Albanese map. This was extended to compact Kähler manifolds by Leroy
[
Ler99
]
in his PhD thesis (see also the survey paper by Claudon
[
Cla12
]
). Based on
4.5
, we can give another proof of Katzarkov’s theorem following same ideas in
[
EKPR12
]
.

We first prove the following lemma.

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold, and let

\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a linear representation whose image is
virtually nilpotent.
Let
a:X\to A
denote the Albanese map, and let
f:Y\to X
be a holomorphic map
from another compact Kähler manifold such that
(a\circ f)(Y)
is a point.
Then
f^{*}\tau(\pi_{1}(X))
has finite image.

Démonstration.

Since
\tau(\pi_{1}(X))
is virtually nilpotent, after replacing
X
by a finite étale cover, we may assume that its Zariski closure in
\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is a connected nilpotent algebraic group
G
. Then
G
decomposes as a direct product of its central torus
T
and its unipotent radical
U
. This induces a natural decomposition of the representation

\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to T\times U.

By fixing a faithful linear representation
U\hookrightarrow\GL_{N_{2}}(\mathbb{C})
, we can view the projection
\tau_{2}
as a unipotent representation.

Let
(V,\nabla)
denote the trivial flat bundle of rank
N_{2}
.
By the universal property of the Albanese map, one has
f^{*}\omega=0
for
every
\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X})
.
Since
(V,\nabla)
is the trivial flat bundle, it follows that

f^{*}\eta=0
for all
\eta\in H^{1}(X,\End(V))
.
Thus the hypotheses of
4.5
are satisfied for the trivial representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N_{2}}(\mathbb{C})
and the map
f:Y\to X
.
Note that
\tau_{2}
belongs to the same irreducible component of

\varrho
.
Hence
f^{*}\tau_{2}
is trivial by
4.5
.

On the other hand, since
\tau_{1}(\pi_{1}(X))
is abelian, the representation

\tau_{1}
factors through
H_{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.
Moreover, the map
a_{*}:H_{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})/{\rm tor}\to H_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})
is an isomorphism.
Thus there exists a factorization

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{N_{1}}(\mathbb{C}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{1}}

It follows that
f^{*}\tau_{1}(\pi_{1}(Y))=\{1\}
.
Consequently,
f^{*}\tau(\pi_{1}(Y))=\{1\}
. The lemma is proved.
∎

Theorem 4.10

(Katzarkov, Leroy)
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold. Assume that
\pi_{1}(X)
is nilpotent. Then the Shafarevich morphism of
X
is the Stein factorization of its Albanese map
a:X\to A
. Moreover, the universal cover of
X
is holomorphically convex.

Démonstration.

Note that any finitely generated nilpotent group is linear. Hence, there exists a faithful representation
\tau\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\operatorname{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
whose image is nilpotent. By
4.7
, it suffices to construct the Shafarevich morphism for this cover.

Let
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\to}}S\to A
be the Stein
factorization of
a
.
Let
f:Y\to X
be a holomorphic map from another compact Kähler manifold
such that
(g\circ f)(Y)
is a point.
By
4.9
,
f^{*}\tau:\pi_{1}(Y)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
has finite image.
Since
\tau
is faithful, the image

{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)]
is thus finite.

Finally, by the characterization of the Albanese map, for any holomorphic
map
h:Z\to X
from a compact Kähler manifold, the group
h^{*}H^{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})

is finite if and only if
(a\circ h)(Z)
is a point.
Combining this with the property established above, we conclude that
g

is the Shafarevich morphism of
X
.

We omit the proof of holomorphic convexity here, as it is identical to the proof of the more general
5.26
established later.
∎

We also provide a new proof of Campana’s abelianity conjecture in the linear
and compact setting.

Theorem 4.11

(
[
Cam04
]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety.
If
X
is special, then every linear representation

\tau:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
has virtually abelian image.

Démonstration.

By
[
CDY25c
]
, after replacing
X
by a finite étale cover, we may assume
that
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
is nilpotent and torsion free.
By
[
Cam04
]
, the Albanese map

a:X\to A
is an algebraic fiber space.
Let
F
be a general smooth fibre of
a
.
By
[
CDY25c
]
, we have an exact sequence

\pi_{1}(F)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(X)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(A)\longrightarrow 0.

(4.11.1)

Let
\iota:F\to X
denote the inclusion.
By
4.9
, the representation

\iota^{*}\tau:\pi_{1}(F)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
has finite image, and hence is trivial
since
\tau(\pi_{1}(X))
is torsion free.
By (
), the representation
\tau
therefore factors through

\pi_{1}(A)
, and thus has abelian image.
This proves the theorem.
∎

4.5
Geometric applications II : a structure theorem for the linear Shafarevich morphism

The following result is implicit in
[
EKPR12
]
. It played a crucial role in the work of Wang and the second author on the proof of the linear Kollár conjecture (cf.
[
DW24
, Proof of Theorem 4.6]
). Here, we provide an alternative proof using
4.5
.

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
X
be a smooth projective variety, and let
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a big linear representation. Let
\sigma:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
denote its semisimplification. Let
{\rm sh}_{\sigma}:X\to{\rm Sh}_{\sigma}(X)
be the Shafarevich morphism associated with
\sigma
, whose existence is guaranteed by
[
Eys04
]
(see also
[
DYK23
]
). If
Y
is a very general fiber of
