{\rm sh}_{\sigma}
, then the Albanese map of
Y
is generically finite onto its image.

Démonstration.

By Selberg’s theorem, we may replace
X
with a finite étale cover such that the images
\sigma(\pi_{1}(X))
and
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
are torsion-free. Let
(V,\nabla)
be the semisimple flat bundle on
X
associated with the monodromy representation of
\sigma
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be the natural morphism. Since
\varrho
is big and
Y
is a very general fiber of
{\rm sh}_{\sigma}
,
Y
is smooth and the pullback
f^{*}\varrho:\pi_{1}(Y)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
remains big. On the other hand, by
4.6
, the image of
\pi_{1}(Y)
under
\sigma
is finite ; given our torsion-free assumption,
f^{*}\sigma
is trivial. Consequently, the pullback bundle
f^{*}(V,\nabla)
is a trivial flat bundle.

Let
a:Y\to A
be the Albanese map. Let
Z
be a connected component of a very general smooth fiber of
a
, and let
g:Z\to Y
denote the natural inclusion. The pullback
g^{*}f^{*}\varrho:\pi_{1}(Z)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
is also big. Since
Z
maps to a point in
A
, the induced map on cohomology vanishes, i.e.,
g^{*}H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{C})=0
. Since
f^{*}(V,\nabla)
is a trivial flat bundle, we have

g^{*}H^{1}(Y,f^{*}\End(V))\simeq g^{*}H^{1}(Y,\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N^{2}}=0.

Define
h:=f\circ g
. The conditions in
4.5
are thus satisfied for
h:Z\to X
.

Claim 4.13
.

The representations
\varrho
and
\sigma
are in the same irreducible component of
R(X,N)
.

Démonstration.

Let
V=\mathbb{C}^{N}
. Since
V
is a finite-dimensional vector space, there exists a Jordan-Hölder filtration of
\pi_{1}(X)
-submodules :

0=V_{0}\subset V_{1}\subset V_{2}\subset\dots\subset V_{k}=V,

such that the successive quotients
W_{i}:=V_{i}/V_{i-1}
are irreducible representations. The semisimplification
\sigma
is defined as the direct sum representation acting on
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}W_{i}
.

We choose a basis for
V
compatible with this filtration. With respect to this basis, for any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(X)
, the matrix
\rho(\gamma)
is block upper-triangular :

\rho(\gamma)=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{1}(\gamma)&*&\dots&*\\
0&\rho_{2}(\gamma)&\dots&*\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\dots&\rho_{k}(\gamma)\end{pmatrix},

where
\rho_{i}(\gamma)
denotes the action on the quotient
W_{i}
. The semisimplification corresponds to the block-diagonal part :

\sigma(\gamma)=\text{diag}(\rho_{1}(\gamma),\rho_{2}(\gamma),\dots,\rho_{k}(\gamma)).

To construct the deformation, we define a one-parameter family of diagonal matrices. Let
n_{i}=\dim(W_{i})
. For
t\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
, define
D(t)\in\mathrm{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
as :

D(t)=\text{diag}(t^{w_{1}}I_{n_{1}},t^{w_{2}}I_{n_{2}},\dots,t^{w_{k}}I_{n_{k}}),

where
w_{i}:=k-i
.

We define an algebraic morphism
\Phi:\mathbb{C}^{*}\to R(X,N)
by conjugation :

\Phi(t)(\gamma):=D(t)\varrho(\gamma)D(t)^{-1}.

A direct calculation shows that the limit
\lim_{t\to 0}\Phi(t)(\gamma)
exists and equals
\sigma(\gamma)
. Thus,
\Phi
extends to a regular morphism on
\mathbb{C}
such that
\Phi(0)=\sigma
. Since
\Phi(1)=\varrho
, the representations
\sigma
and
\varrho
are connected by an affine line, and therefore lie in the same irreducible component of
R(X,N)(\mathbb{C})
.
∎

We now apply
4.5
to conclude that
h^{*}\varrho
is trivial. However, recall that
h^{*}\varrho
is big when restricted to the very general fiber
Z
. The only way a representation can be both trivial and big is if the domain is a point. Therefore,
\dim Z=0
. Since
Z
is the generic fiber of the Albanese map, this implies
\dim a(Y)=\dim Y
. The theorem is thus proved.
∎

In the remainder of the paper, we pursue a similar strategy to study
2.12
. However, in general there is no formality as in (
4.2.4
), and, as we shall see later, the
1-step phenomenon
of
4.5
does not hold in this setting. We therefore have to develop Hodge-theoretic methods for the deformation of local systems over quasi-compact Kähler manifolds, and subsequently establish a
2-step phenomenon
in this more general context.

5
Deformation theory of local systems over quasi-compact Kähler manifolds

The results in this section form the technical core of the paper. Here, we prove
C
and
D
, which provide the foundation for the deformation theory of local systems over quasi-compact Kähler manifolds.

Throughout this section, let
(X,\omega)
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
be a simple normal crossing divisor on
X
. Set
X_{0}=X\setminus D
. Let
(V,\nabla)
be a
unitary
flat bundle on
X
, and let
(E,\nabla)=(\End(V),\nabla)
be the induced bundle of endomorphisms. Note that the unitary structure on
V
induces a hermitian flat metric
h
for
(E,\nabla)
. We write
\nabla=\nabla^{\prime}+\nabla^{\prime\prime}
where
\nabla^{\prime}
is the
(1,0)
-part and
\nabla^{\prime\prime}
is the
(0,1)
-part. Let
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime*}
and
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}
denote the formal adjoints of
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
and
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}
, defined with respect to the metrics
h
and
\omega
. Finally, let
G
be the Green operator associated with the Laplacian
\Delta^{\prime}=\Delta^{\prime\prime}
. These operators satisfy the relations given in (
2.14.1
) ; we will use these identities freely throughout this section.

5.1
Residue maps

Let us first recall the definition of residues.

For a multi-index
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
with entries in
\{1,\ldots,m\}
, we denote its length by
|I|=k
. We say that
I
is
ordered
if
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}
.

For any ordered
I
, we set
D_{I}:=D_{i_{1}}\cap\cdots\cap D_{i_{j}}
.
Set
D^{(j)}:=\sqcup_{|I|=j}D_{I}
and let

D_{(j)}:=\sqcup_{|I|=j}\sum_{p\notin I}D_{I}\cap D_{p}

that is a simple normal crossing divisor on
D^{(j)}
.

Fix any
\eta\in A^{k}(X,D,E)
and any integer
j\leq k
. For any point
x\in D_{I}
with
|I|=j
, choose a coordinate neighborhood
(U;z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
centered at
x
such that there exists
j^{\prime}\geq j
with

D\cap U=(\cup_{\ell=1}^{j^{\prime}}D_{i_{\ell}})\cap U;\quad D_{i_{\ell}}\cap U=\{z_{\ell}=0\}

for each
\ell\in\{1,\ldots,j^{\prime}\}
. On this neighborhood
U
, we can decompose
\eta
as

\displaystyle\eta|_{U}=\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}\wedge a(z)+b(z),

where
a(z)
is a section of
\mathcal{A}^{k-j}(X,\sum_{p\notin I}D_{p},E)(U)
, and
b(z)
is a term that does not contain the full wedge product
\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}
. We then define the residue locally by

\displaystyle\Res_{D_{I}}(\eta|_{U})\coloneqq(2\pi\sqrt{-1})^{j}a(z)|_{D_{I}\cap U}\in\mathcal{A}^{k-j}\Big(D_{I},\sum_{p\notin I}D_{p}\cap D_{I},E\Big)(U\cap D_{I}).

(5.0.1)

One can verify that this definition is well-defined and independent of the choice of local coordinates. Consequently, these local constructions glue together to get

\Res_{D_{I}}\eta\in A^{k-j}(D_{I},\sum_{p\notin I}D_{I}\cap D_{p},E).

for each strata
D_{I}
with
|I|=j
.

We write

\Res_{D}\eta=\left(\Res_{D_{i}}\eta\right)_{i=1,\ldots,m}.

We rely on the following fact throughout this section.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\eta\in A^{k}(X,D,E)
. Then we have

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\eta=\iota_{*}(\Res_{D}\eta)+\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta.

(5.1.1)

Démonstration.

It suffices to verify the statement locally. By (
5.0.1
), we have

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\eta=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\iota_{i})_{*}\Res_{D_{i}}\eta+\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta=\iota_{*}(\Res_{D}\eta)+\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta.

The lemma follows immediately.
∎

5.2
Gysin morphism

Recall the complex of sheaves of twisted logarithmic forms

(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E),\nabla):=\mathcal{A}^{0}(X,D,E)\xrightarrow{\nabla}\cdots\xrightarrow{\nabla}\mathcal{A}^{2n}(X,D,E),

(5.1.2)

where
\mathcal{A}^{k}(X,D,E)
denotes the sheaf of smooth differential
k
-forms on
X
with logarithmic singularities along
D
taking values in the flat vector bundle
E
, and the differential
\nabla
is the exterior covariant derivative induced by the flat connection on
E
.

Consider the weight filtration
W_{\bullet}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)
defined by the number of logarithmic poles. Locally,
W_{k}\mathcal{A}^{m}(X,D,E)
is spanned by forms containing at most
k
logarithmic differentials
dz_{i}/z_{i}
. Explicitly, these are linear combinations of terms of the form

\alpha\wedge\frac{dz_{i_{1}}}{z_{i_{1}}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\frac{dz_{i_{j}}}{z_{i_{j}}},

where
\alpha
is a smooth form and
j\leq k
. In this paper, we are primarily interested in the case
W_{1}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)
, which contains forms with at most one logarithmic factor.

For the general filtration, we have the
k
-th residue map

\operatorname{Res}^{(k)}:W_{k}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\to(\iota_{k})_{*}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet-k}_{D^{(k)}}(E),

(5.1.3)

where
\iota_{k}:D^{(k)}\hookrightarrow X
denotes the inclusion. We adopt the convention that
\mathcal{A}^{j}_{D^{(k)}}(E)=0
if
j<0
. Let
W^{\prime}_{k-1}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)
denote the kernel of this residue map, and define its complex of global sections by

W^{\prime}_{k-1}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E):=\Gamma\left(X,W^{\prime}_{k-1}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\right).

(5.1.4)

Since these are fine sheaves, the functor of taking global sections is exact. Consequently, we obtain the following short exact sequence of complexes :

0\to W^{\prime}_{k-1}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\to W_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Res}^{(k)}}A^{\bullet-k}_{D^{(k)}}(E)\to 0.

(5.1.5)

Note that we have the quasi-isomorphism

\displaystyle{\rm Gr}^{W}_{k}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\to(\iota_{k})_{*}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet-k}_{D^{(k)}}(E)

(5.1.6)

Since we have

0\to W_{k-1}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\to W_{k}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\to\gr_{k}^{W}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\to 0,

if we take global sections, we have

0\to W_{k-1}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\to W_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\to\Gamma(X,\gr_{k}^{W}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E))\to 0.

This implies that

\Gamma(X,\gr_{k}^{W}\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}(X,D,E))\simeq\gr_{k}^{W}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E).

Together with (
5.1.6
), the natural morphism

\displaystyle{\rm Gr}^{W}_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\to A^{\bullet-k}_{D^{(k)}}(E)

induced by the residue gives to an isomorphism of their cohomologies

\displaystyle H^{i}({\rm Gr}^{W}_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E))\to H^{i}(A^{\bullet-k}_{D^{(k)}}(E)).

(5.1.7)

It follows that we have the long exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(W_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}({\rm Gr}^{W}_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(W_{k-1}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(W_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}({\rm Gr}^{W}_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(W_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(A^{\bullet-k}_{D^{(k)}}(E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(W^{\prime}_{k-1}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(W_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(A^{\bullet-k}_{D^{(k)}}(E))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

By the Five Lemma, we obtain :

Lemma 5.2
.

The inclusion

i_{k}:W_{k}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E)\hookrightarrow W_{k}^{\prime}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E)

between the two complexes is a quasi-isomorphism.

We write
A^{\bullet}_{X,D}(E):=W_{0}^{\prime}A^{\bullet}(X,D,E)
for simplicity. Then the natural morphism
A^{\bullet}(X,E)\to A^{\bullet}_{X,D}(E)
induces an isomorphism

\displaystyle H^{i}(X,E)\to H^{i}(A^{\bullet}_{X,D}(E)).

(5.2.1)

For any
\beta\in H^{q}(D^{(1)},E)
, we can find an element
\eta\in W_{1}A^{q+1}(X,D,E)
such that
\Res_{D}\eta
is
\nabla
-closed and represents
\beta
. Then by the snake lemma,
\nabla\eta\in A^{q+2}_{X,D}(E)
that is
\nabla
-closed. Hence it represents a cohomology class in
H^{q+2}(A^{\bullet}_{X,D}(E))\simeq H^{q+2}(X,E)

Definition 5.3

(Gysin morphism)
.

The map

\displaystyle g:H^{q}(D^{(1)},E)

\displaystyle\to H^{q+2}(X,E)

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\nabla\eta\}

is called the
Gysin morphism
.

We will the following result.

Lemma 5.4
.

For any
\beta\in A^{q}(D^{(1)},E)
such that
\nabla\beta=0
, we have

g(\{\beta\})=\{-\iota_{*}\beta\},

where
\iota_{*}\beta\in\mathscr{D}^{q+2}(X,E)
denotes the
E
-valued current defined by the pushforward under the natural inclusion
\iota\colon D^{(1)}\to X
.

Démonstration.

Choose an element
\eta\in W_{1}A^{q+1}(X,D,E)
such that
\Res_{D}\eta
is
\nabla
-closed and represents
\beta
. We then have the following formula :

\bm{\nabla}\eta=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\eta+\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\eta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq :diffresi}}}{{=}}\nabla\eta+\iota_{*}(\beta).

Since
\{\bm{\nabla}\eta\}=0
, it follows that

g(\{\beta\})=\{\nabla\eta\}=-\{\iota_{*}(\beta)\}.

The lemma is proved.
∎

5.3
A canonical lift of residue map

Let
\pi_{i}:U_{i}\to D_{i}
be a deformation retraction of some open neighborhood
U_{i}
of
D_{i}
. Let
s_{i}
be a canonical section of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{i})
and let
h_{i}
be a smooth metric for
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D_{i})
. Let
\varrho_{i}
be a smooth function on
X
supported in
U_{i}
, that is equal to 1 in a neighborhood of
D_{i}
.
Consider a flat section
\beta\in H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
. Then we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime}\beta=0,\quad\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta=0.

(5.4.1)

Since
\pi_{i}
is a deformation retraction, it follows that
\pi_{1}(D_{i})\to\pi_{1}(U_{i})
is an isomorphism. Therefore,
\beta
extends to a flat section of
H^{0}(U_{i},E)
, denoted by
\beta_{i}
.
Since
\nabla\beta=0
, it follows that
\nabla\beta_{i}=0
. Therefore, one has

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\nabla^{\prime}(\sum_{i}\varrho_{i}\log|s_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{2}\cdot\beta_{i})\in A^{1}(X,D,E)

(5.4.2)

and the residue

\Res_{D}\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\nabla^{\prime}(\sum_{i}\varrho_{i}\log|s_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{2}\cdot\beta_{i})=-\beta.

Hence,

h(\beta):=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\nabla^{\prime\prime}\nabla^{\prime}(\sum_{i}\varrho_{i}\log|s_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{2}\cdot\beta_{i})\in A^{2}(X,E)

is
\nabla
-closed, and lies in the cohomology class of
g(\beta)
.

If we consider the covariant derivative in the sense of currents, then we have

\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}(\sum_{i}\varrho_{i}\log|s_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{2}\cdot\beta_{i})=-\iota_{*}\beta+h(\beta).

Set

\gamma:=2\pi\sqrt{-1}(\nabla^{\prime})^{*}G(\nabla^{\prime\prime})^{*}G(h(\beta)).

Then

\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\nabla^{\prime\prime}\nabla^{\prime}\gamma=-(1-\mathcal{H})(h(\beta)),

where
\mathcal{H}
is the harmonic projection.
Therefore, we have

\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}(\sum_{i}\varrho_{i}\log|s_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{2}\cdot\beta_{i}+\gamma)=-\iota_{*}(\beta)+\mathcal{H}(h(\beta)).

Hence, we define a map

f:H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)\to A^{1}(X,D,E)

by setting

\displaystyle f(\beta):=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}(\sum_{i}\varrho_{i}\log|s_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{2}\cdot\beta_{i}+\gamma).

(5.4.3)

Then we have

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}f(\beta)=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}f(\beta)=-\iota_{*}\beta+\mathcal{H}(h(\beta))=-\iota_{*}\beta-\mathcal{H}(g(\beta))

(5.4.4)

where
g
is the Gysin morphism defined in
5.3
,
and

{\rm Res}_{D}(f(\beta))=-\beta.

One can check that
f
is a linear map.

5.4
Regularity

Note that although the complex
A^{\bullet}(X,D,E)
is preserved under the Lie bracket, it is not preserved by Green operator
G
or
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}
. Moreover, since the product of currents is generally ill-defined, extending the analytic constructions in
section

4.1
requires establishing additional regularity properties when applying these operators. In this subsection, we establish various regularity theorems used in the construction of
5.14
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\eta\in W_{1}A^{2}(X,D,E)
such that
\eta=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\sigma
, where
\sigma\in\mathscr{D}^{1}(X,E)
. Assume that

—

\nabla\eta=0

—

\Res_{D}\eta\in A^{1}(D^{(1)},E)
is
\nabla
-closed, such that its cohomology class in
H^{1}(D^{(1)},E)
is trivial.

Then
\Res_{D}\eta=0
. In particular,
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\eta=\bm{\nabla}\eta=0
.

Démonstration.

Let
\delta:=\Res_{D}\eta\in A^{1}(D^{(1)},E)
. Then we have

\bm{\nabla}\eta=\iota_{*}(\delta).

Since
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}\eta=0
, it follows that

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}\eta=\iota_{*}\nabla^{\prime}\delta=0.

Since
\iota_{*}:A^{k}(D^{(1)},E)\to\mathscr{D}^{k+2}(X,E)
is injective, it follows that

\nabla^{\prime}\delta=0.

By the assumption,
\delta=\nabla h
for some
h\in A^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
. Therefore, we have

\nabla^{\prime\prime}\nabla^{\prime}h=0.

This implies that

\Lambda\nabla^{\prime\prime}\nabla^{\prime}h=\Delta^{\prime}h=0.

Hence,
\delta=\nabla h=0
.
Therefore, we have

\bm{\nabla}\eta=0,\quad\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\eta=0.

∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\mathbb{B}^{n}
be the unit ball in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Let
\sigma
be a distribution on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
such that

\partial\sigma=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}(z)\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j}.

Assume that
a_{i}(z)
is smooth for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and
a_{i}(z)|_{D_{i}}=0
when restricted on the divisor
D_{i}:=(z_{i}=0)
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
.
Then
\sigma
is a continuous function on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Moreover, for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial(\sigma|_{D_{i}})=\big(\sum_{j=1,\ldots,m;j\neq i}a_{j}(z)\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j}\big)|_{D_{i}}.

(5.6.1)

Démonstration.

Write
\hat{z}_{i}:=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1},z_{i+1},\ldots,z_{n})
. Since
a_{i}
is smooth and vanishes on
(z_{i}=0)
, the functions defined by

f_{i}(\hat{z}_{i}):=\frac{\partial a_{i}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1},0,z_{i+1},\ldots,z_{n})}{\partial z_{i}},\quad g_{i}(\hat{z}_{i}):=\frac{\overline{\partial}a_{i}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{i-1},0,z_{i+1},\ldots,z_{n})}{\partial\overline{z}_{i}},

are smooth functions.
Then we have

\displaystyle b_{i}(z):=a_{i}(z)-z_{i}f_{i}(\hat{z}_{i})-\bar{z}_{i}g_{i}(\hat{z}_{i})\in O(|z_{i}|^{2}).

(5.6.2)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{z_{i}}\frac{\partial b_{i}(z)}{\partial\bar{z}_{i}}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{\varepsilon})

(5.6.3)

for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.
Consider

f:=\sigma-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bar{z}_{i}\log|z_{i}|^{2}g_{i}(\hat{z}_{i})-z_{i}f_{i}(\hat{z}_{i}),

then we have

\displaystyle\partial f

\displaystyle=\partial\sigma-\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\bar{z}_{i}g_{i}(\hat{z}_{i})\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}+\bar{z}_{i}\log|z_{i}|^{2}\partial g_{i}+f_{i}(\hat{z}_{i})dz_{i}+z_{i}\partial f_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(a_{i}(z)-g_{i}(\hat{z}_{i})\bar{z}_{i}-z_{i}f_{i}(\hat{z}_{i}))\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\bar{z}_{i}\log|z_{i}|^{2}\partial g_{i}+z_{i}\partial f_{i})+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}b_{i}(z)\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}(\bar{z}_{i}\log|z_{i}|^{2}\partial g_{i}+z_{i}\partial f_{i})+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j}.

Hence

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}f

\displaystyle=\Lambda\bar{\partial}\partial f=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\partial b_{i}(z)}{\partial\bar{z}_{i}}\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}+\Lambda\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bar{\partial}(\bar{z}_{i}\log|z_{i}|^{2}\partial g_{i}+z_{i}\partial f_{i})+\Lambda\bar{\partial}(\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\partial b_{i}(z)}{\partial\bar{z}_{i}}\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}+{\mbox{ smooth terms}}.

It follows from (
5.6.3
) that
\Delta^{\prime}f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{\varepsilon})
. Therefore,

f\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
. This implies that

\sigma-\sum_{i=1}^{m}\bar{z}_{i}\log|z_{i}|^{2}g_{i}(\hat{z}_{i})\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n}).

In particular,
\sigma
is a continuous function.

Write

h_{i}(z):=\sigma-\bar{z}_{i}\log|z_{i}|^{2}g_{i}(\hat{z}_{i})-z_{i}f_{i}(\hat{z}_{i}),

which is a continuous function. Note that

\sigma|_{D_{i}}=h_{i}|_{D_{i}}.

Since we have

\displaystyle\partial h_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1,\ldots,m;j\neq i}a_{j}(z)\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j}

\displaystyle+(a_{i}-g_{i}(\hat{z}_{i})\overline{z_{i}}-z_{i}f_{i}(\hat{z}_{i}))\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}+(\bar{z}_{i}\log|z_{i}|^{2}\partial g_{i}+z_{i}\partial f_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1,\ldots,m;j\neq i}a_{j}(z)\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{b_{i}(z)}{z_{i}}dz_{i}+(\bar{z}_{i}\log|z_{i}|^{2}\partial g_{i}+z_{i}\partial f_{i})

By (
5.6.2
),

\frac{b_{i}(z)}{z_{i}}
is continuous and
\frac{b_{i}(z)}{z_{i}}|_{D_{i}}=0
. It follows that

\partial(\sigma|_{D_{i}})=\partial(h_{i}|_{D_{i}})=\big(\sum_{j=1,\ldots,m;j\neq i}a_{j}(z)\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j}\big)|_{D_{i}}.

The lemma is proved.
∎

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\eta\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
be such that
\Res_{D}\eta=0
. If there exists some
f\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(X,E)
such that
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}f=\eta
, then
f
is smooth on
X_{0}
and
f\in A^{0}_{\mathrm{cts}}(X,E)
. Moreover, for any
D_{i}
, we have

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}(f|_{D_{i}})\in A^{1}(D_{i},\sum_{j\neq i}D_{j}\cap D_{i},E).

The following property is satisfied : For any coordinate system
(U;z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
such that
D\cap U=(z_{1}\cdots z_{k}=0)
with
D_{i}\cap U=(z_{1}=0)
, if we write

\eta|_{U}=\sum_{j=1,\ldots,k}a_{j}(z)\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j},

then we have

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}(f|_{D_{i}})|_{U\cap D_{i}}=\big(\sum_{j=2,\ldots,k}a_{j}(z)\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}+\sum_{j=k+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j}\big)|_{(z_{1}=0)}.

(5.7.1)

In the above setting, we shall abusively write

\displaystyle\eta|_{D_{i}}:=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}(f|_{D_{i}})\in A^{1}(D_{i},\sum_{j\neq i}D_{i}\cap D_{j},E).

(5.7.2)

Then for any
j\neq i
, by (
5.7.1
), we have

\displaystyle\Res_{D_{i}\cap D_{j}}(\eta|_{D_{i}})=(\Res_{D_{j}}\eta)|_{D_{i}\cap D_{j}}=0.

(5.7.3)

Proof of
5.7
.

Fix any point
p\in D
. We take admissible coordinates
(U;z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
centered at
p
such that
U\cap D=(z_{1}\cdots z_{m}=0)
. Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}
be a flat frame for
E|_{U}
. Then

\nabla^{\prime\prime}e_{i}=\nabla^{\prime}e_{i}=0.

Let us write
f=\sum_{i}f_{i}e_{i}
and
\eta=\sum_{i}\eta_{i}e_{i}

where
f_{i}\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(U)
and
\eta_{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(X,D)(U)
. Then we have

\bm{\partial}f_{i}=\eta_{i}.

We write

\eta_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}(z)\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}a_{j}(z)dz_{j}

such that
a_{j}(z)
is a smooth function for each
j
. Since
\Res_{D}\eta=0
, it follows that
a_{j}(z)|_{(z_{j}=0)}\equiv 0
for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. By
5.6
,
f_{i}
is continuous, and hence
f
is continuous. The second assertion follows from (
5.6.1
). The lemma is proved.
∎

The following criterion determines when the Lie bracket of two log forms is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
. Assume that
\eta_{1}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-closed and

\eta_{2}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact. Then
[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.

Démonstration.

As
\eta_{2}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact, we have
\eta_{2}=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\sigma
for some current
\sigma
. As
\sigma
is of degree
0
, we have

(\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}+(\bm{\nabla}^{\prime})^{*})\sigma=\eta_{2}.

As
\eta_{2}\in L^{2-\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
, by the elliptic regularity of
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}+(\bm{\nabla}^{\prime})^{*}
, we know that
\sigma\in L^{2+c_{0}}
for some
c_{0}>0
and
\sigma
is smooth on
X\backslash D
.
Since
\eta_{1}
has logarithmic poles, the bracket product

[\eta_{1},\sigma]\in L^{1},

is a well-defined current in
\mathscr{D}^{1}(X,E)
.

Now we show that

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}[\eta_{1},\sigma]=-[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\qquad\text{ on }X

in the sense of currents.
Let
\mu_{\varepsilon}
be the standard cut-off functions with respect to
X\setminus D
, namely

\mu_{\varepsilon}=\rho_{\varepsilon}(\log\log\frac{1}{|s_{D}|^{2}_{h}}),

where
s_{D}
is the canonical section of
\mathcal{O}_{X}(D)
,
h
is a smooth metric,
\rho_{\varepsilon}
is a function which is equal to
1
on
[1,\varepsilon^{-1}]
and is equal to
0
on
[1+\varepsilon^{-1},+\infty[
and
|\rho_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}|\leq 2
.

Let
\beta\in A^{2n-2}(X,E^{*})
. Then

\int_{X}[\eta_{1},\sigma]\wedge(\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\beta)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{X}\mu_{\varepsilon}\cdot[\eta_{1},\sigma]\wedge(\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\beta)

=-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{X}\partial\mu_{\varepsilon}\wedge[\eta_{1},\sigma]\wedge\beta-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{X}\mu_{\varepsilon}\cdot\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}[\eta_{1},\sigma]\wedge\beta.

We now compute the two terms on the right-hand side. Note that on the support of
\mu_{\varepsilon}
,
\eta_{1}
and
\sigma
are smooth. We have thus
\mu_{\varepsilon}\cdot\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}[\eta_{1},\sigma]=-\mu_{\varepsilon}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
.
Therefore we have

-\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{X}\mu_{\varepsilon}\cdot\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}[\eta_{1},\sigma]\wedge\beta=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{X}\mu_{\varepsilon}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\wedge\beta=\int_{X}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\wedge\beta.

For the other term
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{X}\partial\rho_{\varepsilon}\wedge[\eta_{1},\sigma]\wedge\beta
, note that

\partial\mu_{\varepsilon}\wedge\eta_{1}=\rho^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1}{\log\frac{1}{|s_{D}|^{2}_{h}}}(\partial(\log|s_{D}|^{2}_{h})\wedge\eta_{1}).

As
\partial(\log|s_{D}|^{2}_{h})\wedge\eta_{1}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
and
\sigma\in L^{2+c_{0}}
for some
c_{0}>0
,
we know that

\partial(\log|s_{D}|^{2}_{h})\wedge\eta_{1}\cdot\sigma\in L^{1}.

Then by Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{X}\partial\mu_{\varepsilon}\wedge[\eta_{1},\sigma]\wedge\beta=0.

As a consequence, we obtain

\int_{X}[\eta_{1},\sigma]\wedge(\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\beta)=-\int_{X}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\wedge\beta.

Thus,
[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\left(-[\eta_{1},\sigma]\right)
in the sense of currents. The lemma is thus proved.
∎

Corollary 5.9
.

Let
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
. We assume that
\eta_{1}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-closed and
\eta_{2}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact, and
\Res_{D}\eta_{2}=0
. Then
\Res_{D}(\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2})
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.

Démonstration.

By
5.7
and (
5.7.1
), we can check that, for each
D_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\Res_{D_{i}}(\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2})=\Res_{D_{i}}(\eta_{1})\wedge(\eta_{2}|_{D_{i}})-(\eta_{1}|_{D_{i}})\wedge\Res_{D_{i}}(\eta_{2})=\Res_{D_{i}}(\eta_{1})\wedge(\eta_{2}|_{D_{i}}),

(5.9.1)

where
\eta_{1}|_{D_{i}}
and
\eta_{2}|_{D_{i}}
are defined in (
5.7.2
).
By
5.7
again,
\eta_{2}|_{D_{i}}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact. It follows that

\Res_{D_{i}}(\eta_{1})\wedge(\eta_{2}|_{D_{i}})

is also
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact. The corollary is thus proved.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
\eta\in A^{2}(X,D,E)
such that
\eta
is
\bm{\nabla}
-closed and
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact in the sense of currents. If we define

\omega:=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime*}G\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G\eta=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G\eta,

then

\omega\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
, and
\omega
is the unique element in
\mathscr{D}^{1}(X,E)
that is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact, and

\bm{\nabla}\omega=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\omega=\eta.

Démonstration.

It is straightforward to see that

\omega=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime*}G\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G\eta=(1-\mathcal{H})\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G\eta-\bm{\nabla}^{\prime*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}G\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G\eta=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G\eta.

If
\bm{\nabla}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}f=0
for some
f\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(X,E)
, then by elliptic regularity,
f
is smooth. So
f
is pluri-harmonic. By the compactness of
X
, we know that
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}f=0
. This in particular shows the uniqueness of
\omega
.

Now we would like to show that
\omega\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
. Write
f:=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G\eta.

Fix any point
p\in D
. We take an admissible coordinate
(U;z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
centered at
p
such that
U\cap D=(z_{1}\cdots z_{m}=0)
. Let
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}
be a flat frame for
E|_{U}
. Then

\nabla^{\prime\prime}e_{i}=\nabla^{\prime}e_{i}=0

Let us write
f=\sum_{i}f_{i}e_{i}
and
\eta=\sum_{i}\eta_{i}e_{i}

where
f_{i}\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(U)
and
\eta_{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{2}(X,D)(U)
. Then we have

\bm{\bar{\partial}}\bm{\partial}f_{i}=\eta_{i}.

Therefore,
\eta_{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{1,1}(X,D)(U)
.

Note that
(\mathcal{A}^{p,\bullet}(X,D),\bar{\partial})
is a fine resolution for
\Omega_{X}^{p}(\log D)
. It follows that there exists some
h_{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{1,0}(X,D)(U)
such that

\bar{\partial}h_{i}=\eta_{i}.

Since
\Res_{D}\eta_{i}=0
, it follows that
\Res_{D_{j}}\bar{\partial}h_{i}=0
, which implies that

\bar{\partial}\Res_{D_{j}}h_{i}=0.

Hence
\Res_{D_{j}}h_{i}
is a holomorphic function on
D_{j}\cap U
. Define a holomorphic function
g_{i,j}\in\mathcal{O}(U)
such that
g_{i,j}|_{D_{j}}=\Res_{D_{j}}h_{i}
. We replace
h_{i}
by

h_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{m}g_{i,j}\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}.

Then
\Res_{D}h_{i}=0
. It follows from
5.1
that

\bm{\bar{\partial}}h_{i}=\bar{\partial}h_{i}=\eta_{i}.

Therefore, we have

\bm{\bar{\partial}}(h_{i}-\bm{\partial}f_{i})=0.

By the regularity of
\bar{\partial}
-operator, it follows that
h_{i}-\bm{\partial}f_{i}
is a holomorphic 1-form. Hence
\bm{\partial}f_{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{1}(X,D)(U)
. Therefore, we obtain

\omega=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}f\in A^{1}(X,D,E).

∎

Following
[
CDHP25
,
LRW19
]
, we prove an effective estimate of the solution in Proposition
5.10
.
To begin with, we consider the vector bundle

E_{p}:=\Omega_{X}^{p}(\log D)\otimes E

on
X
. We have an identification

\phi:A^{i}(X,D,E)\simeq\oplus_{p+q=i}C^{\infty}_{(0,q)}(X,E_{p})

We equip the vector bundle
E_{p}
with a fixed smooth hermitian metric
h_{p}
, and we fix a Kähler metric
\omega_{X}
on
X
. For any constant
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and any
\alpha\in A^{i}(X,D,E)
, we define

\|\alpha\|_{W^{k}}:=\|\phi(\alpha)\|_{W^{k}},

(5.10.1)

where
\|\phi(\alpha)\|_{W^{k}}
is the norm of the Sobolev space whose derivatives up to order
k
are
L^{2}
with respect to
h_{p}
and
\omega_{X}
. We have the following effective version of Proposition
5.10
.

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
\eta\in A^{2}(X,D,E)
such that
\eta
is
\bm{\nabla}
-closed and
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact in the sense of currents. Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.
Let
\omega\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
be the unique
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact solution of

\bm{\nabla}\omega=\eta\qquad\text{ on }X

as in Proposition
5.10
. Then there exists a constant
C
(depending only on
k
) such that

\|\omega\|_{W^{k}}\leq C\|\eta\|_{W^{k}}.

Démonstration.

We follow ideas from the proof of
[
CDHP25
, Thm 8.7]
. Suppose by contradiction that there is no such uniform constant
C
. Then there exists a sequence of
\eta_{i}\in A^{2}(X,D,E)
and
\omega_{i}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
such that
\omega_{i}\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
,

\bm{\nabla}\omega_{i}=\eta_{i}\qquad\text{ on }X,

\|\omega_{i}\|_{W^{k}}=1
for every
i
, and
\lim_{i\to+\infty}\|\eta_{i}\|_{W^{k}}\to 0
.

After passing to some subsequence, we may assume that
\omega_{i}
converges weakly to some
\omega
in
W^{k}
. Then
\bm{\nabla}\omega=0
, and by the closeness of
\operatorname{Im}\nabla^{\prime}
in the Hodge decomposition, it follows that
\omega\in\operatorname{Im}\nabla^{\prime}
. Therefore
\omega=0
.

For every open set
U\Subset X\setminus D
, by elliptic regularity, we know that
\omega_{i}|_{U}
converges strongly to
\omega|_{U}=0
in
W^{k}(U)
. Near the boundary
D
, we suppose that
D
is defined locall by
\prod_{s\in I}z_{s}=0
. Then
\omega_{i}
is locally of type

\omega_{i}=\sum_{s\in I}\frac{dz_{s}}{z_{s}}f_{i,s}+g_{i,s},

where
g_{i,s}
is
(0,1)
-type or
(1,0)
-type with
dz_{s}
for
s\notin I
.
By using the fact that
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\omega_{i}=0
and
\bm{\nabla}\omega_{i}\to 0
, we know that

\bar{\partial}f_{i,s}\to 0

in the
W^{k}
sense.
Together with the fact that
f_{i,s}\to 0
on
U\Subset X\setminus D
in
W^{k}
, we know that
f_{i,s}\to 0
near
D
in the
W^{k}
sense. For the term
g_{i,s}
, by the same argument, we know also that
g_{i,s}\to 0
near
D
in the
W^{k}
sense. As a consequence, we know that
\omega_{i}\to 0
in
W^{k}
. This contradicts the fact that
\|\omega_{i}\|_{W^{k}}=1
for every
i
.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

Consider
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
. Assume that

—

\bm{\nabla}\eta_{i}=\nabla\eta_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

—

\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
are both
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.

Then

(i)

\bm{\nabla}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]=\nabla[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}],
or equivalently,
\Res_{D}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]=0
.

(ii)

[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.

Démonstration.

Fix any point
p\in D
. We take admissible coordinates
(U;z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
centered at
p
such that
U\cap D=(z_{1}\cdots z_{m}=0)
. With these coordinates, we can write

\eta_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}\otimes a_{i}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}dz_{j}\otimes e_{j}

\eta_{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}\otimes b_{i}+\sum_{j=m+1}^{n}dz_{j}\otimes f_{j}

where
a_{i},b_{i},e_{j},f_{j}\in A^{0}(U,E)
. Since
\bm{\nabla}\eta_{i}=\nabla\eta_{i}
, by
5.1
, we have
\iota_{i}^{*}a_{i}=0
and
\iota_{i}^{*}b_{i}=0
for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Here
\iota_{i}:D_{i}\to X
is the inclusion map. Note that

\displaystyle[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq m}([a_{i},b_{j}]-[a_{j},b_{i}])\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}\wedge\frac{dz_{j}}{z_{j}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{1\leq i\leq m<j\leq n}([a_{i},f_{j}]-[e_{j},b_{i}])\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}\wedge dz_{j}+\sum_{m\leq i<j\leq n}([e_{i},f_{j}]-[e_{j},f_{i}])dz_{i}\wedge dz_{j}

It follows that
\Res_{D}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]=0
. This implies that
\bm{\nabla}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]=\nabla[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
by
5.1
. The first item is proved.

By
5.7
and our assumption, there exists
\sigma_{i}\in A^{0}_{\mathrm{cts}}(X,E)
such that
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\sigma_{i}=\eta_{i}
. Therefore,
[\sigma_{1},\eta_{2}]\in\mathscr{D}^{1}(X,E)
, and by the proof of
5.8
, we have

[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}[\sigma_{1},\eta_{2}].

Hence
[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact. The lemma is thus proved.
∎

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\eta=f(\beta)\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
for some
\beta\in H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
, where
f
is defined in (
5.4.3
). Assume also that for any
i<j
, we have

\displaystyle[\iota_{ij}^{*}(\beta|_{D_{i}}),\iota_{ji}^{*}(\beta|_{D_{j}})]=0,

(5.13.1)

where we denote by
\iota_{ij}:D_{ij}\to D_{i}
the natural map.
Then there exists some
h\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
such that

\displaystyle\Res_{D}[\eta,\eta]

\displaystyle=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}h

(5.13.2)

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\Res_{D}[\eta,\eta]

\displaystyle=2[\beta,\iota^{*}\nabla\eta].

(5.13.3)

In particular, if
g(\beta)=0
, where
g
is the Gysin morphism, then we have

\displaystyle\Res_{D}[\eta,\eta]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}[\eta,\eta]

\displaystyle=0.

Démonstration.

We keep the notations from
Section

5.3
and do not recall their meaning. By (
5.4.4
), there exists some
\gamma\in A^{0}(X,E)
such that we have

\displaystyle f(\beta):=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\varrho_{i}\log|s_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{2}\cdot\beta_{i}+\gamma)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\partial(\varrho_{i}\log|s_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{2})\beta_{i}+\nabla^{\prime}\gamma)

We then have

-4\pi^{2}[\eta,\eta]=\sum_{i\neq j}\partial(\varrho_{i}\log|s_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{2})\wedge\partial(\varrho_{j}\log|s_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})[\beta_{i},\beta_{j}]+2[\sum_{i=1}^{m}\partial(\varrho_{i}\log|s_{i}|_{h_{i}}^{2})\beta_{i},\nabla^{\prime}\gamma]+[\nabla^{\prime}\gamma,\nabla^{\prime}\gamma].

It follows that

\displaystyle\Res_{D_{i}}[\eta,\eta]

\displaystyle=\frac{-\sqrt{-1}}{\pi}\left([\beta|_{D_{i}},\iota_{i}^{*}(\sum_{j\neq i}\partial(\varrho_{j}\log|s_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})\beta_{j}+\nabla^{\prime}\gamma)]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\sqrt{-1}}{\pi}\left([\beta|_{D_{i}},\iota_{i}^{*}(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\partial(\varrho_{j}\log|s_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2})\beta_{j}+\nabla^{\prime}\gamma)]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\sqrt{-1}}{\pi}\left([\beta|_{D_{i}},\iota_{i}^{*}\eta]\right)

(5.13.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\sqrt{-1}}{\pi}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\left([\beta|_{D_{i}},\iota_{i}^{*}(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\varrho_{j}\log|s_{j}|_{h_{j}}^{2}\beta_{j}+\gamma)]\right)

(5.13.5)

This proves (
5.13.2
).

Let us prove (
5.13.3
). Recall that we have assumed that
\Res_{D_{ij}}[\eta,\eta]=0
for any
1\leq i<j\leq m
. By (
5.13.4
) and (
5.13.1
), this implies that for any fixed
j\neq i
, we have

\Res_{D_{j}\cap D_{i}}\Res_{D_{i}}[\eta,\eta]=2[\iota_{ij}^{*}(\beta|_{D_{i}}),\iota_{ji}^{*}(\beta|_{D_{j}})]=0

for each
j>i
.
5.1
together with (
5.13.5
) imply that

\bm{\nabla}\Res_{D_{i}}[\eta,\eta]=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\Res_{D_{i}}[\eta,\eta]=\nabla^{\prime\prime}\Res_{D_{i}}[\eta,\eta]=2[\beta,\iota^{*}\nabla\eta].

Equation

5.13.3
is thus proved.

If
g(\beta)=0
, then by the construction of
f
, we have
\nabla\eta=0
and
\bm{\nabla}\eta=\iota_{*}(\beta)
. By (
5.13.3
),

\bm{\nabla}\Res_{D}[\eta,\eta]=0.

By (
5.13.2
),

\Delta^{\prime}h=\sqrt{-1}\Lambda\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}h=\sqrt{-1}\Lambda\bm{\nabla}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}h=0.

Hence

\Res_{D}[\eta,\eta]=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}h=0.

The last claim is proved.
The lemma is proved.
∎

5.5
Explicit construction of the deformation

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D
be a simple normal crossing divisor on
X
. Let
(V,\nabla)
be a unitary bundle on
X
. Denote by
(E,\nabla):=(\End(V),\nabla)
. Assume that there exist
\eta_{1}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,E)
and
\beta\in H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
(it might happen that
\eta_{1}=0
or
\beta=0
) such that

(a)

g(\beta)+\frac{1}{2}\{[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}]\}=0
, where
g:H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)\to H^{2}(X,E)
is the Gysin morphism.

(b)

\{[\iota^{*}\eta_{1},\beta]\}=0\in H^{1}(D^{(1)},E)
, where
\iota:D^{(1)}\to X
is the natural morphism.

(c)

For any
i\neq j
,
[\iota_{ij}^{*}(\beta|_{D_{i}}),\iota_{ji}^{*}(\beta|_{D_{j}})]=0\in H^{0}(D_{i}\cap D_{j},E)
, where
\iota_{ij}:D_{i}\cap D_{j}\to D_{i}
is the natural morphism.

Then

(i)

for every
i\geq 2
, there exists
\eta_{i}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)\cap\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
such that they solve the following equations :

—

\Res_{D}\eta_{2}=-\beta
and

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\eta_{2}=-\iota_{*}(\beta)-\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{1}

(5.14.1)

—

For every
k\geq 3
, we have

\eta_{k}:=-\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G(\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{k-i}),

such that

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\eta_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{k-i}.

(5.14.2)

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\Res_{D}\eta_{k}=0\quad\mbox{for }k\geq 3.

(5.14.3)

(ii)

There exists some
\varepsilon>0
such that for any
t\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}t^{i}\eta_{i}\in A^{1}(X,D,E).

As a consequence, we have an
analytic
familly of
logarithmic connections

\nabla_{t}
for
V
parametrized by
t\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
in the following explicit way :

\nabla_{t}:=\nabla+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}t^{i}\eta_{i},

with its
(0,1)
-part given by

\nabla_{t}^{\prime\prime}=\nabla^{\prime\prime}+t\eta_{1}^{0,1}.

(iii)

Let
f:Y\to X
be a holomorphic map from a compact Kähler manifold
Y
such that
D_{Y}:=f^{-1}(D)
is a simple normal crossing divisor. Let
f_{0}
denote the restriction
f|_{Y_{0}}
. If
f^{*}\eta_{1}=0
and
f^{*}\eta_{2}=0
, then
f_{0}^{*}\nabla_{t}=f_{0}^{*}\nabla
for all
t\in\mathbb{D}_{\varepsilon}
.

Démonstration.

Step 1.

Construction
\eta_{2}
.

Consider the map

\displaystyle f:H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)\to A^{1}(X,D,E)

(5.14.4)

defined in (
5.4.3
). Then by (
5.4.4
), we have

\Res_{D}f(\beta)=-\beta
,
f(\beta)
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact and

\bm{\nabla}f(\beta)=-\mathcal{H}(g(\beta))-\iota_{*}(\beta).

We define

\eta_{2}:=f(\beta)-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\prime\prime*}G[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}].

Then we have

\bm{\nabla}\eta_{2}=-\mathcal{H}(g(\beta))-\iota_{*}(\beta)+(\mathcal{H}-1)\frac{1}{2}[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}].

Item

(a)
implies that,

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\eta_{2}=-\iota_{*}(\beta)-\frac{1}{2}[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}].

(5.14.5)

It remains to check that
\eta_{2}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
. Indeed,

\bm{\nabla}(\eta_{2}-f(\beta))=-\frac{1}{2}[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}]+\mathcal{H}(g(\beta))\in A^{2}(X,E).

As
\eta_{2}+f(\beta)\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
, by
2.16
, we know that
\eta_{2}+f(\beta)\in A^{1}(X,E)
. Therefore
\eta_{2}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
.
Since
f(\beta)
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact, and

\nabla^{\prime\prime*}G[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}]=\nabla^{\prime}\nabla^{\prime*}G\nabla^{\prime\prime*}G[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}],

it follows that
\eta_{2}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.

Step 2.

Constructing
\eta_{3}
.
Since
\eta_{2}\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
, and
\eta_{1}
is harmonic, we know that

[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\cap W_{1}A^{2}(X,D,E).

Note that

\displaystyle\nabla[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]=\frac{1}{2}[\eta_{1},[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}]]=0

(5.14.6)

by the Jacobian identity. We also have

\Res_{D}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]=[\iota^{*}\eta_{1},\beta]=0\in H^{1}(D^{(1)},E),

where the last equality comes from
Item

(b)
.
Then by
5.1
,
[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\cap\ker\bm{\nabla}\cap A^{2}(X,D,E)
.

Let us define

\eta_{3}:=-\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}].

Applying
2.16
and
Proposition

5.10
to
[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]
, it follows that
\eta_{3}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)\cap\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
, and that

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\eta_{3}=-[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}].

(5.14.7)

In particular, this implies that

\displaystyle\Res_{D}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]=0.

(5.14.8)

Step 3.

Constructing
\eta_{4}
.

We consider
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{4-i}
. Thanks to Lemma
5.8
,
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{4-i}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.

Now we show that
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{4-i}
is
\bm{\nabla}
-closed.

\displaystyle\nabla(\frac{1}{2}[\eta_{2},\eta_{2}]+[\eta_{1},\eta_{3}])=[\eta_{2},\eta_{2}]+[\eta_{1},\eta_{3}]=-\frac{1}{2}[\eta_{2},[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}]]+[\eta_{1},-[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]]=0

(5.14.9)

By the Jacobian identity, we have

\displaystyle[\eta_{2},[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}]]-[\eta_{1},[\eta_{2},\eta_{1}]]+[\eta_{1},[\eta_{1},\eta_{2}]]=0

(5.14.10)

By
5.1
,
\Res_{D}\eta_{3}=0
. Hence

\Res_{D}[\eta_{1},\eta_{3}]=0.

Hence we have

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}(\frac{1}{2}[\eta_{2},\eta_{2}]+[\eta_{1},\eta_{3}])=\frac{1}{2}\iota_{*}(\Res_{D}[\eta_{2},\eta_{2}]).

(5.14.11)

Using
Item

(c)
and by
5.13
, we know that
\Res_{D_{i}}[\eta_{2},\eta_{2}]\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
and

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\Res_{D_{i}}[\eta_{2},\eta_{2}]

\displaystyle=2[\beta_{i},\iota^{*}\nabla\eta_{2}]

\displaystyle=-[\beta_{i},\iota^{*}[\eta_{1},\eta_{1}]]

\displaystyle=2[\iota^{*}\eta_{1},[\beta_{i},\iota^{*}\eta_{1}]]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq :residue}}}{{=}}0.

Hence we have
\Res_{D_{i}}[\eta_{2},\eta_{2}]\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\cap\ker\bm{\nabla}
. By
2.16
and the fact that
\Res_{D_{i}}[\eta_{2},\eta_{2}]
is a
1
-form, we know that
\Res_{D_{i}}[\eta_{2},\eta_{2}]=0
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{4-i})=0.

(5.14.12)

Since
\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{4-i}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{4-i}\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\cap\ker\bm{\nabla}\cap A^{1}(X,D,E).

Let us define

\eta_{4}:=-\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{4-i}).

By
Proposition

5.10
,
\eta_{4}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)\cap\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
and

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\eta_{4}=-(\sum_{i=1}^{3}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{4-i}).

(5.14.13)

In particular,
\Res_{D}\eta_{4}=0
.

Step 4.

Constructing general
\eta_{k}
for
k\geq 5
.
The construction of higher order
\eta_{k}
is similar and we continue this process by induction. Assume that for some
k\geq 5
, we obtain a sequence of
\eta_{3},\ldots,\eta_{k-1}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
such that for any
i\in\{3,\ldots,k-1\}
, we have

(a)

\eta_{i}\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}

(b)

\bm{\nabla}\eta_{i}=-\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\eta_{j}\wedge\eta_{i-j}

(c)

\Res_{D}\eta_{i}=0
.

Let us define

\gamma_{k}:=-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\eta_{j}\wedge\eta_{k-j}.

It follows from
Items

(a)
and
(b)
together with
5.8
that
\gamma_{k}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.
Now we prove that
\gamma_{k}
is
\bm{\nabla}
-closed. First, by indcution (
(b)
), we have

\displaystyle-\nabla\gamma_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\nabla\eta_{j}\wedge\eta_{k-j}-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\eta_{j}\wedge\nabla\eta_{k-j}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{j-i-1}\wedge\eta_{k-j}+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{k-j-1}\eta_{j}\wedge\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{k-j-i-1}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i+j+s=k-1}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{j}\wedge\eta_{s}+\sum_{i+j+s=k-1}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{j}\wedge\eta_{s}=0,

where the index
i,j,s\geqq 1
.

By (
5.9.1
), for each
D_{i}
, we have

\displaystyle\Res_{D_{i}}\gamma_{k}

\displaystyle=-\Res_{D_{i}}\eta_{2}\wedge\eta_{k-2}+\eta_{k-2}\wedge\Res_{D_{i}}\eta_{2}

\displaystyle=-\beta_{i}\wedge(\eta_{k-2}|_{D_{i}})+(\eta_{k-2}|_{D_{i}})\wedge\beta_{i}.

Here
(\eta_{k-2}|_{D_{i}})
is defined in (
5.7.2
).
By
Item

(c)
together with (
5.7.3
), we have

\Res_{D_{i}\cap D_{j}}(\eta_{k-2}|_{D_{i}})=0

for each
j\neq i
.
This implies that

\displaystyle\Res_{D_{j}\cap D_{i}}\Res_{D_{i}}\gamma_{k}=0.

(5.14.14)

for each
j\neq i
.

As
(\eta_{k-2}|_{D_{i}})
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact by (
5.7.2
),
\beta_{i}\wedge(\eta_{k-2}|_{D_{i}})
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.
It follows that

\Res_{D_{i}}\gamma_{k}

is also
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.
Note that

\nabla\Res_{D_{i}}\gamma_{k}=-\Res_{D_{i}}(\nabla\gamma_{k})=0.

By
5.1
together with (
5.14.14
),

\bm{\nabla}\Res_{D_{i}}\gamma_{k}=0

for each
i
.
Then

\Res_{D_{i}}\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\cap\ker\bm{\nabla}.

Since
\Res_{D_{i}}\gamma_{k}\in A^{1}(D_{i},\sum_{j\neq i}D_{j}\cap D_{i},E)
, by
2.16
,
\Res_{D_{i}}\gamma_{k}=0
. It follows from
5.1
that
\gamma_{k}
is
\bm{\nabla}
-closed. Hence we have

\gamma_{k}\in\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\cap\ker\bm{\nabla}.

Let us define

\eta_{k}:=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G\gamma_{k}.

Then by
Proposition

5.10
,
\eta_{k}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)\cap\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
and we have

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\eta_{k}=-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\eta_{j}\wedge\eta_{k-j}.

In particular,
\Res_{D}\eta_{k}=0
.
Therefore,
Items

(a)
,
(b)
and
(c)
all hold for
\eta_{k}
. So the induction is proved. We conclude the existence of each
\eta_{k}
that satisfies the above conditions. This proves Item (i).

Step 5.
We would like to show the convergence of
\sum_{i}t^{i}\eta_{i}
for
|t|\ll 1
.

By our construction, for
k\geq 3
,
\eta_{k}
is the unique solution such that
\eta_{k}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact and satisfying

\bm{\nabla}\eta_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\eta_{i}\wedge\eta_{k-i}.

By using Corollary
5.11
, there exists some
uniform
constant
C>1
such that

\|\eta_{k}\|_{W^{1}}\leq C(\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\|\eta_{i}\|_{W^{1}}\cdot\|\eta_{k-i}\|_{W^{1}}),

where the
W^{1}
-norm is defined in (
5.10.1
).
Therefore by induction and the recurrence formula for Catalan numbers, we have

\|\eta_{k}\|_{W^{1}}\leq\sum_{n=\lceil k/2\rceil}^{k}\frac{1}{n}\binom{2n-2}{n-1}C^{n-1}\binom{n}{k-n}\|\eta_{1}\|_{W^{1}}^{2n-k}\|\eta_{2}\|_{W^{1}}^{k-n}.

This yields the following estimate valid for all
k\geq 3
:

\|\eta_{k}\|_{W^{1}}\leq\frac{1}{2C}\Lambda^{k}.

(5.14.15)

where

\Lambda=2C\|\eta_{1}\|_{W^{1}}+\sqrt{4C^{2}\|\eta_{1}\|_{W^{1}}^{2}+4C\|\eta_{2}\|_{W^{1}}}.

Then
\sum_{i}t^{i}\eta_{i}
converges in the
W^{1}
-normed space for
|t|\leq\frac{1}{2\Lambda}
.

Set
\eta(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}t^{i}\eta_{i}
. It remains to show that
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}t^{i}\eta_{i}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
for
|t|\ll 1
.
By the above paragraph, we know that
\|\eta(t)\|_{W^{1}}<+\infty
for
|t|\ll 1
.
Moreover, by construction, we have

\nabla\eta(t)+\eta(t)\wedge\eta(t)=0.

Moreover, as
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\eta_{i}=0
for every
i
, we know that
and
\nabla^{\prime}\eta(t)=0
.

Then by the standard elliptic bootstrapping argument, we know that
\eta(t)\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
. To see this, we suppose by induction that
\eta(t)
is in
W^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
in the sense of (
5.10.1
).
We suppose that
D
is locally defined by
\prod_{i\in I}z_{i}=0
.
Then
\eta(t)
can be locally written as

\eta(t)=\sum_{i\in I}f_{i}\frac{dz_{i}}{z_{i}}+g

where
f_{i}
is
E
-valued function in
W^{k}
and
g\in W^{k}
is of
(0,1)
-type or
(1,0)
not containing
dz_{i}
for
i\in I
.
As
\nabla(\eta(t))
and
\nabla^{\prime}\eta(t)
are in
W^{k}
, we know that
\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta(t)
is also in
W^{k}
. This implies that
\bar{\partial}f_{i}\in W^{k}
.
Then
f_{i}\in W^{k+1}
by elliptic regularity. The term
g
is also in
W^{k+1}
by the classical elliptic estimates. Therefore
\eta(t)
is in
W^{k+1}
. It follows that
\eta(t)\in W^{k}
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\eta(t)\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
. Furthermore, since
\eta_{i}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)\cap\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
for
i\geq 2
, we have

\nabla_{t}^{\prime\prime}=\nabla^{\prime\prime}+t\eta_{1}^{0,1}.

This proves Item (ii).

Step 6.
We now prove Item (iii). It suffices to show that
f^{*}\eta_{k}=0
for all
k\geq 2
. We proceed by induction on
k
. The base cases
k=1,2
hold by assumption. Assume that
f^{*}\eta_{i}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
. Then by (
5.14.2
), we have

\bm{\nabla}f^{*}\eta_{k}=-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}f^{*}\eta_{j}\wedge f^{*}\eta_{k-j}=0.

(5.14.16)

Since
\eta_{k}
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact, its pullback
f^{*}\eta_{k}
lies in
\operatorname{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\cap A^{1}(Y,D_{Y},f^{*}E)
. By
2.16
, we conclude that
f^{*}\eta_{k}=0
. This completes the inductive step and the proof of Item (iii).

The theorem is proved.
∎

5.6
Precise Zigzag between dglas

Consider the global dgla
(T^{\bullet},d)
defined by the logarithmic Dolbeault complex :

(T^{\bullet},d):=A^{0}(X,D,E)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\to}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla}}{{\to}}A^{2\dim X}(X,D,E).

Although this dgla is not formal in general, we can nevertheless construct a quasi-isomorphic dgla
(C^{\bullet},d)
where each
C^{i}
is a finite-dimensional vector space. This construction enables us to compute the analytic germ that pro-represents the deformation functor associated with
(T^{\bullet},d)
. Roughly speaking, the terms of
(C^{\bullet},d)
are formed by the direct sums of the cohomology groups of the divisor strata :

C^{k}:=\bigoplus_{p+q=k}H^{q}(D^{(p)},E).

The differential
d:C^{k}\to C^{k+1}
is defined using Gysin morphisms with suitable signs, and the graded Lie bracket is naturally induced by the intersection structure. Precise definitions will be given later in this subsection.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
L^{\bullet}:=(\ker\nabla^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime\prime})
. Then
\phi:L^{\bullet}\to(A^{\bullet}(X,D,E),\nabla)
is 1-equivalent.

Démonstration.

It is obvious that
H^{0}(\phi)
is an isomorphism. Note that by the degeneration of
E_{1}
-level of Deligne (see also
[
LRW19
]
), we have

H^{1}(A^{\bullet}(X,D,E),\nabla)=H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D)\otimes E)\oplus H^{0,1}(X,E).

Here we endow
E
with the holomorphic vector bundle structure induced by
\nabla^{\prime\prime}
. Assume that
\alpha\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
such that
\nabla^{\prime}\alpha=0
and
\nabla^{\prime\prime}\alpha=0
. We decompose
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}+\alpha^{\prime\prime}
, where
\alpha^{\prime}\in A^{1,0}(X,D,E)
and
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in A^{0,1}(X,E)
. Then we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime\prime}\alpha^{\prime}=0\quad\nabla^{\prime\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}=0

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}=0\quad\nabla^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime\prime}=0.

This implies that
\alpha^{\prime}\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D)\otimes E)
and
\alpha^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X,E)
. Hence,
\phi
induces an isomorphism for
H^{1}
.

Let us prove that
H^{2}(\phi)
is injective. Again by the degeneration at the
E_{1}
-level, we have

H^{2}(A^{\bullet}(X,D,E),\nabla)=H^{0}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X}(\log D)\otimes E)\oplus H^{0,2}(X,E)\oplus H^{1}(X,\Omega_{X}^{1}(\log D)\otimes E).

Assume that
\alpha\in A^{2}(X,D,E)
such that
\nabla^{\prime}\alpha=0
and
\nabla^{\prime\prime}\alpha=0
. We decompose
\alpha=\alpha^{2,0}+\alpha^{1,1}+\alpha^{0,2}
, where
\alpha^{i,2-i}\in A^{i,2-i}(X,D,E)
. Then we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime\prime}\alpha^{i,2-i}=0,\quad\nabla^{\prime}\alpha^{i,2-i}=0.

This implies that
\alpha^{2,0}\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X}(\log D)\otimes E)
and
\alpha^{0,2}\in\mathcal{H}^{0,2}(X,E)
.

Assume that
\phi(\alpha)=0
. Then there exists some
\beta\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
such that
\alpha=\nabla\beta
. It follows that

\alpha^{2,0}=\nabla^{\prime}\beta^{1,0},\quad\alpha^{0,2}=\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{0,1},\quad\alpha^{1,1}=\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{1,0}+\nabla^{\prime}\beta^{0,1},

where
\beta^{1,0}\in A^{1,0}(X,D,E)
and
\beta^{0,1}\in A^{0,1}(X,E)
.
Since

\nabla^{\prime}\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{0,1}=\nabla^{\prime}\alpha^{0,2}=0,

by
2.16
, we have

\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{0,1}=0,\quad\nabla^{\prime}\beta^{0,1}=\nabla^{\prime}\nabla^{\prime\prime}h

for some
h\in A^{0}(X,E)
. Therefore, if we replace
\beta^{0,1}
by
\beta^{0,1}-\nabla^{\prime\prime}h
, and
\beta^{1,0}=\beta^{1,0}+\nabla^{\prime}h
, then we have

\alpha^{0,2}=\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{0,1},\quad\nabla^{\prime}\beta^{0,1}=0.

Thus, by replacing
\alpha
with
\alpha-\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{0,1}
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\alpha^{0,2}=0
and that
\beta
is of type
(1,0)
. It follows that

\alpha^{2,0}+\alpha^{1,1}=\nabla\beta^{1,0}.

Note that we have

\nabla^{\prime}\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{1,0}=\nabla^{\prime}\alpha^{1,1}=0.

This implies that

\nabla^{\prime}\nabla^{\prime\prime}\Res_{D}\beta^{1,0}=\Res_{D}(\nabla^{\prime}\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{1,0})=0.

Hence
\Res_{D}\beta^{1,0}\in H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
.
By
5.1
, we have

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\beta^{1,0}=\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{1,0}+\iota_{*}\Res_{D}\beta^{1,0}.

This implies that

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\beta^{1,0}=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{1,0}+\iota_{*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\Res_{D}\beta^{1,0}=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{1,0}=\nabla^{\prime}\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{1,0}=0.

By
2.16
, we have

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\beta^{1,0}=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}h

for some
h\in\mathscr{D}^{1}(X,E)
. By the type comparison, it implies that
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\beta^{1,0}=0.

Hence
\alpha^{2,0}=0,
and we have

\alpha^{1,1}=\nabla^{\prime\prime}\beta^{1,0}

for some
\beta^{1,0}\in\ker\nabla^{\prime}
. This proves that the class
\{\alpha\}
vanishes in
H^{2}(L^{\bullet},d)
, thereby establishing the injectivity of
H^{2}(\phi)
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Let us now introduce a new dgla. Let
D=\sum_{i=1}^{m}D_{i}
. Dentoe by
D^{(k)}:=\sqcup_{|I|=k}D_{I}
and let

D_{(k)}:=\sqcup_{|I|=k}\sum_{j\notin I}D_{I}\cap D_{j}

that is a simple normal crossing divisor on
D^{(k)}
.

We first introduce some terminology. For an arbitrary multi-index
I
, let
\sigma\in S_{|I|}
be the unique permutation such that
(i_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,i_{\sigma(j)})
is ordered. We denote by
\sigma(I):=(i_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,i_{\sigma(j)})
this ordered multi-index. The sign of this permutation by
\varepsilon(I):=\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)
.
We set
C^{p,q}:=H^{q}(D^{(p)},E)
and the
differential

d:C^{p,q}\to C^{p-1,q+2}
is defined to be the
signed
Gysin morphism as follows :
Let
I
be an ordered multi-index with
|I|=p
. For any
i\in I
, let
I_{i}
denote the ordered multi-index corresponding to the set
I\setminus\{i\}
. Then for any element
\alpha\in H^{q}(D_{I},E)
, let

g_{I,i}:H^{q}(D_{I},E)\to H^{q+2}(D_{I_{i}},E)

be the Gysin morphism defined in (
5.3
). Then
we define a differential

\displaystyle d\alpha:=\sum_{i\in I}(-1)^{\varepsilon(I,i)}g_{I,i}(\alpha),

(5.15.1)

where
\varepsilon(I,i)
denotes the number of elements in
I
that are strictly smaller than
i
.

For any element
\alpha\in C^{k}
, we denote by
|\alpha|=k
its degree.

Lemma 5.16
.

(C^{\bullet},d)
is a chain complex.

Démonstration.

It suffices to verify that
d^{2}=0
. For any ordered multi-index
I
and any distinct
i
and
j
in
I
, we denote by
K
the ordered multi-index
I\backslash\{i,j\}
. Then the summmand of

d^{2}(\alpha)
in
H^{q+4}(D_{K},E)
is given by

(-1)^{\varepsilon(I_{i},j)}g_{I_{i},j}\left((-1)^{\varepsilon(I,i)}g_{I,i}(\alpha)\right)+(-1)^{\varepsilon(I_{j},i)}g_{I_{j},i}\left((-1)^{\varepsilon(I,j)}g_{I,j}(\alpha)\right)

By
5.4
, we have

g_{I_{i},j}\circ g_{I,i}(\alpha)=g_{I_{j},i}\circ g_{I,j}(\alpha)=\{(\iota_{K,I})_{*}\eta\}

where
\eta\in A^{q}(D_{I},E)
such that
\nabla\eta=0
, and is a representative of
\alpha
, and
\iota_{I,K}:D_{I}\to D_{K}
is the inclusion map.
We also note that

(-1)^{\varepsilon(I_{i},j)}(-1)^{\varepsilon(I,i)}+(-1)^{\varepsilon(I_{j},i)}(-1)^{\varepsilon(I,j)}=0.

This implies that
d^{2}(\alpha)=0
. Hence
(C^{\bullet},d)
is a chain complex.
∎

Definition-Lemma 5.17
.

We define a bracket

C^{p,q}\times C^{k,\ell}\to C^{p+k,q+\ell}

as follows.
Pick any
\alpha\in A^{q}(D_{I},E)
and
\beta\in A^{\ell}(D_{J},E)
with
|I|=p
and
|J|=k
, that are both
\nabla
-closed. We abusively denote by
\alpha
and
\beta
their cohomolgy classes. We define

[\alpha,\beta]:=\begin{cases}0\quad\mbox{ if }I\cap J\neq\varnothing\\
(-1)^{\varepsilon(IJ)+q|J|}\{[\iota_{1}^{*}\alpha,\iota_{2}^{*}\beta]\}\quad\mbox{ if }I\cap J=\varnothing\end{cases}

(5.17.1)

Here,

—

IJ
denotes the multi-index obtained by appending
J
to
I

—

K
denotes the ordered multi-index corresponding to the set
I\cup J
.

—

\iota_{1}:D_{K}\to D_{I}
and
\iota_{2}:D_{K}\to D_{J}
denote the inclusion maps.

Then the chain complex
(C^{\bullet},d)
endowed with the above bracket is a dgla.

Démonstration.

It is straightforward to see that the above bracket is a (graded) Lie algebra. Let us prove the graded Leibniz rule.

Pick any
i\in I
. Let
L
be the ordered multi-index corresponding to the set
K\backslash\{i\}
.
Let
\eta\in A^{q+1}(D_{I_{i}},D_{I},E)
be such that
\Res_{D_{I}}\eta=\alpha
. Let
\iota_{3}:D_{L}\to D_{I_{i}}
and
\iota_{4}:D_{L}\to D_{J}
.
Then

\iota_{3}^{*}\eta\in A^{q+1}(D_{L},D_{K},E).

Hence

[\iota_{3}^{*}\eta,\iota_{4}^{*}\beta]\in A^{q+\ell+1}(D_{L},D_{K},E)

such that

\Res_{D_{K}}[\iota_{3}^{*}\eta,\iota_{4}^{*}\beta]=[\iota_{1}^{*}\alpha,\iota_{2}^{*}\beta].

It follows that
\nabla[\iota_{3}^{*}\eta,\iota_{4}^{*}\beta]
represents

g_{K,i}(\{[\iota_{1}^{*}\alpha,\iota_{2}^{*}\beta]\})\in H^{q+\ell+2}(D_{L},E),

and thus

g_{K,i}([\alpha,\beta])=(-1)^{\varepsilon(IJ)+qk}\{\nabla[\iota_{3}^{*}\eta,\iota_{4}^{*}\beta]\}.

On the other hand, note that

\nabla[\iota_{3}^{*}\eta,\iota_{4}^{*}\beta]=[\iota_{3}^{*}\nabla\eta,\iota_{4}^{*}\beta].

Since we have

g_{I,i}(\alpha)=\{\nabla\eta\}\in H^{q+2}(D_{I_{i}},E),

it follows that

\{\nabla[\iota_{3}^{*}\eta,\iota_{4}^{*}\beta]\}=(-1)^{\varepsilon(I_{i}J)+(q+2)k}[g_{I,i}(\alpha),\beta].

This implies that

\displaystyle(d[\alpha,\beta])_{L}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\varepsilon(K,i)}g_{K,i}([\alpha,\beta])=(-1)^{\varepsilon(K,i)+\varepsilon(IJ)+qk}\{\nabla[\iota_{3}^{*}\eta,\iota_{4}^{*}\beta]\}.

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\varepsilon(K,i)+\varepsilon(IJ)+qk+\varepsilon(I_{i}J)+(q+2)k}[g_{I,i}(\alpha),\{\beta\}]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\varepsilon(K,i)+\varepsilon(IJ)+qk+\varepsilon(I_{i}J)+(q+2)k+\varepsilon(I,i)}[(d\alpha)_{I_{i}},\{\beta\}]

\displaystyle=[(d\alpha)_{I_{i}},\{\beta\}].

(5.17.2)

On the other hand, pick any
i\in I
. Let
L
be the ordered multi-index corresponding to the set
K\backslash\{i\}
. Then

\displaystyle(d[\alpha,\beta])_{L}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|+1}(d[\beta,\alpha])_{L}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq :dg}}}{{=}}(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|+1}([(d\beta)_{J_{i}},\alpha])

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\alpha||\beta|+1}(-1)^{|\alpha|(|\beta|+1)+1}([\alpha,(d\beta)_{J_{i}}])

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\alpha|}([\alpha,(d\beta)_{J_{i}}]).

Hence, we have

d[\alpha,\beta]=[d\alpha,\beta]+(-1)^{|\alpha|}[\alpha,d\beta].

This shows the graded Leibniz rule. The lemma is proved.
∎

For any
ordered
multi-index
I
,
we set
\eta_{I}:=\Res_{D_{I}}\eta
.
If
I
not not ordered, then we set

\displaystyle\eta_{I}:=(-1)^{\varepsilon(I)}\eta_{\sigma(I)}.

(5.17.3)

By
5.1
and (
5.17.3
), we obtain the identity

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\eta_{I}=\nabla\eta_{I}+\sum_{i\notin I}(\iota_{I,i})_{*}\eta_{Ii},

(5.17.4)

where
\iota_{I,i}\colon D_{I}\cap D_{i}\hookrightarrow D_{I}
is the natural inclusion, and the subscript
Ii
denotes the multi-index formed by appending
i
to
I
.

Note that

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\eta_{I}=\nabla^{\prime}\eta_{I}=(-1)^{|I|}\Res_{D_{I}}(\nabla^{\prime}\eta)=0.

(5.17.5)

We are ready to construct the morphism of
\psi:L^{\bullet}\to C^{\bullet}
.
Consider any
\eta\in L^{k}
. Define

\displaystyle\psi(\eta)=\left((-1)^{|I|}\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{I})\}\right)_{I}\in C^{k}

(5.17.6)

where
I
ranges over all ordered multi-index contained in
\{1,\ldots,m\}
, and
\mathcal{H}(I)\in A^{k-|I|}(D_{I},E)
is the harmonic projection of
\eta_{I}
.

Proposition 5.18
.

The map
\psi:L^{\bullet}\to C^{\bullet}
is a morphism of dgla, and is moreover a
1
-quasi-isomorphism.

Démonstration.

Step 1.
We first show that
\psi
is a morphism of chain complexes.

Pick any
\eta\in L^{k}
. Note that

\displaystyle(\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta)_{I}=(-1)^{|I|}(\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta_{I})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq :resicur3}}}{{=}}(-1)^{|I|}(\bm{\nabla}\eta_{I}+\sum_{i\notin I}(\iota_{I,i})_{*}\eta_{Ii}).

(5.18.1)

It follows that

\mathcal{H}((\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta)_{I})=(-1)^{|I|}\mathcal{H}(\sum_{i\notin I}(\iota_{I,i})_{*}\eta_{Ii}).

Since

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\eta_{I}=(\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\eta)_{I}=0,

it follows that

0=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\eta_{I}=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}G\eta_{I}=\Delta^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}G\eta_{I}-\bm{\nabla}^{\prime*}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}G\eta_{I}=\Delta^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}G\eta_{I}

Therefore,
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}G\eta_{I}=0
, and we have

\displaystyle\eta_{I}=\mathcal{H}(\eta_{I})+\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime*}G\eta_{I}.

(5.18.2)

Hence for any
i\notin I
, we have

(\iota_{I,i})_{*}\eta_{Ii}=(\iota_{I,i})_{*}\mathcal{H}(\eta_{Ii})+\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}(\iota_{I,i})_{*}\left(\bm{\nabla}^{\prime*}G\eta_{Ii}\right),

which yields

\mathcal{H}\left((\iota_{I,i})_{*}\eta_{Ii}\right)=\mathcal{H}\left((\iota_{I,i})_{*}\mathcal{H}(\eta_{Ii})\right).

Therefore, we have

\mathcal{H}((\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta)_{I})=(-1)^{|I|}\sum_{i\notin I}\mathcal{H}((\iota_{I,i})_{*}\mathcal{H}(\eta_{Ii})).

By
5.4
, we have

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{H}\left((\iota_{I,i})_{*}\mathcal{H}(\eta_{Ii})\right)\}=-g_{K(i)}(\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{Ii})\})

(5.18.3)

where we denote by
K(i)
the ordered multi-index corresponding to the set
I\cup\{i\}
, and

g_{K(i)}:H^{q}(D_{K(i)},E)\to H^{q+2}(D_{I},E)

be the Gysin morphism defined in (
5.3
).
This implies that

\{\mathcal{H}((\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta)_{I})\}=(-1)^{|I|+1}\sum_{i\notin I}g_{K(i)}(\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{Ii})\})

Hence

\psi(\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta)_{I}=-\sum_{i\notin I}g_{K(i)}(\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{Ii})\})

On the other hand, by (
5.15.1
), we note that

\displaystyle\left(d\psi(\eta)\right)_{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\notin I}(-1)^{\varepsilon(K(i),i)}g_{K(i)}\left((-1)^{|I|+1}\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{K(i)})\}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq :permu}}}{{=}}\sum_{i\notin I}(-1)^{\varepsilon(K(i),i)+\varepsilon(Ii)+|I|+1}g_{K(i)}\left(\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{Ii})\}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i\notin I}g_{K(i)}(\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{Ii})\})

This implies that

d\psi(\eta)=\psi(\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta).

Hence
\psi
is a morphism of complexes.

Step 2.
We show
\psi
preserves the Lie brackets.

For any
\eta\in L^{k}
and
\sigma\in L^{\ell}
, we have

\Res_{D_{K}}[\eta,\sigma]=\sum_{I,J}(-1)^{\varepsilon(IJ)+|J|(k-|I|)}[\iota_{1}^{*}\eta_{I},\iota_{2}^{*}\sigma_{J}]

where the sum varies among all ordered multi-indicies
I
and
J
such that
I\cap J=\varnothing
, and
K=I\cup J
. Here we denote by
\iota_{1}:D_{K}\to D_{I}
and
\iota_{2}:D_{K}\to D_{J}
the inclusion maps.

By (
5.18.2
), we have

\mathcal{H}(\Res_{D_{K}}[\eta,\sigma])=\sum_{I,J}(-1)^{\varepsilon(IJ)+|J|(k-|I|)}\mathcal{H}\left([\iota_{1}^{*}\mathcal{H}(\eta_{I}),\iota_{2}^{*}\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{J})]\right).

On the other hand, by (
), we have

[\{H(\eta_{I})\},\{\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{J})\}]=\sum_{I,J}(-1)^{\varepsilon(IJ)+|J|(k-|I|)}\{\left([\iota_{1}^{*}\mathcal{H}(\eta_{I}),\iota_{2}^{*}\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{J})]\right)\}.

which yields

[\psi(\eta),\psi(\sigma)]_{K}=\sum_{I,J}(-1)^{\varepsilon(IJ)+(k-|I|)|J|}\{\left([(-1)^{|I|}\iota_{1}^{*}\mathcal{H}(\eta_{I}),(-1)^{|J|}\iota_{2}^{*}\mathcal{H}(\sigma_{J})]\right)\},

where the sum is also taken as above. It follows that

[\psi(\eta),\psi(\sigma)]_{K}=(-1)^{|I|+|J|}\{\mathcal{H}(\Res_{D_{K}}[\eta,\sigma])\}=(-1)^{|K|}\{\mathcal{H}(\Res_{D_{K}}[\eta,\sigma])\}=\psi([\eta,\sigma])_{K}.

Hence
\psi
preserves the Lie bracket. In conclusion,
\psi
is a morphism of dglas.

Step 3.
We show that
H^{i}(\psi)
is an isomorphim for
i=0,1
.

Note that
d:C^{0}\to C^{1}
is the zero map. Therefore,
H^{0}(\psi):H^{0}(L^{\bullet})\to H^{0}(C^{\bullet})
is an identity map.
Consider
H^{1}(\psi):H^{1}(L^{\bullet})\to H^{1}(C^{\bullet})
. By the proof of
5.15
, any element
\alpha\in H^{1}(L^{\bullet})
has a unique representative

(\eta^{1,0},\eta^{0,1})\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}(\log D)\otimes E)\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(X,E).

Write
\eta:=\eta^{1,0}+\eta^{0,1}
, and
\eta_{(1)}:=\Res_{D}\eta\in H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
. Then

\bm{\nabla}\eta^{1,0}=\iota_{*}\eta_{(1)},

and
f(\eta_{(1)})\in A^{1,0}(X,D,E)
where
f
defined in (
5.4.3
) satisfies

\bm{\nabla}f(\eta_{(1)})=\iota_{*}\eta_{(1)},

and
f(\eta_{(1)})
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact. It follows that

\Delta^{\prime\prime}(\eta^{1,0}-f(\eta_{(1)})=0

which yields

\eta^{1,0}-f(\eta_{(1)})\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}\otimes E).

In particular,

\eta^{1,0}-f(\eta_{(1)})=\mathcal{H}(\eta^{1,0}-f(\eta_{(1)}))=\mathcal{H}(\eta^{1,0}).

Hence we have

\psi(\eta)=(\{\eta^{1,0}-f(\eta_{(1)})+\eta^{0,1}\},-\{\eta_{(1)}\}).

Thus if
\{\psi(\eta)\}=0
in
H^{1}(C^{\bullet})
, then

\eta_{(1)}=0,\quad\eta^{1,0}-f(\eta_{(1)})=0,\quad\eta^{0,1}=0.

This implies that

\eta=0.
We obtain the injectivity of
H^{1}(\psi)
.

Let
(a^{0,1},a^{1,0})\in C^{0,1}\oplus C^{1,0}
such that
d(a^{0,1},a^{1,0})=0
. It follows that

g(a^{1,0})=0

where
g:H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)\to H^{2}(X,E)
is the Gysin morphism.
By the construction of
f
in (
5.4.3
), this implies that

\bm{\nabla}f(a^{1,0})=\iota_{*}a^{1,0}.

In particular,

f(a^{1,0})\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}\otimes E).

Let
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,E)
be a representative of
a^{0,1}\in H^{1}(X,E)
. Then
\eta+f(a^{1,0})\in L^{1}
, such that

\psi(\eta-f(a^{1,0}))=(\{\eta-f(a^{1,0})\},-\{\mathcal{H}(\Res_{\eta-f(a^{1,0})})\}=(a^{0,1},a^{1,0}).

Here we use the fact that
f(a^{1,0})
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.
This shows that
H^{1}(\psi)
is surjective.

Step 4.
We now prove that
H^{2}(\psi)
is injective. Pick any
\eta\in L^{2}
such that
\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta=0
. We decompose
\eta=\eta^{2,0}+\eta^{1,1}+\eta^{0,2}
, where
\eta^{i,2-i}\in A^{i,2-i}(X,D,E)
. Then we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta^{i,2-i}=0,\quad\nabla^{\prime}\eta^{i,2-i}=0.

This implies that
\eta^{2,0}\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{2}_{X}(\log D)\otimes E)
and
\eta^{0,2}\in\mathcal{H}^{0,2}(X,E)
.

If
\psi(\eta)=d(a^{0,1},a^{1,0})
for some
(a^{0,1},a^{0,1})\in C^{0,1}\oplus C^{1,0}
, then we have

\psi(\eta)\in C^{0,2},

which yields that

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{H}(\eta)\}=g(a^{1,0}),\quad\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{(1)})\}=0,\quad\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{I})\}=0

(5.18.4)

for each multi-index
I
with
|I|=2
by (
5.15.1
).
By the definition of
f
in (
5.4.3
), we have

\bm{\nabla}f(a^{1,0})=\mathcal{H}(g(a^{1,0}))+\iota_{*}(a^{1,0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq :three}}}{{=}}\mathcal{H}(\eta)+\iota_{*}(a^{1,0}).

Note that
\nabla^{\prime\prime}f(a^{1,0})=\mathcal{H}(\eta)\in A^{1,1}(X,E)
, that is
\nabla
-closed. It follows (
5.18.4
) that

\psi(\eta-\nabla^{\prime\prime}f(a^{1,0}))=0.

Therefore, we can replace
\eta
by
\eta-\nabla^{\prime\prime}f(a^{1,0})
and assume that

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{H}(\eta)\}=0,\quad\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{(1)})\}=0,\quad\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{I})\}=0.

(5.18.5)

for each multi-index
I
with
|I|=2
. Fix any such
I
.
Since
\nabla^{\prime}\eta=0
, it follows that

\nabla^{\prime}\eta_{I}=0.

(
5.18.5
) then implies that
\eta_{I}=\eta^{2,0}_{I}=0
. Note that

\eta_{(1)}=\eta^{2,0}_{(1)}+\eta^{1,1}_{(1)}

with

\eta^{2,0}_{(1)}\in H^{0}(D^{(1)},\Omega_{D^{(1)}}(\log D_{(1)})\otimes E)

and

\eta^{1,1}_{(1)}\in A^{0,1}(D^{(1)},E).

By (
5.18.1
), we have

\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta_{(1)}=-(\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta)_{(1)}=0,\quad\nabla^{\prime}\eta_{(1)}=-(\nabla^{\prime}\eta)_{(1)}=0.

This implies that

\eta^{1,1}_{(1)}\in\mathcal{H}^{0,1}(D^{(1)},E).

Since

\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{(1)})\}=0,\quad\{\mathcal{H}(\eta_{ij})\}=\{\mathcal{H}(\Res_{D_{i\cap D_{j}}}\eta_{i})\}=0\quad\mbox{ for any }i<j,

the arguments in Step 3 in proving the injectivity of
H^{1}(\psi)
show that
\eta_{(1)}=0
. Hence, we have

\eta^{2,0}\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{2}\otimes E),\quad{\rm Res}_{D}\eta^{1,1}=0.

Hence

\bm{\nabla}\eta^{1,1}=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\eta^{1,1}=\nabla^{\prime\prime}\eta^{1,1}=0.

Note that

\mathcal{H}(\eta^{1,1})\in\mathcal{H}^{1,1}(X,E).

By (
5.18.5
) together with the Hodge composition of harmonic forms

\mathcal{H}^{2}(X,E)=\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(X,E)\oplus\mathcal{H}^{1,1}(X,E)\oplus\mathcal{H}^{0,2}(X,E),

we deduce that

\eta^{2,0}=0,\quad\eta^{0,2}=0,\quad\{\eta^{1,1}\}=0\in H^{2}(X,E).

By
2.16
, we have

\eta^{1,1}=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\sigma

for some
\sigma\in\mathscr{D}^{0}(X,E)
. By
proposition

5.10
, there exists
\omega\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
such that

\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime}\omega=\eta^{1,1}.

Hence by
5.1
,
\nabla^{\prime\prime}\omega=\eta^{1,1}.
This implies that

\{\eta\}=0\in H^{2}(L^{\bullet})
. The injectivity of
H^{2}(\psi)
is proved.

The proof of the proposition is accomplished.
∎

In summary, we obtain the following result :

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
T^{\bullet}:=(A^{\bullet}(X,D,E),\nabla)
be the dgla of logarithmic forms. Let
L^{\bullet}:=(\ker\nabla^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime\prime})
be the dgla defined previously, and let
(C^{\bullet},d)
be the dgla introduced in
5.17
. Let
\varepsilon:T^{0}\to E_{x}
,
\varepsilon:L^{0}\to E_{x}
, and
\varepsilon:C^{0}\to E_{x}
denote the evaluation maps at
x
, which define the corresponding augmentations.

Then, the inclusion map
\phi:L^{\bullet}\to T^{\bullet}
and the map
\psi:(L^{\bullet},\nabla^{\prime\prime})\to(C^{\bullet},d)
defined in (
5.17.6
) are morphisms of dglas, and both are
1
-quasi-isomorphisms. In particular, the diagram

T^{\bullet}\xleftarrow{\phi}L^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\psi}C^{\bullet}

establishes a zigzag of
1
-quasi-equivalences connecting
(T^{\bullet},d)
and
(C^{\bullet},d)
. ∎

5.7
Constructing the universal connection (I)

In this subsection, we construct the
universal
connection on the representation variety, in the sense specified in
5.23
. This subsection is dedicated to the analytic construction ; we defer the proof of its “universal property” to
5.23
, using the Goldman–Millson theory in
section

3
and the zigzags of
1
-quasi-equivalences established in
section

5.6
.

Fix a basis
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{p}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,E)
, and a basis
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{q}\in H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
. Consider the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{p},w_{1},\ldots,w_{q}]
.
Let
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{C}^{p+q}
be the quasi-homogeneous cone defined by

\displaystyle d(\sum_{\gamma=1}^{q}w_{\gamma}\beta_{\gamma})+\frac{1}{2}[\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\alpha_{i},\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\alpha_{i}]=0\in C^{2}

(5.19.1)

where
d\colon C^{1}\to C^{2}
is the differential of the dgla
(C^{\bullet},d)
defined in (
5.15.1
), and
[-,-]
is the Lie bracket defined in (
).
Let
f(\beta_{\gamma})\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
be defined in (
5.4.3
). Then
\Res_{D}f(\beta_{\gamma})=-\beta_{\gamma}
and we have
\nabla f(\beta_{\gamma})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(X,E)
by our construction. Let us denote by
\eta_{i,0}:=\alpha_{i}
and
\eta_{0,\gamma}:=f(\beta_{\gamma})
.

For any
(z,w):=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{p};w_{1},\ldots,w_{q})\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
, we define

\displaystyle\eta_{1}(z,w)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i,0}

(5.19.2)

\displaystyle\eta_{2}(z,w)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{q}w_{\gamma}\eta_{0,\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\prime\prime*}G[\eta_{1}(z,w),\eta_{1}(z,w)]

(5.19.3)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma=1}^{q}w_{\gamma}\eta_{0,\gamma}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq p}z_{i}z_{j}\nabla^{\prime\prime*}G[\eta_{i,0},\eta_{j,0}]

We proceed by induction on
k
. Let
k\geq 3
and assume that
\eta_{j}(z,w)\in A^{1}(X,D,E)\cap\mathrm{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
has been defined for all
1\leq j\leq k-1
. Then the
2
-form
\gamma_{k}(z,w)
given by

\displaystyle\gamma_{k}(z,w)

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\eta_{j}(z,w)\wedge\eta_{k-j}(z,w),

(5.19.4)

lies in
A^{2}(X,D,E)
. Next, we define
\eta_{k}(z,w)
using the Green operator :

\eta_{k}(z,w):=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G\gamma_{k}(z,w).

Note that (
5.19.1
) is exactly the three equations in
5.14
. By
5.14
, when
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
, this
\eta_{k}(z,w)
satisfies the regularity condition

\eta_{k}(z,w)\in A^{1}(X,D,E)\cap\mathrm{Im}\bm{\nabla}^{\prime},

and solves the equation

\bm{\nabla}\eta_{k}(z,w)=\gamma_{k}(z,w).

Moreover, there exists some sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
such that, for
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}^{p+q}
, where
\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}^{p+q}
is the ball in
\mathbb{C}^{p+q}
of radius
\varepsilon
,
the connection

\nabla_{z,w}:=\nabla+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{i}(z,w).

is flat over
X_{0}
and has logarithmic poles.

Let us write explicitly the formula of
\eta_{i}(z,w)
.
Inductively, for any multi-indices
I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
and
\Gamma=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{\ell})
with entries satisfying
1\leq i_{j}\leq p
and
1\leq\gamma_{m}\leq q
, we define 1-forms on
X_{0}
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{I,\Gamma}

\displaystyle:=-(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=(I_{1},I_{2});\Gamma=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\\
1\leq|I_{1}|+2|\Gamma_{1}|<|I_{2}|+2|\Gamma_{2}|\end{subarray}}[\eta_{I_{1},\Gamma_{1}},\eta_{I_{2},\Gamma_{2}}]+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=(I_{1},I_{2});\Gamma=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\\
|I_{1}|+2|\Gamma_{1}|=|I_{2}|+2|\Gamma_{2}|\end{subarray}}[\eta_{I_{1},\Gamma_{1}},\eta_{I_{2},\Gamma_{2}}])

(5.19.5)

\displaystyle\eta_{I,\Gamma}

\displaystyle:=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G\Phi_{I,\Gamma}

(5.19.6)

Note that the
\eta_{I,\Gamma}
might not extend to log 1-forms on
A^{1}(X,D,E)
!

Then for any
k\geq 1
, we have

\eta_{k}(z,w)=\sum_{|I|+2|\Gamma|=k}z_{I}w_{\Gamma}\eta_{I,\Gamma},

where we denote by
z_{I}:=z_{i_{1}}\cdots z_{i_{|I|}}
, and
w_{\Gamma}:=w_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots w_{\gamma_{|\Gamma|}}
.

We list one fact that will be used later.

Lemma 5.20
.

For any
k\geq 2
and any
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
,
\eta_{k}(z,w)
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact.

Démonstration.

It follows from
5.14
.
∎

We extend
5.14
to the universal setting as follows.

Theorem 5.21
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D
be a simple normal crossing divisor on
X
. Let
(V,\nabla)
be a unitary bundle on
X
. Denote by
(E,\nabla):=(\End(V),\nabla)
.

Fix a basis
\eta_{1,0},\ldots,\eta_{p,0}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,E)
, and a basis
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{q}\in H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
. Set
\eta_{0,\gamma}:=f(\beta_{\gamma})
where
f:H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)\to A^{1}(X,D,E)
is defined in (
5.4.3
). For any multi-indices
I
with entries in
\{1,\ldots,p\}
and
\Gamma
with entries in
\{1,\ldots,q\}
, we define inductively
E
-valued smooth 1-forms on
X_{0}
:

\displaystyle\eta_{I,\Gamma}:=-\bm{\nabla}^{\prime\prime*}G(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=(I_{1},I_{2});\Gamma=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\\
1\leq|I_{1}|+2|\Gamma_{1}|<|I_{2}|+2|\Gamma_{2}|\end{subarray}}[\eta_{I_{1},\Gamma_{1}},\eta_{I_{2},\Gamma_{2}}]+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I=(I_{1},I_{2});\Gamma=(\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2})\\
|I_{1}|+2|\Gamma_{1}|=|I_{2}|+2|\Gamma_{2}|\end{subarray}}[\eta_{I_{1},\Gamma_{1}},\eta_{I_{2},\Gamma_{2}}])

(5.21.1)

For any
k\geq 1
, we define

\displaystyle\eta_{k}(z,w)=\sum_{|I|+2|\Gamma|=k}z_{I}w_{\Gamma}\eta_{I,\Gamma},

(5.21.2)

where we write
z_{I}:=z_{i_{1}}\cdots z_{i_{|I|}}
, and
w_{\Gamma}:=w_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots w_{\gamma_{|\Gamma|}}
,
and

\displaystyle\nabla_{z,w}:=\nabla+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{k}(z,w).

(5.21.3)

Then for every
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}^{p+q}
,
\eta_{k}(z,w)
extend to logarithmic 1-forms in
\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{k}(z,w)
converges and lies in
A^{1}(X,D,E)
.

Let
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{C}^{p+q}
be the quasi-homogeneous cone defined by the following three equations with values in
H^{2}(X,E)
,
H^{1}(D^{(1)},E)
and
H^{0}(D^{(2)},E)
respectively :

(i)

g(\sum_{i=1}^{q}w_{i}\beta_{i})+\frac{1}{2}\{[\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i,0},\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i,0}]\}=0,

where
g:H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)\to H^{2}(X,E)
is the Gysin morphism defined in
5.3
.

(ii)

\displaystyle\{[\iota^{*}\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i,0},\sum_{j=1}^{q}w_{j}\beta_{j}]\}=0\in H^{1}(D^{(1)},E),

where
\iota:D^{(1)}\to X
denotes the natural morphism.

(iii)

For any
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle[\iota_{ij}^{*}(\sum_{\ell=1}^{q}w_{k}\beta_{k}|_{D_{i}}),\iota_{ji}^{*}(\sum_{k=1}^{q}w_{k}\beta_{k}|_{D_{i}})]=0\in H^{0}(D_{i}\cap D_{j},E),

where
\iota_{ij}:D_{i}\cap D_{j}\to D_{i}
is the natural morphism.

Then there exists some
\varepsilon>0
such that,
\nabla_{z,w}
is an analytic family of flat connections for
V|_{X_{0}}
with logarithmic poles around
D
parametrized by
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{B}_{\varepsilon}^{p+q}
.

Démonstration.

Suppose that
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
. Then
\eta_{1}(z,w)
and

\Res_{D}\eta_{2}(z,w)=-\sum_{j=1}^{q}w_{j}\beta_{j}

satisfy the three conditions in
5.21
. Moreover,
\eta_{k}(z,w)
defined in (
5.21.2
) satisfies the inductive equations in the proof of
5.14
for each
k\geq 1
. Therefore, we have

\bm{\nabla}\eta_{k}(z,w)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\eta_{i}(z,w)\wedge\eta_{k-i}(z,w)=0

for
k\geq 3
. By (
), we have

\|\eta_{k}(z,w)\|_{W^{1}}\leq\frac{1}{2C}\Lambda(z,w)^{k},

(5.21.4)

where

\Lambda(z,w):=2C\|\eta_{1}(z,w)\|_{W^{1}}+\sqrt{4C^{2}\|\eta_{1}(z,w)\|_{W^{1}}^{2}+4C\|\eta_{2}(z,w)\|_{W^{1}}}.

By the definitions of
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
, there exists some
\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
such that

\Lambda(z,w)\leq 1
for any
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
. Consequently,
\|\eta(z,w)\|_{W^{1}}<\infty
for all
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
. The bootstrapping arguments used in the proof of
5.14
imply that
\eta(z,w)\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
and satisfies

\nabla\eta(z,w)+\eta(z,w)\wedge\eta(z,w)=0.

Thus, we conclude that
\nabla_{z,w}:=\nabla+\eta(z,w)
defines an analytic family of flat logarithmic connections on
V
, parametrized by
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
. This completes the proof.
∎

5.8
Constructing the universal connection (II)

In this subsection, we prove that the connection constructed in
5.21
is universal. The argument closely follows that of
4.4
, and we include all details for the sake of completeness.

Consider the dgla
T^{\bullet}:=(A^{\bullet}(X,D,E),\nabla)
. Define
L^{\bullet}:=(\ker\nabla^{\prime},\nabla^{\prime\prime})
. Let
\varepsilon:T^{0}\to E_{x}
,
\varepsilon:L^{0}\to E_{x}
, and
\varepsilon:C^{0}\to E_{x}
be the evaluation maps at a fixed base point
x\in X_{0}
, which define the corresponding augmentations.
Then by
5.19
, we have the 1-quasi-equivalences

(T^{\bullet},d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\leftarrow}}(L^{\bullet},d)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\psi}}{{\rightarrow}}(C^{\bullet},d).

By
3.4
, for any Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebra, the natural maps

\displaystyle[\mathscr{C}(T^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)/\exp(T^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]\leftarrow[\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)/\exp(L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]\to[\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)/\exp(C^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]

(5.21.5)

are equivalences of groupoids.

We note that augmentation

\varepsilon:C^{0}\to E_{x}
