defined by the evaluation is injective. In this case, we have the following result. Let
\mathscr{Q}
be the quasi-homogeneous cone in
C^{1}=C^{1,0}\oplus C^{0,1}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}_{0}:=\left\{u\in C^{1}\mid du+\frac{1}{2}[u,u]=0\right\}.

(5.21.6)

Denote by

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}:=\mathscr{Q}_{0}\times\mathfrak{g}/\varepsilon(C^{0}).

(5.21.7)

Lemma 5.22
.

The analytic germ
(\mathscr{Q},0)
(non-canonically) prorepresents the functor

\displaystyle{\rm Art}

\displaystyle\to{\rm Grp}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto\Def(C^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)

where
\Def(C^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)
is the small groupoid defined in (
3.3.1
).

Démonstration.

Let
\ell:=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathfrak{g}/\varepsilon(C^{0}))
. We choose a subset
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}\}
of
\mathfrak{g}
such that
the images of
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}\}
form a basis for the quotient space
\mathfrak{g}/\varepsilon(C^{0})
. Let
W
be the subspace of
\mathfrak{g}
spanned by
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}\}
. Then the natural map
p:W\to\mathfrak{g}/\varepsilon(C^{0})
is an isomorphism. Since
\varepsilon:C^{0}\to E_{x}
and
\varepsilon:D^{0}\to E_{x}

defined by the evaluation is injective, it follows that
\exp\left(C^{0}\otimes\mathrm{m}\right)
acts freely on
G_{A}^{0}:=\exp(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{m})
by left-multiplication. Hence the following (non-canonical) maps

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}/\varepsilon(C^{0})\otimes\mathfrak{m}\;\leftarrow\;

\displaystyle W\otimes\mathfrak{m}\;\rightarrow\;\frac{\exp(\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathfrak{m})}{\exp(\varepsilon(C^{0})\otimes\mathfrak{m})}

\displaystyle p(r)\;\mapsfrom\;

\displaystyle r\;\mapsto\;\exp(\varepsilon(C^{0})\otimes\mathfrak{m})\cdot\exp(r).

are both bijective. Here
\exp(\varepsilon(C^{0})\otimes\mathfrak{m})\cdot\exp(r)
is the right coset of
\exp(r)
.
Hence the following maps

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A)\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle\to\frac{\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)}{\exp(C^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})}

(5.22.1)

\displaystyle(\alpha,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto[(\alpha,\exp(r))].

and

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},A)\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle\to\frac{\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A)}{\exp(L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})}

(5.22.2)

\displaystyle(\alpha,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto[(\alpha,\exp(r))].

are both equivalences of groupoids.

Since
C^{1}
is a finite-dimensional complex vector space, we view
C^{1}\times W
as an affine space over
\mathbb{C}
. Let
\mathcal{I}
be the ideal of
\mathbb{C}[C^{1}\times W]
generated by the quasi-homogeneous equations

\displaystyle dq(u)+\frac{1}{2}[q(u),q(u)]=0

for
u\in C^{1}\times W
,
where
q:C^{1}\times W\to C^{1}
denotes the projection map,
d\colon C^{1}\to C^{2}
is the differential of the dgla
(C^{\bullet},d)
defined in (
5.15.1
), and
[-,-]
is the Lie bracket defined in (
). It follows that

\textrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[C^{1}\times W]/\mathcal{I}=\mathscr{Q}.

Let
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{p}\}
be a basis of
\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,E)
, and we denote abusively
\{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{p}\}
their cohomology classes in
H^{1}(X,E)=C^{0,1}
. Let
\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{q}\}\in C^{1,0}
be a basis. Then, any elements
\alpha\in C^{1}\otimes\mathfrak{m}
and
r\in W\otimes\mathfrak{m}
, can be written as

\displaystyle\alpha=(\sum_{i=1}^{p}\alpha_{i}\otimes f_{i},\sum_{\gamma=1}^{q}\beta_{\gamma}\otimes g_{\gamma}),\quad r=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}v_{j}\otimes h_{j},

(5.22.3)

where
f_{i},g_{\gamma},h_{j}\in\mathfrak{m}
. Hence,
(\alpha,r)
gives rise to a morphism

\displaystyle\Hom(\mathbb{C}[C^{1}\times W],A)

\displaystyle((\alpha_{i})^{*},(\beta_{\gamma})^{*},(v_{j})^{*})\mapsto f_{i}+g_{\gamma}+h_{j}

Here we consider
(\alpha_{i})^{*}\in(C^{0,1})^{*}
,
(\beta_{\gamma})^{*}\in(C^{1,0})^{*}
and
(v_{j})^{*}\in W^{*}
.
Note that the above morphism factors through

\Hom(\mathbb{C}[C^{1}\times W]/\mathcal{I},A)

if and only if

d(\alpha)+\frac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]=0.

Equivalently, this holds if and only if

\alpha\in\mathscr{C}^{1}(C^{\bullet},A).

Let
\mathcal{I}_{1}
be the ideal of
\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]
generated by the image of

(C^{1}\times W)^{*}\subset\mathbb{C}[C^{1}\times W]
under the natural projection

\mathbb{C}[C^{1}\times W]\to\mathbb{C}[C^{1}\times W]/\mathcal{I}
.
Let
\widehat{\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}
denote the
\mathcal{I}_{1}
-adic completion of
\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]
.
Since
A
is an Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebra, it follows that

\displaystyle\Hom(\mathbb{C}[C^{1}\times W]/\mathcal{I},A)\simeq\Hom(\widehat{\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}},A).

By standard properties of completion of quotient rings, there is a natural isomorphism

\widehat{\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\;\simeq\;\frac{\mathbb{C}[[C^{1}\times W]]}{\mathcal{I}},

where, by abuse of notation, we continue to denote by
\mathcal{I}
the ideal

\mathcal{I}\cdot\mathbb{C}[[C^{1}\times W]]
.

On the other hand,
\mathcal{I}_{1}
-adic completion commutes with localization at the
maximal ideal
\mathcal{I}_{1}
of
\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]
.
Therefore we have a natural isomorphism

\widehat{\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}}.

Therefore, the following map

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A)\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}

\displaystyle\to\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}},A)

(5.22.4)

\displaystyle(\alpha,r)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(((\alpha_{i})^{*},(\beta_{\gamma})^{*},(v_{j})^{*})\mapsto f_{i}+g_{\gamma}+h_{j}\right)

is an equivalence, where
(\alpha,r)
is written as in (
5.22.3
), and
\alpha_{i}^{*}
,
\beta_{\gamma}^{*}
, and
v_{j}^{*}
are understood as the images of
\alpha_{i}^{*}
,
\beta_{\gamma}^{*}
, and
v_{j}^{*}
under the composite morphisms

\mathbb{C}[C^{1}\times W]\to\mathbb{C}[\mathscr{Q}]\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}\to\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0}.

We remark that
\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}}
is generated by

\alpha_{i}^{*}
,
\beta_{\gamma}^{*}
, and
v_{j}^{*}
.
Therefore, for any morphism in

\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}},A)
, the images of these generators
completely determine the morphism.
Combining (
5.22.4
) with (
5.22.1
) yields the lemma.
∎

Consider the polynomial ring
\mathbb{C}[z_{1},\ldots,z_{p},w_{1},\ldots,w_{q}]
.
Let
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{C}^{p+q}
denote the quasi-homogeneous cone defined in (
5.19.1
). We set

\displaystyle\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime}:=\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{C}^{\ell}.

Write
\eta_{i,0}:=\alpha_{i}
and let
\eta_{0,\gamma}:=f(\beta_{\gamma})\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
be defined in (
5.4.3
). Then
\Res_{D}\eta_{0,\gamma}=-\beta_{\gamma}
and we have

\nabla f(\beta_{\gamma})\in\mathcal{H}^{2}(X,E)

by our construction.
Let

\displaystyle\nabla_{z,w}:=\nabla+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{k}(z,w)

(5.22.5)

be the connection parametrized by
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
,
where

\eta_{k}(z,w):=\sum_{|I|+2|\Gamma|=k}z_{I}w_{\Gamma}\eta_{I,\Gamma},

with the notation
z_{I}:=z_{i_{1}}\cdots z_{i_{|I|}}
, and
w_{\Gamma}:=w_{\gamma_{1}}\cdots w_{\gamma_{|\Gamma|}}
, where the forms
\eta_{I,\Gamma}\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
are defined in (
5.21.1
).

By
5.20
, we know for each
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
, each

\eta_{k}(z,w)\in A^{1}(X,D,E)
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact for
k\geq 2
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{\prime}\eta_{k}(z,w)=\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}\eta_{k}(z,w)=0\quad\mbox{ for }k\geq 1.

(5.22.6)

Fix a
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Set
A_{k}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
, that is an Artin local
\mathbb{C}
-algebra. Let
\mathfrak{m}
be the maximal ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}
. Write
\mathfrak{m}_{k}:=\mathfrak{m}A_{k}
, that is the maximal ideal for
A_{k}
. We now regard
z_{I}
,
w_{\Gamma}
, and
x_{j}
as the image of
z_{I}
,
w_{\Gamma}
and
x_{j}
under the morphism

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}^{p+q+\ell},0}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}=A_{k}.

Then we have
z_{I}\in\mathfrak{m}_{k}
and
w_{\Gamma}\in\mathfrak{m}_{k}
for
|I|\geq 1
and
|\Gamma|\geq 1
, and
x_{i}\in\mathfrak{m}_{k}
. Thus by (
5.22.6
),
we can consider

\eta_{i}(z,w)\in L^{1,0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}

for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
. It follows that

\eta_{j}(z,w)=0\in L^{1,0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}

for any
j\geq 2k+1
.
By
5.21
, we have

\nabla\big(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}(z,w)\big)+\frac{1}{2}[\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}(z,w),\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}(z,w)]=0\in L^{1}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}.

This implies that,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}(z,w)\in\obj\Def(L^{\bullet},A_{k}).

(5.22.7)

Consider the morphism
\psi:L^{\bullet}\to C^{\bullet}
in (
5.17.6
). By
5.20
, each

\eta_{j}(z,w)
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact for any
j\geq 2
. By (
5.14.3
) and (
5.17.6
), for any
k\geq 2
, we have

\psi(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}(z,w))=\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i,0}+\sum_{\gamma=1}^{q}w_{\gamma}\beta_{\gamma}\in\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A_{k}).

Here we denote abusively by
\{\eta_{1,0},\ldots,\eta_{p,0}\}
and
\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{q}\}
their cohomology class in
H^{1}(X,E)=C^{0,1}
and
H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)=C^{1,0}
respectively.
Therefore, using the isomorphisms of groupoids defined in (
5.22.1
) and (
5.22.4
), we can see that we have the isomorphism

\displaystyle\Phi:\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},A)\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}\to\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A)\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}{\to}\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},A_{k})

(5.22.8)

such that

\Phi(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}(z,w),\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}x_{j}v_{j})=\left((\eta_{i,0}^{*},\beta_{\gamma}^{*},v_{j}^{*})\mapsto z_{i}+w_{\gamma}+x_{j}\right).

As remarked above, since
\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}}
is generated by

\eta_{i,0}^{*}
,
\beta_{\gamma}^{*}
, and
v_{j}^{*}
, any morphism in

\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q},0}},A_{k})
is determined by images of these generators.

We state and prove the universal property of the connection constructed in
5.21
.

Theorem 5.23
.

Let
X
be a compact Kähler manifold and let
D
be a simple normal crossing divisor on
X
. Assume that
(V,\nabla)
be a unitary flat bundle of rank
N
on
X
and we denote by
(E,\nabla):=(\End(V),\nabla)
. Let
\nabla_{z,w}
denote the analytic family of flat connections on
V|_{X_{0}}
,
parametrized by
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{r}
for some
r>0
, as defined in (
5.22.5
).
Let
{\rm Mon}(\nabla_{z,w})
be the monodromy representation for any
(z,w)\in\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{r}
. Let
R(X_{0},N)
be the representation variety for
\pi_{1}(X_{0},x)
into
\GL_{N}
. Consider the evaluation map
\operatorname{ev}_{x}:H^{0}(X,E)\to E_{x}
. Let
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{\ell}\}\subset E_{x}
be a subset such that its image forms a basis of the quotient space
E_{x}/\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_{x})
. Then the natural map between analytic germs

\displaystyle f:\left(\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{\ell},0\right)

\displaystyle\to R(X_{0},N)

\displaystyle(z,w;x)

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}x_{i}v_{i}){\rm Mon}(\nabla_{z,w})\exp(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}x_{i}v_{i})

is an isomorphism.

Démonstration.

We recall that we have morphisms of groupoids

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom\!\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},A_{k}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[R_{\varrho}(A_{k})/{\rm Id}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},A_{k})\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A_{k})\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathscr{C}(T^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A_{k})/\exp(T^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k})]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A_{k})/\exp(L^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k})]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},\varepsilon,A_{k})/\exp(C^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k})]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi\times\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

(5.23.1)

in which each of them is an isomorphism by (
5.22.1
), (
5.22.2
), and
5.19
,
3.4
and
3.9
. Here

\displaystyle h:[\mathscr{C}(T^{\bullet},\varepsilon_{x},A_{k})/\exp(T^{0}\otimes\mathfrak{m})]

\displaystyle\to[R_{\varrho}(A_{k})/{\rm Id}]

\displaystyle(\alpha,e^{r})

\displaystyle\mapsto\exp(-r){\rm hol}_{x}(\nabla+\alpha)\exp(r)

is defined in
3.9
.
We denote by

\Upsilon:\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},A_{k})\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}\to[R_{\varrho}(A_{k})/{\rm Id}]

the induced equivalence of groupoids in (
). Then, for

\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}(z,w)\in\mathscr{C}(L^{\bullet},A_{k})

defined in (
5.22.7
),

\Upsilon(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}(z,w),\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}x_{j}v_{j})=\exp(-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}x_{i}v_{i}){\rm Mon}(\nabla_{z,w})\exp(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}x_{i}v_{i}),

corresponds to the morphism

f_{k}:\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{R(X_{0},N),\varrho}\to A_{k}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}

induced by
f
.

Let us define a morphism

g_{k}\in\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},A_{k})

by setting

\displaystyle g_{k}(\alpha_{i}^{*})=z_{i},\quad g_{k}(\beta_{\gamma}^{*})=w_{\gamma},\quad g_{k}(v_{j}^{*})=x_{j}.

(5.23.2)

By (
5.22.8
), we have

\Phi(\sum_{j=1}^{2k}\eta_{j}(z,w),\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}x_{i}v_{i})=g_{k}.

One can see the compatibility of
{g}_{k}
by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g}_{k+1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{g}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}

In conclusion, there exists natural maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom\!\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},A_{k}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom\!\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},A_{k-1}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A_{k})\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}(C^{\bullet},A_{k-1})\times W\otimes\mathfrak{m}_{k-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{{R(X_{0},N)},\varrho},A_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{{R(X_{0},N)},\varrho},A_{k-1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Upsilon}

such that all vertical maps are isomorphisms. We have the inverse limit

\varprojlim_{k}g_{k}\in\Hom\!\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},\varprojlim_{k}A_{k}\right)=\Hom\!\left(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q},0},\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0}\right)

and by (
5.23.2
), it is an isomorphism.
By (
), it follows that

f^{*}=\varprojlim_{k}f_{k}:\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{R(X_{0},N),\varrho}\to\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathscr{Q}^{\prime\prime},0},

is also an isomorphism.
By
3.5
, this implies that

f:(\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}_{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{\ell},0)\to(R(X_{0},N),\varrho)
is an
isomorphism
between analytic germs.
The theorem is therefore proved.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

The above theorem shows that, for extendable unitary representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
, the analytic germ of the representation variety
R(X_{0},N)
at
\varrho
is isomorphic to a quasi-homogeneous singularity defined by generators of weights
1
and
2
, subject to relations of weights
2,3,
and
4
. Similar result for representations with finite image were obtained by Kapovich-Millson
[
KM98
]
based on Morgan’s theorems
[
Mor78
]
. See also
[
Lef19
]
.

5.9
2-step phenomenon

We are now in a position to establish the main theorem concerning the so-called
2-step phenomenon
for extendable unitary systems on quasi-compact Kähler manifolds.

Let
\varrho
be a unitary representation of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X)
, and let
(V,\nabla)
be the associated flat bundle. We denote by
(E,\nabla):=(\operatorname{End}(V),\nabla)
the induced bundle of endomorphisms. Let
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{C}^{p+q}
be the quasi-homogeneous cone defined in (
5.19.1
). Fix some
r>0
sufficiently small as in
5.23
, let
\mathcal{T}:=\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{B}_{r}^{p+q}
. Then the universal connection
\nabla_{z,w}
defined in (
5.22.5
) induces a tautological flat connection on
V\otimes\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{T})
. It corresponds to a representation

\pi_{1}(X_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varrho_{\mathcal{T}}}}{{\to}}\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},0}).

Let
\mathfrak{m}
be the maximal ideal for the local ring
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},0}
. For each
i
, we have

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mathcal{T},i}:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varrho_{\mathcal{T}}}}{{\to}}\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{i}).

(5.24.1)

We are interested in the case
i=3
.
Note that as a complex vector bundle, we have a decomposition

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle C^{\infty}}}{{\simeq}}\oplus_{i=0}^{2}E\otimes{\mathfrak{m}^{i}/\mathfrak{m}^{i+1}}.

(5.24.2)

With respect to this decomposition, we write the connection explicitly.

Recall that we consider
z_{1},\ldots,z_{p},w_{1},\ldots,w_{q}
as linear functions on the vector space
H^{1}(X,E)\oplus H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
given by

\displaystyle z_{k}(\sum_{i=1}^{p}a_{i}\{\eta_{i,0}\},\sum_{\gamma=1}^{q}b_{\gamma}\eta_{\gamma})\mapsto a_{k}

\displaystyle w_{\alpha}(\sum_{i=1}^{p}a_{i}\{\eta_{i,0}\},\sum_{\gamma=1}^{q}b_{\gamma}\beta_{\gamma})\mapsto b_{\alpha}.

(5.24.3)

Now we rearrange the basis
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{q}
of
H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)
so that

\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}

is a basis for
\ker g
, where
g:H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)\to H^{2}(X,E)
is the Gysin morphism defined in
5.3
. We have

\ell:=\dim H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)-\rank g.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the local system on
X_{0}
corresponding to
(E,\nabla)
. By
[
Del70
, II, 6.10]
together with
5.19
, we have

H^{1}(C^{\bullet},d)\simeq H^{1}(A^{\bullet}(X,D,E),\nabla)\simeq H^{1}(X_{0},\mathcal{E}),

where
(C^{\bullet},d)
is the dgla defined in
5.17
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{-1}:

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}=\frac{\left(H^{1}(X,E)\oplus H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)\right)^{*}}{(w_{\ell+1},\ldots,w_{q})}\cong\left(H^{1}(C^{\bullet},d)\right)^{*}\cong\left(H^{1}(X_{0},\mathcal{E})\right)^{*}

(5.24.4)

where the isomorphism follows from
5.19
, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{-2}:

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{m}^{2}/\mathfrak{m}^{3}=\frac{{\rm Sym}^{2}\left(H^{1}(X,E)\oplus H^{0}(D^{(1)},E)\right)^{*}}{Q}

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{Sym}^{2}\left(z_{1},\ldots,z_{p},w_{1},\ldots,w_{\ell}\right)}{\overline{Q}}

where
\overline{Q}
is the linear space generated by the quadratic equations in
5.21.(ii)
and
5.21.(iii)
restricted to the subspace
w_{\ell+1}=\dots=w_{q}=0
. In other words, we set

w_{\ell+1}=\cdots=w_{q}=0

in the equations in
5.21.(ii)
and
5.21.(iii)
.
These subsequently become linear equations in the quadratic monomials

\{z_{i}z_{j},\,z_{i}w_{\gamma},\,w_{\alpha}w_{\beta}\}_{i,j\in\{1,\ldots,p\};\,\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\{1,\ldots,\ell\}}.

The above quadratic monomials generate
\mathfrak{m}^{2}/\mathfrak{m}^{3}
, and the linear equations describe all the relations among them.
Hence, a basis of
\mathfrak{m}^{2}/\mathfrak{m}^{3}
is obtained by taking these quadratic monomials

modulo these linear relations
.

Since
g(\beta_{\ell+1}),\dots,g(\beta_{q})\in H^{2}(X,E)
are linearly independent, the equations in
5.21.(i)
simplify to the following form :

w_{\alpha}=Q_{\alpha}(z_{1},\dots,z_{p}),\quad\text{for }\alpha=\ell+1,\dots,q,

(5.24.6)

where each
Q_{\alpha}(z_{1},\dots,z_{p})
is a quadratic polynomial. Consequently, we identify the class of
w_{\alpha}
with
Q_{\alpha}(z_{1},\dots,z_{p})
in
\mathbb{E}_{-2}
. Finally, we define the degree zero component as

\mathbb{E}_{0}:=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T}}/\mathfrak{m}\cong\mathbb{C}.

In conclusion, we have the following expression of the connection
\nabla_{3}
on
\mathbf{V}_{3}|_{X_{0}}
within the smooth decomposition (
5.24.2
) :

\nabla_{3}:=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{E}_{-2}}&\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i,0}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\ell}w_{\alpha}\eta_{0,\alpha}&\omega_{2}(z,w)\\
0&\nabla\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{E}_{-1}}&\sum_{i=1}^{p}z_{i}\eta_{i,0}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\ell}w_{\alpha}\eta_{0,\alpha}\\
0&0&\nabla\otimes{\rm Id}_{\mathbb{E}_{0}}\end{pmatrix}

(5.24.7)

Here we denote by

\displaystyle\omega_{2}:=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\sum_{\gamma=1}^{\ell}z_{i}w_{\gamma}\eta_{i,\gamma}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{p}z_{i}z_{j}\eta_{ij,0}+\sum_{\beta,\gamma=1}^{\ell}w_{\beta}w_{\gamma}\eta_{0,\beta\gamma}+\sum_{\alpha=\ell+1}^{q}Q_{\alpha}(z)\eta_{0,\alpha},

(5.24.8)

where

\eta_{i,\gamma},\eta_{ij,0},\eta_{0,\beta\gamma},\eta_{0,\alpha}\in A^{1}(X_{0},E)

are all defined in (
5.21.1
). Note that
\nabla_{3}
is flat on
\mathbf{V}_{3}|_{X_{0}}
by
5.23
.

Theorem 5.25

(2-Step Phenomenon)
.

Let
h:Y\to X
be a holomorphic map between compact Kähler manifolds. Let
D
be a simple normal crossing divisor on
X
such that
D_{Y}:=h^{-1}(D)
is also a simple normal crossing divisor. Set
X_{0}:=X\backslash D
and
Y_{0}:=Y\backslash D_{Y}
and fix a base point
x\in X_{0}
. Denote by
h_{0}:Y_{0}\to X_{0}
the restriction of
h
on
Y_{0}
.
Let
(V,\nabla)
be a unitary flat bundle on
X
with monodromy
representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X,x)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
, and set
x=h(y)
.
Denote by

h_{0}^{*}:R(X_{0},N)\longrightarrow R(Y_{0},N),

the induced morphism of representation varieties, given by

\tau\mapsto h_{0}^{*}\tau
for any
\tau\in R(X_{0},N)
. We denote by
i_{0}:X_{0}\hookrightarrow X
the inclusion map, and
\varrho_{0}=i_{0}^{*}\varrho:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
.

(i)

Assume that
h_{0}^{*}\varrho_{\mathcal{T},3}
is a trivial representation.
Then for any representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
that is in the same geometric irreducible component of
\varrho_{0}
,

h_{0}^{*}\tau
is a trivial representation.

(ii)

Assume that
D_{Y}=\varnothing
, i.e.
Y=Y_{0}
, and
\varrho
is trivial. Assume moreover that
a\circ h(Y)
is a point, where
a:X_{0}\to A
denotes the quasi-Albanese map of
X_{0}
.
Then for any representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
that is in the same irreducible component of
\varrho_{0}
,

h_{0}^{*}\tau
is a trivial representation. In particular, for any unipotent representation
\tau:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
, we have
h_{0}^{*}\tau
is trivial.

Démonstration.

It suffices to prove that
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{z,w}=h_{0}^{*}\nabla
for all
(z,w)\in\mathcal{T}
. Indeed, since
(h_{0}^{*}V,h_{0}^{*}\nabla)
is assumed to be a trivial flat bundle in both items above, this equality combined with
5.23
implies that the algebraic morphism

h_{0}^{*}\colon R(X_{0},N)\longrightarrow R(Y_{0},N)

is constant on an analytic neighborhood of
\varrho_{0}
in
R(X_{0},N)
. Since the map is algebraic, it follows that
h_{0}^{*}
is constant on the entire irreducible component of
R(X_{0},N)
containing
\varrho_{0}
.

Fix an arbitrary
(z_{0},w_{0})\in\mathcal{T}
. We aim to show that
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{z_{0},w_{0}}=h_{0}^{*}\nabla
. Note that
\mathscr{Q}^{\prime}
is a quasi-homogeneous cone with weight 2 in
z
and weight 1 in
w
.
We then can define a holomorphic curve
\varphi:\mathbb{D}_{1+\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{T}
by
\varphi(t)=(t^{2}z_{0},tw_{0})
for some small
\varepsilon>0
. Then we have the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{D}_{1+\varepsilon},0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{D}_{1+\varepsilon},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}[t]/(t^{3}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{\mathcal{T}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho_{\mathcal{T},3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}}

(5.25.1)

Consider the family of connections over the disk given by
\nabla_{t}:=\nabla_{t^{2}z_{0},tw_{0}}
. The monodromy of this family corresponds to the representation
\varphi^{*}\circ\varrho_{\mathcal{T}}:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{D}_{1+\varepsilon},0})
.
Let
(E_{3},\nabla_{3})
be the corresponding flat bundle for the representation

\sigma:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}[t]/(t^{3})).

Then the connection matrix
\nabla_{3}
is given by

\nabla_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}\nabla\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{t^{2}/t^{3}}&t\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})&t^{2}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})\\
0&\nabla\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{t/t^{2}}&t\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})\\
0&0&\nabla\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{C}}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.25.2)

Proof of (i)
: By assumption, the pullback representation

h_{0}^{*}\varrho_{\mathcal{T},3}:\pi_{1}(Y_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{3})

is trivial. Consequently, the induced representation

h_{0}^{*}\sigma:\pi_{1}(Y_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}[t]/(t^{3}))

is also trivial. The triviality of the connection
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{3}
implies that

—

h_{0}^{*}\nabla
is trivial ;

—

we have
\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})=0
and
\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})=0
.

By
5.14.(iii)
, it follows that
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{t}=h_{0}^{*}\nabla
for all
t\in\mathbb{D}_{1+\varepsilon}
. In particular, this implies
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{z_{0},w_{0}}=h_{0}^{*}\nabla
. Since
(z_{0},w_{0})
is an arbitrary point in
\mathcal{T}
, we conclude that
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{z,w}=h_{0}^{*}\nabla
for all
(z,w)\in\mathcal{T}
, that is the trivial representation.
This proves item (i).

Proof of (ii)
: Since
\varrho
is trivial, it follows from (
5.21.2
) that

\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,E)\simeq\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N^{2}}.

By the assumption that
a\circ h(Y)=\{\rm pt\}
, we have

h^{*}\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})=0.

By
5.14
, we have

\nabla\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})+\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})\wedge\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})=0.

Hence,

\nabla h^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})=\nabla h^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})+h^{*}\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})\wedge h^{*}\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})=0.

Recall that
h(Y_{0})\cap D=\varnothing
. It follows that
h^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})\in A^{1}(X,E)\cap\ker\nabla
. By
5.14
, we know that
\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact ; hence, its pullback
h^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})
is also
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact. By
2.16
, we have

h^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})=0.

By
5.14.(iii)
, it follows that
h^{*}\nabla_{t}=h^{*}\nabla
for all
t\in\mathbb{D}_{1+\varepsilon}
. In particular, this implies
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{z_{0},w_{0}}=h_{0}^{*}\nabla
. This proves Item (ii).
∎

Proof of
C
.

Since
(E,\nabla)
is a trivial flat bundle, the augmentation map

\varepsilon_{x}\colon H^{0}(\overline{X},E)\to E_{x}

is surjective. Consequently, the complex vector space
W
, defined in the proof of
5.22
, is zero-dimensional.

By
5.23
, the natural map of analytic germs

\displaystyle f\colon(\mathcal{T},0)

\displaystyle\to\left(R(X,N),\varrho\right)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto\operatorname{Mon}(\nabla_{z,w})

is an isomorphism. The theorem is proved.
∎

5.10
A geometric application

As a direct consequence of
5.25.(ii)
, we provide a new proof of a result established by Green–Griffiths–Katzarkov
[
GGK24
]
and Aguilar–Campana
[
AAC25
]
.

Theorem 5.26
.

Let
X
be a quasi-compact Kähler manifold such that its quasi-Albanese map
a:X\to A
is proper. If
\pi_{1}(X)
is nilpotent, then its Shafarevich morphism is the Stein factorization
g:X\to S
of
a
. Moreover, the universal covering of
X
is holomorphically convex.

Démonstration.

Note that any finitely generated nilpotent group is linear. Hence, we may assume the existence of a faithful representation
\tau\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\operatorname{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
with nilpotent image.

After replacing
\tau
by some conjugate, we may decompose the representation as

\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),\qquad\tau_{i}:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N_{i}}(\mathbb{C}),

where
\tau_{1}(\pi_{1}(X))
is abelian and torsion-free, and
\tau_{2}(\pi_{1}(X))
is unipotent.

Let
f:Y\to X
be any compactifiable holomorphic map from a quasi-compact Kähler manifold
Y
to
X
such that the image
a(f(Y))
is a point.
Since
a
is proper, the image
f(Y)
is contained in a compact fiber. Consequently, by replacing
Y
with a suitable bimeromorphic modification (or compactification), we may assume that
Y
is a compact Kähler manifold. Under this assumption,
5.25.(ii)
implies that
f^{*}\tau_{2}
is trivial.

On the other hand, since
\tau_{1}(\pi_{1}(X))
is abelian,
\tau_{1}
factors through
H_{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover, the induced map
a_{*}:H_{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})/{\rm tor}\to H_{1}(A,\mathbb{Z})
is an isomorphism. Thus, there exists a factorization :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GL_{N_{1}}(\mathbb{C}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{1}}

Since
a(f(Y))
is a point, the image of
\pi_{1}(Y)
in
\pi_{1}(A)
is trivial. It follows that
f^{*}\tau_{1}(\pi_{1}(Y))=\{1\}
.
Consequently,
f^{*}\tau(\pi_{1}(Y))=\{1\}
. Since
\tau
is faithful, this implies that
f_{*}\pi_{1}(Y)
is trivial.

Next, by the characterization of the quasi-Albanese map, for any compactifiable holomorphic map
f:Y\to X
from a quasi-compact Kähler manifold, if
a(f(Y))
is not a point, then the image of
f^{*}H^{1}(X,\mathbb{C})
is non-trivial. This implies that
f_{*}\pi_{1}(Y)
is infinite.
Combining this with the contracting property established above, we conclude that
g
is the Shafarevich morphism of
X
.

We now show the last assertion. By Selberg’s lemma, any finitely generated linear group is virtually torsion-free. Hence, up to replacing
X
by a finite étale cover, we may assume that
\pi_{1}(X)
is torsion-free. Consequently, we have

\displaystyle{\rm Im}[\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(X)]=\{1\}\quad\mbox{ for each fiber }F\mbox{ of }g.

(5.26.1)

Let
\widetilde{X}^{\rm ab}
be a connected component of the fiber product
X\times_{A}\widetilde{A}
, where
\widetilde{A}\to A
denotes the universal covering of
A
, and let
\varphi:\widetilde{X}^{\rm ab}\to W
be the Stein factorization of the induced map
\widetilde{X}^{\rm ab}\to\widetilde{A}
.

Let
\pi:\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{X}^{\rm ab}
be the universal cover of
\widetilde{X}^{\rm ab}
(which is also the universal cover of
X
), and consider the composition
\Phi:=\varphi\circ\pi:\widetilde{X}\to W
. By (
5.26.1
), for any
y\in W
, its preimage
\Phi^{-1}(y)=\pi^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(y))
in
\widetilde{X}
is a disjoint union of copies of the compact connected space
\varphi^{-1}(y)
. In particular, the connected components of the fibers of
\Phi
are compact.

By a theorem of Cartan
[
Car60
]
, the set
Z
of connected components of the fibers of
\Phi
can be endowed with the structure of a normal complex space such that
\Phi
factors as
h\circ{\psi}
, where
{\psi}:\widetilde{X}\to Z
is a proper holomorphic fibration and
h:Z\to W
is a map with discrete fibers. This yields the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{X}^{\rm ab}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

Let
G:=\mathrm{Aut}(\widetilde{X}/\widetilde{X}^{\rm ab})
be the Galois group of the covering
\pi
. By (
5.26.1
) again, the preimage
\pi^{-1}(\varphi^{-1}(y))
is exactly the
G
-orbit of a single connected component in
\widetilde{X}
, and thus the group
G
acts transitively and freely on the set of connected components of the fibers of
\Phi
. Consequently,
G
induces a free action on the complex space
Z
. Furthermore, this action is properly discontinuous (see, e.g.,
[
DYK23
, Lemma 3.34]
). This implies that the natural action of
G
on
Z
is free and properly discontinuous.

By construction, the quotient
Z/G
is canonically isomorphic to
W
, meaning
h:Z\to W
is a topological covering map with Galois group
G
. Note that the map
W\to\widetilde{A}
is finite and proper, and since
\widetilde{A}\cong\mathbb{C}^{\dim A}
is Stein,
W
is Stein. Since
h:Z\to W
is a topological covering of a Stein space,
Z
is also Stein.

Finally, since
{\psi}:\widetilde{X}\to Z
is a proper holomorphic map to the Stein space
Z
, it follows from the Cartan-Remmert theorem that
\widetilde{X}
is holomorphically convex. The theorem is proved.
∎

6
Two-step nilpotent monodromy of local systems on special varieties

6.1
Two-step phenomenon for trivial local systems

It is natural to ask when the condition in
5.25.(i)
is satisfied, enabling the application of the 2-step phenomenon. In
6.1
, we show that for the trivial flat bundle
(V,\nabla)
of rank
N
on
X
, the associated representation

\varrho_{\mathcal{T},3}:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},0}/\mathfrak{m}^{3}),

defined in (
5.24.1
), becomes trivial when restricted to any fiber
F
of the second iterated quasi-Albanese map ; that is, the quasi-Albanese map of a fiber of the quasi-Albanese map of
X_{0}
. Consequently, the 2-step phenomenon applies to these fibers
F
. As an application, we prove
A
.

Throughout this subsection, we will keep the same notations as in
section

5.9
, and we will only recall their meaning when necessary.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
X
,
Y
, and
Z
be compact Kähler manifolds. Let
f\colon Y\to X
and
g\colon Z\to Y
be holomorphic maps such that
D_{Y}:=f^{-1}(D)
and
D_{Z}:=g^{-1}(D_{Y})
are simple normal crossing divisors in
Y
and
Z
, respectively. Set
Y_{0}:=Y\setminus D_{Y}
, and
Z_{0}:=Z\setminus D_{Z}
, and denote the restrictions by
f_{0}:=f|_{Y_{0}}
and
g_{0}:=g|_{Z_{0}}
.

Assume that the images of
Y_{0}
and
Z_{0}
under the respective quasi-Albanese maps are points ; that is,

\displaystyle\operatorname{alb}_{X_{0}}(f(Y_{0}))=\{\mathrm{pt}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{alb}_{Y_{0}}(g(Z_{0}))=\{\mathrm{pt}\},

(6.1.1)

where
\operatorname{alb}_{X_{0}}\colon X_{0}\to\operatorname{Alb}(X_{0})
and
\operatorname{alb}_{Y_{0}}\colon Y_{0}\to\operatorname{Alb}(Y_{0})
denote the quasi-Albanese maps.

Then the pullback representation

(f_{0}\circ g_{0})^{*}\varrho_{\mathcal{T}}:\pi_{1}(Z_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},0})

is trivial. In particular, for any representation
\tau\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0},x)\to\operatorname{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
belonging to the irreducible component of the representation variety
{R}(X_{0},N)
that contains the trivial representation, the pullback representation
(f_{0}\circ g_{0})^{*}\tau
is trivial.

Démonstration.

Let
h_{0}:=f_{0}\circ g_{0}
. We proceed analogously to the proof of
5.25.(i)
and adopt the notation used therein. It suffices to show that
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{z,w}=h_{0}^{*}\nabla
for all
(z,w)\in\mathcal{T}
.

Fix an arbitrary point
(z_{0},w_{0})\in\mathcal{T}
. For some small
\varepsilon>0
, define a holomorphic curve
\varphi:\mathbb{D}_{1+\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{T}
by
\varphi(t)=(t^{2}z_{0},tw_{0})
. Consider the family of connections parametrized by the disk, given by
\nabla_{t}:=\nabla_{t^{2}z_{0},tw_{0}}
. Let
(E_{3},\nabla_{3})
be the flat bundle associated with the induced representation modulo
t^{3}
:

\sigma:\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C}[t]/(t^{3}))

defined in (
), where the connection matrix
\nabla_{3}
is defined in
(
). Since
(E,\nabla)
is a trivial flat bundle, by (
5.21.2
),

\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,E)\simeq\mathcal{H}^{1}(X,\mathbb{C})\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N^{2}}.

By (
6.1.1
), we have

f^{*}\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})=0.

By
5.14
, we have

\nabla\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})+\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})\wedge\eta_{1}(z_{0},w_{0})=0.

This implies that,
\nabla f^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})=0
. Hence we have

f^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})\in A^{1}(Y,D_{Y},E)\cap\ker\nabla\simeq\left(A^{1}(Y,D_{Y},\mathbb{C})\cap\ker d\right)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N^{2}}

Let
(f^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0}))^{1,0}
denote the
(1,0)
-component in
A^{1}(Y,D_{Y},E)
. Then

\displaystyle(f^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0}))^{1,0}\in H^{0}(Y,\Omega_{Y}(\log D_{Y})\otimes E)\simeq H^{0}(Y,\Omega_{Y}(\log D_{Y}))\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N^{2}}.

(6.1.2)

By (
6.1.1
),

g^{*}(f^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0}))^{1,0}=0.

Consequently, the form
g^{*}f^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})
is purely of type
(0,1)
and is
\nabla
-closed :

g^{*}f^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})\in A^{0,1}(Z,D_{Z},E)\cap\ker\nabla\simeq\left(A^{0,1}(Z)\cap\ker d\right)\otimes\mathbb{C}^{N^{2}}.

(6.1.3)

By
5.14
, we know that
\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})
is
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact ; hence, its pullback
g^{*}f^{*}\eta_{2}(z_{0},w_{0})
is also
\bm{\nabla}^{\prime}
-exact. Combining this with (
), we conclude that it vanishes identically.

By
5.14.(iii)
, it follows that
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{t}=h_{0}^{*}\nabla
for all
t\in\mathbb{D}_{1+\varepsilon}
. In particular, this implies
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{z_{0},w_{0}}=h_{0}^{*}\nabla
. Since
(z_{0},w_{0})
is an arbitrary point in
\mathcal{T}
, we conclude that
h_{0}^{*}\nabla_{z,w}=h_{0}^{*}\nabla
for all
(z,w)\in\mathcal{T}
.

Consequently, the pullback representation

(f_{0}\circ g_{0})^{*}\varrho_{\mathcal{T}}:\pi_{1}(Z_{0})\to\GL_{N}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{T},0})

is trivial. By
5.23
, for any representation
\tau\colon\pi_{1}(X_{0},x)\to\operatorname{GL}_{N}(\mathbb{C})
belonging to the irreducible component of the representation variety
R(X_{0},N)
containing the trivial representation, the pullback representation
(f_{0}\circ g_{0})^{*}\tau
is trivial. The proposition is proved.
∎

6.2
Proof of
A

We are now in a position to prove
A
, assuming
E
. The proof of the latter is deferred to the next section.

Theorem 6.2

(=
A
)
.

Let
X
be smooth quasi-projective variety that is special. Then for any linear representation
\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
, its image
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
is virtually nilpotent of class 2.

Démonstration.

By
2.11
, after replacing
X
by a finite étale cover, we may assume that the Zariski closure of
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
decomposes as a direct product of its central torus and its unipotent radical. This induces a natural decomposition of the representation

\varrho=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\colon\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N_{1}}(\mathbb{C})\times\GL_{N_{2}}(\mathbb{C}),

where
\tau_{1}(\pi_{1}(X))
is abelian and torsion-free, and
\tau_{2}(\pi_{1}(X))
is unipotent. It then suffices to show that
\tau_{2}(\pi_{1}(X))
is nilpotent of class
2
.

Let
\operatorname{alb}_{X}\colon X\to\operatorname{Alb}(X)
be the quasi-Albanese map of
X
. By
proposition

2.13
, it is a dominant morphism with a connected, smooth general fiber
Y
, yielding the exact sequence

\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(\operatorname{Alb}(X))\to\{1\}.

Applying
\tau_{2}
, we see that the quotient group

\tau_{2}(\pi_{1}(X))\big/\tau_{2}(\operatorname{Im}[\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)])

is therefore abelian.

By
E
,
Y
is also special. Applying
proposition

2.13
again, the quasi-Albanese morphism
\operatorname{alb}_{Y}\colon Y\to\operatorname{Alb}(Y)
is a dominant morphism with a connected general fiber
F
. Since
\tau_{2}(\pi_{1}(X))
is unipotent, it follows from
4.13
that
\tau_{2}
lies in the irreducible component of the representation variety
R(X,N_{2})(\mathbb{C})
that contains the trivial representation. Letting
\iota\colon Y\hookrightarrow X
denote the inclusion map,
6.1
yields

\iota^{*}\tau_{2}(\operatorname{Im}[\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(Y)])=\{1\}.

Furthermore, by
proposition

2.13
we have the exact sequence

\pi_{1}(F)\to\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(\operatorname{Alb}(Y))\to\{1\}.

This implies that
\iota^{*}\tau_{2}\colon\pi_{1}(Y)\to\GL_{N_{2}}(\mathbb{C})
factors through the abelian group
\pi_{1}(\operatorname{Alb}(Y))
. Consequently, the image
\iota^{*}\tau_{2}(\pi_{1}(Y))=\tau_{2}(\operatorname{Im}[\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)])
is abelian. It follows that
\tau_{2}(\pi_{1}(X))
is virtually nilpotent of class
2
. The theorem is proved.
∎

6.3
A structure theorem

As a consequence of
6.2
, we obtain the following structure theorem.

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
X
be a special smooth quasi-projective variety, and let

\varrho:\pi_{1}(X)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})
be a big representation. Then, after replacing

X
by a finite étale cover, for a very general fiber
Y
of the
quasi-Albanese map
a:X\to A
, the quasi-Albanese map

{\rm alb}_{Y}:Y\to{\rm Alb}(Y)
is birational.

Démonstration.

By Selberg’s lemma, after replacing
X
by a finite étale cover, we may assume
that
\varrho(\pi_{1}(X))
is torsion free. Let
Y
be a very general fiber of the
quasi-Albanese map
a:X\to A
, and let
f:Y\to X
denote the inclusion. By the
proof of
6.2
, the image
f^{*}\varrho(\pi_{1}(Y))
is abelian and torsion
free. Since
\varrho
is big and
Y
is a very general fiber of
a
, it follows
that the restricted representation

f^{*}\varrho:\pi_{1}(Y)\to\GL_{N}(\mathbb{C})

is also big. By
[
CDY25c
, Theorem B.(iv)]
, the quasi-Albanese map

{\rm alb}_{Y}:Y\to{\rm Alb}(Y)
is birational.
∎

7
Quasi-Albanese map of special varieties

In this section, we establish Theorem
E
. To help the reader navigate the ensuing technicalities, we first provide a rough idea of the proof in
[
CC16
]
. Suppose
Y
is a smooth special projective variety, and let
a:Y\to A
be its Albanese map. To show that the general fiber of
a
is special, Campana and Claudon
[
CC16
]
argue by contradiction. They consider the relative core map
c:Y\to X
of the Albanese map
a:Y\to A
, meaning the general fiber of the induced map
f:X\to A
is the core of the corresponding fiber of
a
. By replacing
c
with a suitable neat model, the properties of the core map ensure that for the orbifold base
(X,D_{X})
of
c
, the general fibers
f:(X,D_{X})\to A
are of general type. By a result of
C_{n,m}
conjecture proved by Birkar-Chen, the Kodaira dimension of the orbifold
(X,D_{X})
is at least the relative dimension of
f
, and is therefore strictly positive.

One then considers the Iitaka fibration
j:X\to J
induced by a suitable birational model of
(X,D_{X})
. A crucial step in
[
CC16
]
is showing that
(X,D_{X})
admits a birational model
(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}})
whose Iitaka fibration
\bar{j}:\bar{X}\to J
is the quotient of a locally trivial fibration of abelian varieties by a finite group. This structure implies that the orbifold base
(J,D_{J})
of the map
(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}})\to J
is of general type. Furthermore, by carefully choosing the birational models for
c
and
j
, one can show that the orbifold base of the composition
j\circ c:Y\to J
is neat and coincides exactly with
(J,D_{J})
. This shows
j\circ c:Y\to J
is a fibration of general type, which contradicts the assumption that
Y
is special.

When
Y
is quasi-projective, we follow a similar strategy, but additional technical difficulties arise. In particular, the delicate orbifold structure of the Iitaka fibration
\bar{j}\colon\bar{X}\to J
forces us to make supplementary reductions compared to the arguments in
[
CC16
]
. To bypass these technical obstructions, we do not prove the theorem directly for
Y
. Instead, our strategy crucially relies on making an
a priori
base change : we establish the result for a carefully chosen étale cover of
Y
and its suitable birational models. Passing to this specific étale cover from the very beginning ensures that the resulting Iitaka fibration is a locally trivial fibration of toroidal compactifications of semiabelian varieties over the relevant open locus. The goal of
sections

7.4
and
7.5
is to show that passing to an étale cover and a suitable birational model is a valid reduction that does not introduce any new obstacles for the subsequent arguments in the proof of
E
.

7.1
Some results on semi-abelian varieties

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
A
be a semi-abelian variety and let
f:C\to A
be a morphism from another semi-abelian variety such that
f:C\to f(C)
is an isogeny. Then there exists a surjective isogeny
i:A^{\prime}\to A
such that
i^{-1}(B)=C
, where
B:=f(C)
. Moreover, the natural morphism

A^{\prime}/C\to A/B

is an isomorphism.

Démonstration.

Let
B:=f(C)
, which is a semi-abelian subvariety of
A
.
Write
A=\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma_{A}
, where
\Gamma_{A}\cong\pi_{1}(A)
is a discrete subgroup of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. It follows that there exists a complex subspace
V\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that
B=V/(V\cap\Gamma_{A})
. Write
\Gamma_{B}:=V\cap\Gamma_{A}
. Since
C\to B
is an isogeny, there exists a subgroup
\Gamma_{C}\subset\Gamma_{B}
of finite index such that
C=V/\Gamma_{C}
.

Since
B
is a closed semi-abelian subvariety of
A
, the quotient
A/B\cong(\mathbb{C}^{n}/V)/(\Gamma_{A}/\Gamma_{B})
is also a semi-abelian variety. Consequently, the quotient group
\Gamma_{A}/\Gamma_{B}
is a free abelian group (thus torsion-free). Therefore, there exists an isomorphism

g:\Gamma_{A}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Gamma_{B}\oplus\Gamma_{A}/\Gamma_{B}

given by a (non-canonical) splitting of the exact sequence

0\to\Gamma_{B}\to\Gamma_{A}\to\Gamma_{A}/\Gamma_{B}\to 0.

Consider the subgroup

\Gamma_{A}^{\prime}:=g^{-1}(\Gamma_{C}\oplus\Gamma_{A}/\Gamma_{B})

of
\Gamma_{A}
. Because
\Gamma_{C}
has finite index in
\Gamma_{B}
,
\Gamma_{A}^{\prime}
has finite index in
\Gamma_{A}
. It follows directly from the construction that

\Gamma_{A}^{\prime}\cap V=\Gamma_{A}^{\prime}\cap\Gamma_{B}=\Gamma_{C}.

Therefore, for the semi-abelian variety
A^{\prime}:=\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma_{A}^{\prime}
, the natural morphism

i:\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma_{A}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}/\Gamma_{A}

is a surjective isogeny
i:A^{\prime}\to A
such that

V/(\Gamma_{A}^{\prime}\cap V)=i^{-1}(B)

is a semi-abelian subvariety of
A^{\prime}
that is canonically isomorphic to
C
.
It follows that the induced map on the quotients
A^{\prime}/C\to A/B
is an isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
A
be a semi-abelian variety and let
0\to T\to A\to A_{0}\to 0
be its Chevalley decomposition, where
T\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{d}
is an algebraic torus and
A_{0}
is an abelian variety. Let
Y
be any smooth projective toric compactification of
T
. Then there exists a smooth compactification
\bar{A}
of
A
with a simple normal crossing boundary divisor
D_{A}:=\bar{A}\backslash A
such that :

1.

\bar{A}
is a fiber bundle over
A_{0}
with fiber
Y
(specifically, the associated bundle
\bar{A}=A\times^{T}Y
).

2.

K_{\bar{A}}+D_{A}\sim 0
.

3.

\bar{A}
is a semi-toric variety ; specifically, the translation action
A\times A\to A
extends to an action
A\times\bar{A}\to\bar{A}
.

A compactification satisfying the above properties will be called a
toroidal compactification
for
A
.

Furthermore, let
f:B\to A
be a surjective morphism from another semi-abelian variety
B
.
If
f
is an isogeny, then
f
extends to a morphism
\bar{f}:\bar{B}\to\bar{A}
between some toroidal compactifications
\bar{B}
and
\bar{A}
with simple normal crossing boundary divisors
D_{B}:=\bar{B}\backslash B
and
D_{A}:=\bar{A}\backslash A
, such that
\bar{f}
is a generically finite surjective morphism, and the log-canonical divisors satisfy the pullback relation :

\bar{f}^{*}(K_{\bar{A}}+D_{A})\cong K_{\bar{B}}+D_{B}.

If
f
is surjective with connected kernel
C
, then there exist toroidal compactifications
\bar{B}
and
\bar{A}
with simple normal crossing boundary divisors
D_{B}:=\bar{B}\backslash B
and
D_{A}:=\bar{A}\backslash A
such that :

(i)

the morphism
f
extends to
\bar{f}:\bar{B}\to\bar{A}
, that is isotrivial over
A
.

(ii)

Moreover, every fiber of
\bar{f}
over
A
is isomorphic to a toroidal compactification
\bar{C}
of
C
, satisfying

K_{\bar{C}}+D_{\bar{C}}\sim 0,

where
D_{\bar{C}}:=\bar{C}\backslash C
is the boundary divisor.

Démonstration.

We begin with the
Chevalley decomposition
of
A
:

0\to T\to A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}A_{0}\to 0,

where
A_{0}
is an abelian variety and
T\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{d}
. Analytically,
A
is a principal
T
-bundle over
A_{0}
.

Step 1 : Construction of
\bar{A}
.

Let
Y
be the given smooth projective toric compactification of
T
. The torus
T
acts on
Y
via the standard toric action. We define
\bar{A}
as the associated fiber bundle :

\bar{A}:=A\times^{T}Y=(A\times Y)/\sim,

where
T
acts by
t(a,y)=(t\cdot a,t^{-1}\cdot y)
for
t\in T
. Since
A\to A_{0}
is locally trivial in the analytic topology,
\bar{A}\to A_{0}
is a smooth toric fiber bundle with fiber
Y
.

The boundary
D_{A}:=\bar{A}\backslash A
corresponds to the sub-bundle associated with the toric boundary
D_{Y}=Y\backslash T
. Since
Y
is smooth,
D_{Y}
is a simple normal crossing divisor, and consequently,
D_{A}
is a simple normal crossing divisor on
\bar{A}
.

Set
D_{Y}:=Y\backslash T
. Since
K_{Y}+D_{Y}\sim 0
, it follows that the relative log-canonical bundle
K_{\bar{A}/A_{0}}+D_{A}
is trivial. Since
A_{0}
is an abelian variety then
K_{A_{0}}\sim 0
and it follows that

K_{\bar{A}}+D_{A}\sim 0.

Step 2 : Extension Setup.

Let
0\to T^{\prime}\to B\to B_{0}\to 0
be the Chevalley decomposition of
B
. The morphism
f:B\to A
induces a morphism of exact sequences :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

(7.2.1)

where
h
is a morphism of abelian varieties and
g
is a homomorphism of algebraic tori.
Let
N^{\prime}
and
N
be the co-character lattices of
T^{\prime}
and
T
. The morphism
g
induces a linear map
g_{*}:N^{\prime}\to N
.
Y
is determined by a smooth fan
\Sigma_{Y}
in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
. To construct
\bar{B}
, we must construct a suitable smooth fan
\Sigma_{X}
in
N^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
.

Step 3 : The Isogeny Case.

Assume
f
is an isogeny. Then
g:T^{\prime}\to T
is an isogeny, implying that
g_{*}:N^{\prime}\to N
is an injection of lattices with finite cokernel, inducing an isomorphism of the underlying vector spaces
g_{*,\mathbb{R}}:N^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}\xrightarrow{\sim}N_{\mathbb{R}}
.

We define a fan
\Sigma^{\prime}_{X}
in
N^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
as the exact pullback of the smooth fan
\Sigma_{Y}
under this vector space isomorphism. Since
\Sigma^{\prime}_{X}
may not be regular with respect to the lattice
N^{\prime}
, we let
\Sigma_{X}
be a smooth subdivision of
\Sigma^{\prime}_{X}
.

Let
X
be the smooth toric variety associated to
\Sigma_{X}
. The lattice injection and the fan refinement induce a toric morphism
\bar{g}:X\to Y
which is proper, generically finite, and surjective. We define
\bar{B}:=B\times^{T^{\prime}}X
. Then we have a morphism
\bar{f}:\bar{B}\to\bar{A}
induced by the map
\bar{g}
.
Note that
\bar{g}
pulls back the logarithmic volume form of
Y
to the logarithmic volume form of
X
(up to a scalar factor). Since
K_{\bar{A}}+D_{A}
and
K_{\bar{B}}+D_{B}
are trivialized by these forms extended over the base, we obtain the isomorphism
\bar{f}^{*}(K_{\bar{A}}+D_{A})\cong K_{\bar{B}}+D_{B}
.

Step 4 : The Surjective Case.

Let
N^{\prime}
and
N
be the co-character lattices of
T^{\prime}
and
T
. The surjective group homomorphism
g
induces a linear map of lattices
g_{*}:N^{\prime}\to N
. Its kernel
N_{C}:=\ker(g_{*})
is necessarily a saturated sublattice of
N^{\prime}
, i.e.
N^{\prime}\cap(N_{C})_{\mathbb{R}}=N_{C}
, which is exactly the co-character lattice of the maximal torus
T_{C}\subset C
.

Let
\Sigma_{Y}
be any smooth projective fan in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
compactifying
T
. We choose
\Sigma_{X}
to be any smooth regular fan in
N^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}}
that refines the preimage
g_{*,\mathbb{R}}^{-1}(\Sigma_{Y})
. Because
\Sigma_{X}
is a refinement, the induced toric morphism
\bar{g}:X\to Y
is well-defined.

We define
\Sigma_{C}:=\Sigma_{X}\cap(N_{C})_{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
\Sigma_{X}
is a smooth regular fan, its restriction
\Sigma_{C}
is also a smooth regular fan, which defines a smooth toric compactification
\bar{T}_{C}
of
T_{C}
.

We define the compactifications as associated fiber bundles :
\bar{A}:=A\times^{T}Y
and
\bar{B}:=B\times^{T^{\prime}}X
. The compatible maps of bundles and toric varieties induce the extended morphism
\bar{f}:\bar{B}\to\bar{A}
.

We show that
\bar{f}
is isotrivial over
A
.
The open stratum
A\subset\bar{A}
corresponds to the trivial cone
\{0\}\in\Sigma_{Y}
by our construction. Its preimage in
\Sigma_{X}
is exactly the sub-fan
\Sigma_{C}
. Since the fan
\Sigma_{C}
is contained in the real subspace
(N_{C})_{\mathbb{R}}
generated by the saturated sublattice
N_{C}\subset N^{\prime}
, the toric variety it defines with respect to the larger ambient lattice
N^{\prime}
naturally decomposes as the associated bundle
T^{\prime}\times^{T_{C}}\bar{T}_{C}
. Thus, the restriction of the toric variety
X
over the open torus
T
is precisely
X|_{T}=T^{\prime}\times^{T_{C}}\bar{T}_{C}
.

Consequently, the restriction of the total space over
A
is precisely computed by the associated bundle cancellation :

\bar{B}|_{A}=B\times^{T^{\prime}}(X|_{T})=B\times^{T^{\prime}}(T^{\prime}\times^{T_{C}}\bar{T}_{C})=B\times^{T_{C}}\bar{T}_{C}.

This implies that, the fibers of
\bar{f}
over
A
is isomorphic to
C\times^{T_{C}}\bar{T}_{C}=:\bar{C}.

Note that
\bar{C}
is a toroidal compactification of
C
. This shows the last assertion. The lemma is proved.
∎

Convention :
Throughout this section, unless stated otherwise, any compactification
\bar{A}
of a semiabelian variety
A
is assumed to be a smooth toroidal compactification as in
7.2
such that
K_{\bar{A}}+D_{A}
is trivial, where
D_{A}:=\bar{A}\backslash A
is a simple normal crossing divisor.

7.2
Subadditivity of the logarithmic Kodaira dimension

Theorem 7.3
.

Let
(X,D)
be a smooth projective log pair, where
D
is a simple normal crossing divisor with rational coefficients
\leq 1
,
\bar{A}
a smooth toroidal compactification of a semi-abelian variety
A
, and

f:X\to\bar{A}

be a morphism with connected fibers, such that

\lfloor D\rfloor\geq f^{-1}(D_{A})_{\mathrm{red}}.

If a general fiber
(X_{a},D_{a})
of

f:(X,D)\to\bar{A}

is of log general type, then
\kappa(K_{X}+D)\geq\dim X_{a}
.

This is an immediate consequence of the following more general statement :

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
f:(X,D)\to(Y,E)
be a surjective morphism of projective log
canonical pairs with both
(X,D_{\rm red})
and
(Y,E)
log-smooth. Assume that
\lfloor D\rfloor

contains the support of
f^{*}(E)
, and the generic fiber
(X_{\eta},D_{\eta})
is of log-general type. Then

\kappa(X,D)\geq\kappa(X_{\eta},D_{\eta})+\kappa(Y,E).

Démonstration.

Note that this theorem is stated in
[
Wei20
, Theorem 12]
, however the proof is omitted and it is stated that it can
be easily deduced from
[
KP17
, Theorems 9.5, 9.6]
. We include the details for the benefit of the reader but claim no originality.

We follow
[
KP17
, Section 9]
. Note that if
D
is reduced, then this follows from
[
KP17
, Theorem 9.6]
. Clearly we may assume
E=E_{\rm red}
and we may assume that
D^{v}\leq\lfloor D\rfloor
where
D=D^{v}+D^{h}
and each component of
D^{v}
is vertical over
Y
and each component of
D^{h}
dominates
Y
.
We now follow the proof of
[
KP17
, Proposition 9.12]
.
In Step 1, we define
D^{\prime}=\rho^{-1}_{*}D^{h}+(\rho^{*}D^{v})_{\rm red}
. All the required properties are easily seen to hold (that is
\kappa(K_{X_{\eta}}+D_{\eta})=\kappa(K_{{X^{\prime}}_{\eta}}+D^{\prime}_{\eta})
,
\rho_{*}(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})=K_{X}+D
, and
\kappa(K_{X}+D)\geq\kappa(K_{X^{\prime}}+D^{\prime})
). Thus, we may assume that the consequences of [Lemma 9.10, KP16] hold
for
f:(X,D)\to Y
.
In Step 2, we apply
[
KP17
, Lemma 9.10]
to
f:(X,G:=D^{v})\to Y
to obtain
f^{\prime}:(X^{\prime},G^{\prime})\to Y^{\prime}
. We let
\rho:X^{\prime}\to X
and set
D^{\prime}:=G^{\prime}+\rho^{-1}_{*}D^{h}
. Since
\rho^{-1}_{*}D^{h}\leq\rho^{*}D^{h}
, it follows easily from the last part of the proof of
[
KP17
, Lemma 9.11]
that

h^{0}(m(K_{X^{\prime}/Y^{\prime}}+D^{\prime}+{f^{\prime}}^{*}\tau^{*}(K_{Y}+E)))\geq h^{0}(\zeta^{*}(m(K_{X}+D))).

(7.4.1)

Recall that
\zeta:X^{n}\to X
where
X^{n}
is the normalization of
X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
. We then have

\kappa(K_{X}+D)=\kappa(\zeta^{*}(m(K_{X}+D)))\geq\kappa(K_{X^{\prime}/Y^{\prime}}+D^{\prime}+{f^{\prime}}^{*}\tau^{*}(K_{Y}+E))\geq

\kappa(K_{X^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}}}+D^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}})+\kappa(\tau^{*}(K_{Y}+E))=\kappa(K_{X_{\eta}}+D_{\eta})+\kappa(K_{Y}+E)

where the first (in)-equality follows as
\zeta
is finite, the second from (
7.4.1
), the third by
[
KP17
, Theorem 9.9]
, and the fourth as
(X^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}},D^{\prime}_{\eta^{\prime}})=(X_{\eta},D_{\eta})_{\eta^{\prime}}
and
\tau
is finite.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
f:(X,D_{X})\to(A,D_{A})
be an orbifold morphism of smooth orbifolds such that
f:X\to A
is birational, where
(A,D_{A})
is a toroidal compactification of a semi-abelian variety
A^{0}:=A\backslash D_{A}
. If
\kappa(K_{X}+D_{X})=0
, then
f_{*}D_{X}=D_{A}
, in other words ;
f:(X,D_{X})\to(A,D_{A})
is an elementary birational orbifold morphism (cf.
2.2
).

Démonstration.

Since
f:(X,D_{X})\to(A,D_{A})
is an orbifold morphism, by
2.2
, we have

D:=f^{-1}(D_{A})\leq\lfloor D_{X}\rfloor.

Note that

0=\bar{\kappa}(X\backslash f^{-1}(D_{A}))\leq\bar{\kappa}(X\backslash\lfloor D_{X}\rfloor)=\kappa(K_{X}+\lfloor D_{X}\rfloor)\leq\kappa(K_{X}+D_{X})=0.

By
[
CDY25a
, Lemma 1.3]
, it follows that,
X\backslash\lfloor D_{X}\rfloor\to A^{0}
is proper over a big open subset. This implies that

\lfloor D_{X}\rfloor=D+R

where
R
is some
f
-exceptional effective divisor.

Note that
K_{X}+D=f^{*}(K_{A}+D_{A})+F
where
F
is
f
-exceptional. Note that
K_{A}+D_{A}\cong 0
. Write
G:=D_{X}-\lfloor D_{X}\rfloor
. It follows that

\kappa(F+R+G)=\kappa(K_{X}+D_{X})=0

where
F+R
is some
f
-exceptional and effective divisor. Hence we have

\kappa(G)=\kappa(F+R+G)=0.

Therefore, we have

\kappa(K_{X}+\lceil D_{X}\rceil)=\kappa(F+R+\lceil G\rceil)=\kappa(\lceil G\rceil)=\kappa(G)=0.

By
[
CDY25a
, Lemma 1.3]
again, it follows that
X\backslash\lceil D_{X}\rceil\to A^{0}
is proper over a big open subset. Therefore, we have
f_{*}(D_{X})=D_{A}
. The lemma is proved. ∎

7.3
A structure theorem

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
f:(X,D_{X})\to(A,D_{A})
be a surjective morphism of smooth projective orbifold pairs with connected fibers, where
D_{A}
is reduced and
(A,D_{A})
is a toroidal compactification of the semi-abelian variety
A^{0}:=A\backslash D_{A}
. Define
X_{0}:=f^{-1}(A^{0})
. Suppose that
j:X\to J
is the Iitaka fibration of
(X,D_{X})
and that for a general point
y\in J
, the restricted morphism
f_{y}:=f|_{X_{y}}
is birational onto its image, where
X_{y}:=j^{-1}(y)
.

Then after replacing
(X,D_{X})
and
J
with higher birational models
(X^{\prime},D_{X^{\prime}})
and
J^{\prime}
, where
\nu:X^{\prime}\to X
is a birational morphism and
D_{X^{\prime}}:=\nu^{-1}_{*}D_{X}+{\rm Ex}(\nu)
, there exists a birational morphism
(X,D_{X})\to(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}})
such that the following diagram commutes :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{j}}

such that the following properties hold :

—

For
\bar{X}_{0}:=p^{-1}(A^{0})
, there exists a Zariski dense open subset
J^{0}\subset J
satisfying
\bar{j}(\bar{X}_{0})=J^{0}
, and the restriction
\bar{j}|_{\bar{X}_{0}}:\bar{X}_{0}\to J^{0}
is a locally trivial fibration whose fibers are isomorphic to a fixed semi-abelian subvariety
B^{0}\subset A^{0}
.

—

The composition
\bar{j}\circ h
is the Iitaka fibration
j
of
(X,D_{X})
.

—

We define the divisor
D_{\bar{X}}:=h_{*}(D_{X})
as push-forward of
D_{X}
via
h
. Then the orbifold base
(J,\Delta(\bar{j},D_{\bar{X}}))
of
j:(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}})\to J
is of general type.

Démonstration.

Throughout the proof, we will frequently replace
(X,D_{X})
with a higher birational model
(X^{\prime},D_{X^{\prime}})
, where
\nu:X^{\prime}\to X
is a birational morphism and
D_{X^{\prime}}:=\nu^{-1}_{*}D_{X}+\text{Ex}(\nu)
.

Set
D_{1}:=f^{-1}(D_{A})
. For a general
y\in J
, let
D_{1,y}:=D_{1}|_{X_{y}}
and
D_{X_{y}}:=D_{X}|_{X_{y}}
. Since
j
is the Iitaka fibration of
(X,D_{X})
, we have
\kappa(K_{X_{y}}+D_{X_{y}})=0
. Since we assume that
f_{y}
is birational onto its image, we have :

0\leq\kappa(K_{X_{y}}+D_{1,y})\leq\kappa(K_{X_{y}}+D_{X_{y}})=\kappa((K_{X}+D_{X})|_{X_{y}})=0.

Let
X^{0}:=f^{-1}(A^{0})
and let
f_{0}:X^{0}\to A^{0}
be the restricted morphism. For a general
y\in J
, let
X^{0}_{y}:=X_{y}\cap X^{0}
. Then the logarithmic Kodaira dimension satisfies
\bar{\kappa}(X^{0}_{y})=\kappa(K_{X_{y}}+D_{1,y})=0
. By
[
CDY25a
, Lemmas 1.3, 1.4]
, this implies that
f_{0}|_{X_{y}^{0}}:X_{y}^{0}\to A^{0}
factors through a proper birational morphism
X^{0}_{y}\to B_{y}^{0}
and a closed immersion
B_{y}^{0}\hookrightarrow A^{0}
, where
B_{y}^{0}
is a translate of a semi-abelian subvariety of
A^{0}
. Since
A^{0}
contains at most countably many semi-abelian subvarieties,
B_{y}^{0}
must be a translate of a fixed semi-abelian subvariety
B^{0}\subset A^{0}
. Consequently, there exists a Zariski dense open subset
J^{*}\subset J
together with a morphism
J^{*}\to A^{0}/B^{0}
satisfying the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{0}/B^{0}}

where
j_{0}:=j|_{X^{0}}
and
X^{*}:=j_{0}^{-1}(J^{*})
.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{0}:=A^{0}/B^{0}
, and let
\mathcal{A}
be a toroidal compactification of
\mathcal{A}^{0}
. Up to replacing
J
with a smooth birational model, we may assume that the morphism
J^{*}\to\mathcal{A}
extends to a regular morphism
q:J\to\mathcal{A}
. Write
J^{0}:=q^{-1}(\mathcal{A}^{0})
. We may further assume that the boundary divisor
\partial J:=J\backslash J^{0}
has simple normal crossings. By
7.2
, we can modify the toroidal compactification of
A^{0}
so that the projection
A^{0}\to\mathcal{A}^{0}
extends to a morphism
\pi:A\to\mathcal{A}
with the property that the family
(A,D_{A})\to\mathcal{A}
is isotrivial over
\mathcal{A}^{0}
. Furthermore, the fibers of this family over
\mathcal{A}^{0}
are isomorphic to
(B,D_{B})
, where
B
is a toroidal compactification of
B^{0}
satisfying
K_{B}+D_{B}\sim 0
, with boundary divisor
D_{B}:=B\backslash B^{0}
.

Then
X\to J\times_{\mathcal{A}}A
is a birational morphism, and

X^{0}\to J\times_{\mathcal{A}}A^{0}=J^{0}\times_{\mathcal{A}^{0}}A^{0}=:\bar{X}^{0}

is a proper birational morphism, where the latter is a locally trivial fibration of semi-abelian varieties over
J^{0}
with fibers isomorphic to
B^{0}
. Applying
7.2
again, the projection
J^{0}\times_{\mathcal{A}}A\to J^{0}
is a smooth isotrivial fibration whose fibers are isomorphic to
B
. We take a smooth projective compactification
\bar{X}
for
J^{0}\times_{\mathcal{A}}A
with boundary a simple normal crossing divisor such that
\bar{X}\to J
and
p:\bar{X}\to A
are regular. We replace
X
by a higher birational model and assume that the birational map
h:X\to\bar{X}
is regular. Set
D_{\bar{X}}:=h_{*}(D_{X})
. Note that for a very general fiber
(X_{y},D_{X_{y}})
, we have

\kappa(K_{X_{y}}+D_{X_{y}})=0.

We let
D_{\bar{X}}=D_{\bar{X}}^{\rm vert}+D_{\bar{X}}^{\rm hor}
be its vertical and horizontal components over
J
, and for a general fiber
\bar{X}_{y}
of
\bar{j}
, we denote by
D_{\bar{X}_{y}}:=D_{\bar{X}}|_{\bar{X}_{y}}
.
Note that by applying Lemma
7.5
,

D_{\bar{X}}^{\rm hor}\subset p^{-1}(D_{A})
where
p:\bar{X}\to A
is the induced morphism (and these divisors agree on a general fiber
\bar{X}_{y}
).

We now analyze
D_{\bar{X}}^{\rm vert}
. Since
{\rm Supp}(D_{A})\supset{\rm Supp}(\pi^{*}D_{\mathcal{A}})
and
(X,D_{X})\to(A,D_{A})
is an orbifold morphism, then we have

{\rm Supp}(\lfloor D_{\bar{X}}\rfloor)\supset(\pi\circ p)^{*}D_{\mathcal{A}}.

Note that

(\pi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{A}^{0})=J^{0}\times_{\mathcal{A}}A,

we thus have
{\rm Supp}(\lfloor D_{\bar{X}}^{\rm vert}\rfloor)\supset\bar{j}^{*}(\partial J)
. If
P
is a component of the support of
D_{\bar{X}}^{\rm vert}
not contained in
\bar{j}^{*}(\partial J)
, then
P
intersects
\bar{X}\times_{J}J^{0}
and hence, over
J^{0}
, we have
P=\bar{j}^{-1}(Q)
for some divisor
Q
on
J
not contained in
\partial J
.
Since
j^{0}:J^{0}\times_{\mathcal{A}}A\to J^{0}
is a smooth proper fibration, we may write
D_{\bar{X}}
as :

D_{\bar{X}}=D_{\bar{X}}^{\rm hor}+\bar{j}^{-1}(\partial J)+\sum_{i\in I}(1-\frac{1}{m_{i}})P_{i}

where each
P_{i}
is a prime divisor on
\bar{X}
such that
\bar{j}(P_{i})=Q_{i}
for some prime divisor
Q_{i}\subset J
not contained in
\partial J
. We then have

P_{i}=\bar{j}^{*}Q_{i}+R_{i},

where
R_{i}
is a
\bar{j}
-exceptional divisor supported within
(\bar{j}^{*}\partial J)_{\text{red}}
. The orbifold base of
\bar{j}:(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}})\to J
, is given by

D_{J}:=\Delta(\bar{j},D_{\bar{X}})=\sum_{i\in I}(1-\frac{1}{m_{i}})Q_{i}+\partial J.

Claim 7.7
.

We have
\kappa(K_{\bar{X}}+D_{\bar{X}})=\kappa(K_{J}+D_{J})
.

Démonstration.

We begin by observing that

(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}})
has a good minimal model over
J
. To see this we will apply
[
HX13
, Theorem 1.1]
. To this end, note that if
G=\sum_{i\in I}Q_{i}+\partial J
, then
(X,D_{X}-\epsilon j^{*}G)
is dlt for any
0<\epsilon\ll 1
and all log canonical centers of
(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}}-\epsilon\bar{j}^{*}G)
dominate
J
. By
[
HX13
, Theorem 1.1]

(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}}-\epsilon\bar{j}^{*}G)
has a good minimal model over
J
. Since
K_{\bar{X}}+D_{\bar{X}}-\epsilon\bar{j}^{*}G\equiv_{J}K_{\bar{X}}+D_{\bar{X}}
, this is also a good minimal model over
J
for
(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}})
. We denote this by
\phi:\bar{X}\dasharrow\bar{X}^{\prime}
where
\bar{j}^{\prime}:\bar{X}^{\prime}\to J^{\prime}
is the induced morphism and
\psi:J^{\prime}\to J
is a birational morphism. We now have
K_{\bar{X}^{\prime}}+D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}}\sim_{\mathbb{Q},J^{\prime}}0
so that we can apply the canonical bundle formula. Note that
\phi
is an isomorphism over
J^{0}
(which we henceforth identify with
\psi^{-1}(J^{0})
) and
(\bar{X}^{\prime},D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}})\to J^{\prime}
is isotrivial (over the open subset
J^{0}
). Therefore, the moduli part is trivial and so, by the canonical bundle formula, we have
K_{\bar{X}^{\prime}}+D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}}=(j^{\prime})^{*}(K_{J^{\prime}}+B_{J^{\prime}})
where
B_{J^{\prime}}
is the boundary divisor whose coefficients over codimension 1 points
Q
are computed by
1-{\rm lct}_{Q}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}};(\bar{j}^{\prime})^{*}Q)
.
Here

{\rm lct}_{Q}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}};(\bar{j}^{\prime})^{*}Q):={\rm sup}\{t\geq 0|(\bar{X}^{\prime},D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}}+t\bar{j}^{*}Q)\ {\rm is\ lc\ over}\ \eta_{Q}\}.

Since any divisor contained in the inverse image of
\partial J^{\prime}=\psi^{-1}(\partial J)
has coefficient 1 in
D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}}
,
it follows immediately that
{\rm lct}_{Q}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}};(\bar{j}^{\prime})^{*}Q)=0
for any divisor in
\partial J^{\prime}
. If
Q
is not contained in
\partial J^{\prime}
then we can identify it with a divisor on
J^{0}
and hence (over
\eta_{Q}
)
(\bar{j}^{\prime})^{*}Q=P
for some prime divisor
P
on
\bar{X}^{\prime}
. If
m
is the multiplicity of
D_{\bar{X}}
along
P
(which is the same as the multiplicity of
D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}}
along
P
), then, the log canonical threshold is

{\rm lct}_{Q}(\bar{X}^{\prime},D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}};(\bar{j}^{\prime})^{*}Q)={\rm sup}\{t\geq 0|1-\frac{1}{m}+t\leq 1\}=\frac{1}{m}.

Therefore the coefficient of
B_{J^{\prime}}
along
Q
is
1-\frac{1}{m}
. Thus
B_{J^{\prime}}=\Delta(g,D_{\bar{X}})
where
g:\bar{X}\to J^{\prime}
is the induced map.

Finally note that since
(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}})
is log canonical and
\phi
is a
K_{\bar{X}}+D_{\bar{X}}
minimal model, then

\displaystyle\dim J=\kappa(K_{X}+D)=\kappa(K_{\bar{X}}+D_{\bar{X}})=\kappa(K_{\bar{X}^{\prime}}+D_{\bar{X}^{\prime}})=

(7.7.1)

\displaystyle\kappa(K_{J^{\prime}}+B_{J^{\prime}})\geq\kappa(K_{J}+B_{J})=\kappa(K_{J}+D_{J})\geq\dim J.

So
\kappa(K_{\bar{X}}+D_{\bar{X}})=\kappa(K_{J}+D_{J})
.
∎

It follows from (
7.7.1
) that the orbifold base
(J,D_{J})
of
\bar{j}:(\bar{X},D_{\bar{X}})\to J
is of general type. The proposition is proved.
∎

7.4
Some reductions

To prove
E
, we will replace
Y_{0}
with suitable finite étale covers and some birational models.

We start with the following lemma, whose proof is standard (cf.
[
CDY25b
, Lemma 4.8]
) so we omit it.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
\nu:({\widehat{X}},D_{\widehat{X}})\to(X,D_{X})
be a generically finite surjective orbifold morphism between smooth orbifolds. Let
D\subset\lfloor D_{X}\rfloor
be such that
{\widehat{X}}\backslash\hat{D}\to X\backslash D
is a finite étale morphism, where
\hat{D}:=\nu^{-1}(D)
. Write
{\widehat{X}}_{0}:={\widehat{X}}\backslash\hat{D}
and
X_{0}:=X\backslash D
. If
\nu^{*}D_{X}\cap{\widehat{X}}_{0}=D_{\widehat{X}}\cap{\widehat{X}}_{0}
, then we have

\kappa(K_{X}+D_{X})=\kappa(K_{\widehat{X}}+D_{\widehat{X}}).

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
c:Y\to X
be an algebraic fiber space between smooth projective varieties. Let
D_{Y}
be a reduced simple normal crossings divisor on
Y
. Let
(X,D_{X})
be the orbifold base of
(Y,D_{Y})
, and assume that the support of
D_{X}
has simple normal crossings. Let
\nu:{\widehat{X}}\to X
be a generically finite surjective morphism from a smooth projective variety
{\widehat{X}}
such that
\nu^{-1}(D_{X})
has simple normal crossings support, and let
D\subset\lfloor D_{X}\rfloor
be a divisor such that the restriction
{\widehat{X}}\backslash\hat{D}\to X\backslash D
is a finite étale morphism, where
\hat{D}:=\nu^{-1}(D)
. Assume further that
c^{-1}(D)\subset D_{Y}
. Let
\mu:{\widehat{Y}}\to Y
be the natural morphism from a strong resolution of singularities
{\widehat{Y}}\to{\widehat{X}}\times_{X}Y
, chosen such that
D_{{\widehat{Y}}}:=\mu^{-1}(D_{Y})
is also a simple normal crossings divisor. Then, letting
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})
be the orbifold base of the natural morphism
\hat{c}:({\widehat{Y}},D_{{\widehat{Y}}})\to{\widehat{X}}
, we have

\kappa(K_{X}+D_{X})=\kappa(K_{{\widehat{X}}}+D_{{\widehat{X}}}).

Démonstration.

Write
Y_{0}:=Y\backslash D_{Y}
. Since
c^{-1}(D)\subset D_{Y}
, the morphism
{\widehat{X}}\backslash\hat{D}\to X\backslash D
is finite étale, and
{\widehat{Y}}\to{\widehat{X}}\times_{X}Y
is a strong resolution of singularities, it follows that
{\widehat{Y}}_{0}:=\mu^{-1}(Y_{0})
is étale over
Y_{0}
. Furthermore, because the induced map
\pi_{1}(Y)\to\pi_{1}(X)
is surjective,
{\widehat{Y}}
is connected. Since we have the Cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{Y}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{X}}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}

where the vertical morphisms are étale, and since
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})
is the orbifold base of
\hat{c}:({\widehat{Y}},D_{{\widehat{Y}}})\to{\widehat{X}}
, it follows that

\nu^{*}D_{X}\cap{\widehat{X}}_{0}=D_{{\widehat{X}}}\cap{\widehat{X}}_{0}.

Note that since
\hat{c}^{-1}(\hat{D})\subset D_{{\widehat{Y}}}
, it follows that
\hat{D}\subset\lfloor D_{{\widehat{X}}}\rfloor
. Hence,
\nu:({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})\to(X,D_{X})
is an orbifold morphism. The lemma then follows from
7.8
.∎

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
\mu_{0}:X_{0}\to Y_{0}
be a birational morphism between smooth quasi-projective varieties that is proper over a big open subset of
Y_{0}
. If
X_{0}
is special, then
Y_{0}
is special.

Démonstration.

Let
X
and
Y
be smooth projective compactifications of
X_{0}
and
Y_{0}
, respectively, such that
D_{X}:=X\backslash X_{0}
and
D_{Y}:=Y\backslash Y_{0}
are simple normal crossings divisors, and such that
\mu_{0}
extends to a morphism
\mu:X\to Y
. By assumption, there exists a Zariski closed subset
Z\subset Y_{0}
of codimension at least two such that
\mu_{0}
is proper over
Y_{0}\backslash Z
. Then, for any prime divisor
E
contained in
D_{X}
, if
\mu(E)
is not contained in
D_{Y}
, it follows that
\mu(E)
must be contained in the closure of
Z
in
Y
, and hence has codimension at least two. Therefore, we have
\mu_{*}(D_{X})=D_{Y}
. Thus,
\mu:(X,D_{X})\to(Y,D_{Y})
is an elementary birational morphism between orbifolds (see
2.2
). Since specialness is defined via birational models of orbifolds (see
2.9
), it follows that
(X,D_{X})
is special if and only if
(Y,D_{Y})
is special.∎

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
\mu_{0}:X_{0}\to Y_{0}
be a birational morphism between smooth quasi-projective varieties that is proper over a big open subset of
Y_{0}
. Then to prove
E
for
Y_{0}
, it suffices to prove
E
for
X_{0}
.

Démonstration.

By assumption, the induced map
\pi_{1}(X_{0})\to\pi_{1}(Y_{0})
is an isomorphism. Thus, their quasi-Albanese varieties coincide, yielding the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

where
a
and
b
are their respective quasi-Albanese maps. If
Y_{0}
is special, then by
7.10
,
X_{0}
is also special. By
proposition

2.13
,
a
and
b
are both dominant morphisms with connected general fibers. Let
Z
be the Zariski closed subset of
Y_{0}
of codimension at least two such that
\mu_{0}
is proper over
Y_{0}\backslash Z
. It follows that for a general fiber
Y_{0,t}:=b^{-1}(t)
of
b
, the intersection
Y_{0,t}\cap Z
has codimension at least two in
Y_{0,t}
. Therefore, the restriction of
\mu_{0}
to the general fiber
X_{0,t}:=a^{-1}(t)
is a birational morphism
X_{0,t}\to Y_{0,t}
that is proper over a big open subset. Hence, by
7.10
,
X_{0,t}
is special if and only if
Y_{0,t}
is special.∎

This lemma enables us to take birational models of orbifolds in the proof of
E
.

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
c:Y\to X
be an algebraic fiber space between smooth projective varieties. Let
f:(Y,D_{Y})\to(A,D_{A})
be a morphism between smooth orbifolds with
D_{A}=(D_{A})_{\rm red}
. Assume that we have a commuting tower of algebraic fiber spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Consider the orbifold divisor
D_{X}:=\Delta(c,D_{Y})
of
c:(Y,D_{Y})\to X
, which we assume has simple normal crossings support. If
a\in A
is a general point, let
c_{a}:Y_{a}\to X_{a}
denote the restriction of
c
to the fibers
Y_{a}
and
X_{a}
, and let
D_{X,a}
denote the restriction of
D_{X}
to
X_{a}
. We then have :

D_{X,a}\geq D_{c_{a}},

where
D_{c_{a}}=\Delta(c_{a},D_{Y,a})
is the orbifold divisor of
c_{a}:(Y_{a},D_{Y,a})\to X_{a}
. In particular, if
c
is the relative core of
(Y,D_{Y})\to A
, then the general fibers of
(X,D_{X})\to A
are of general type.

Démonstration.

Let
D_{i}
be a component of
D_{X}
such that
g(D_{i})=A
. We write the decomposition :

c^{*}(D_{i})=\sum_{j\in J}m_{j}^{(i)}D_{j}^{(i)}+R_{i}

where the divisors
D_{j}^{(i)}
(contained in
Y
) map surjectively onto
D_{i}
, and where
R_{i}
is
c
-exceptional. By restricting this equality to the general fiber
Y_{a}
, it follows that

c_{a}^{*}(D_{i}|_{X_{a}})=c^{*}(D_{i})|_{Y_{a}}=\sum_{j\in J}m_{j}^{(i)}D_{j}^{(i)}|_{Y_{a}}+R_{i}|_{Y_{a}}

where
c_{a}(D_{j}^{(i)}|_{Y_{a}})=D_{i}|_{X_{a}}
. We observe that the set of divisors over which the infimum is taken to calculate the multiplicity of
D_{i}|_{X_{a}}
in
D_{c_{a}}
is greater than or equal to the set corresponding to
J
. Indeed, certain components of
R_{i}|_{Y_{a}}
may no longer be
c_{a}
-exceptional. This yields the stated inequality.∎

7.5
Suitable base changes

Lemma 7.13
.

Let
(Y,D_{Y})
be a smooth orbifold with
D_{Y}=(D_{Y})_{\rm red}
. Assume that we have an orbifold morphism
a:(Y,D_{Y})\to(A,D_{A})
with connected fibers, where
A
is a toroidal compactification of a semiabelian variety
A^{0}:=A\backslash D_{A}
. Let
c:(Y,D_{Y})\to X
be an algebraic fiber space such that its orbifold base
(X,D_{X})
is smooth. We assume the following :

—

There exists an algebraic fiber space
f:X\to A
such that
f\circ c=a
.

—

There exists a birational morphism
g:Y\to Y^{\prime}
onto a smooth projective variety such that
c
is neat relative to
g
. Moreover, there exists a morphism
a^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\to A
such that the following diagram commutes :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

—

The Iitaka fibration
j:X\to J
of
(X,D_{X})
is a regular morphism, where
J
is a smooth projective variety.

—

For a very general fiber
(X_{y},D_{X_{y}}):=(X,D_{X})|_{X_{y}}
of
j:X\to J
, the restriction
f_{y}:=f|_{X_{y}}:X_{y}\to A
is generically finite onto its image.

Then there exists a surjective isogeny
A_{1}^{0}\to A^{0}
of semi-abelian varieties together with suitable extended morphism between their toroidal compactifications
A_{1}\to A
as in
7.2
such that the following holds : let
X^{0}:=X\backslash f^{-1}(D_{A})
and
Y^{0}:=Y\backslash D_{Y}
. Let
{\widehat{X}}^{0}:=X^{0}\times_{A^{0}}A_{1}^{0}
and let
{\widehat{X}}
and
{\widehat{Y}}
be suitable strong desingularizations of
X\times_{A}A_{1}
and
Y\times_{A}A_{1}
such that
{\widehat{X}}\backslash{\widehat{X}}^{0}
and
D_{{\widehat{Y}}}:={\widehat{Y}}\backslash\mu^{-1}(Y^{0})
are both simple normal crossing divisors, admitting a natural morphism
\hat{c}:{\widehat{Y}}\to{\widehat{X}}
. Let
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})
be the orbifold base of
\hat{c}:({\widehat{Y}},D_{{\widehat{Y}}})\to{\widehat{X}}
. Let
{\widehat{X}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{j}}}{{\to}}\hat{J}\to J
be the Stein factorization of
j\circ\nu:{\widehat{X}}\to J
. Then
\hat{j}:{\widehat{X}}\to\hat{J}
is the Iitaka fibration of
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})
, and for a general
y\in\hat{J}
, the induced morphism
{\widehat{X}}_{y}\to A_{1}
is birational onto its image, where
A_{1}
is a suitable toroidal compactification of
A_{1}^{0}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widehat{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y\times_{A}A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{A}A_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

Démonstration.

Write
D:=f^{-1}(D_{A})
. Since
a
is a morphism of orbifolds, it follows that
D_{Y}\geq a^{-1}(D_{A})
. Since
(X,D_{X})
is the orbifold base of
c:(Y,D_{Y})\to X
, we have

D_{X}\geq f^{-1}(D_{A})=:D.

Let
D_{y}:=D|_{X_{y}}
and
D_{X_{y}}:=D_{X}|_{X_{y}}
for a general
y\in J
. Since
f_{y}
is generically finite onto its image, and
j:X\to J
is the Iitaka fibration of
(X,D_{X})
, we have

0\leq\kappa(K_{X_{y}}+D_{y})\leq\kappa(K_{X_{y}}+D_{X_{y}})=0.

Then
\bar{\kappa}(X^{0}_{y})=\kappa(K_{X_{y}}+D_{y})=0
, where
X^{0}_{y}
is the fiber of
X^{0}\to J
at
y
. By
[
CDY25a
, Lemmas 1.3, 1.4]
, it follows that the Stein factorization of
X_{y}^{0}\to A^{0}
factors through a proper birational morphism
X^{0}_{y}\to C_{y}^{0}
, and a surjective isogeny
C_{y}^{0}\to B_{y}^{0}
between semi-abelian varieties, where
B_{y}^{0}
is a semi-abelian subvariety of
A^{0}
. Since
A^{0}
contains at most countably many semi-abelian subvarieties, it follows that
B_{y}^{0}
is a translate of a fixed semi-abelian subvariety
B^{0}
of
A^{0}
. In this case,
C_{y}^{0}
does not depend on
y
, and we shall denote by
C^{0}
. By
7.1
, there exists a surjective isogeny
i:A_{1}^{0}\to A^{0}
such that

i^{-1}(B^{0})=C^{0},

and the induced map
A_{1}^{0}/C^{0}\to A^{0}/B^{0}
is an isomorphism.

By
7.2
, after changing the toroidal compactification
A
for
A^{0}
, there exists a toroidal compactification
A_{1}
for
A_{1}^{0}
