such that the surjective isogeny
A_{1}^{0}\to A^{0}
extends to a morphism
A_{1}\to A
. Consider the fiber product
X\times_{A}A_{1}
, which is connected since
\pi_{1}(X)\to\pi_{1}(A)
is surjective. Let
{\widehat{X}}
be a strong resolution of the singularities of
X\times_{A}A_{1}
and let
\nu:{\widehat{X}}\to X
be the induced natural morphism. Set
{\widehat{X}}^{0}:=\nu^{-1}(X^{0})
. Then the restriction
{\widehat{X}}^{0}\to X^{0}
is finite étale.

The fiber of
{\widehat{X}}^{0}\to J
over a general point
y\in J
is exactly the fiber product
X^{0}_{y}\times_{A^{0}}A_{1}^{0}
. Since the image of
X^{0}_{y}
in
A^{0}
is a translate
B^{0}_{y}
of
B^{0}
, and the Stein factorization of
X^{0}_{y}\to B^{0}_{y}
is the composition of a proper birational morphism
X^{0}_{y}\to C^{0}
and the isogeny
C^{0}\to B^{0}\simeq B^{0}_{y}
, it follows that the fiber product
X^{0}_{y}\times_{A^{0}}A_{1}^{0}
decomposes into a disjoint union of connected components, each canonically isomorphic to
X^{0}_{y}
.
Hence, for each connected component, its image under the natural morphism
{\widehat{X}}^{0}\to A_{1}^{0}
is mapped proper birationally to a translate of
C^{0}
.

Let
{\widehat{X}}\xrightarrow{\hat{j}}\hat{J}\to J
be the Stein factorization of
j\circ\nu:{\widehat{X}}\to J
. The general fibers of
{\widehat{X}}\to J
are disjoint unions of the connected general fibers
{\widehat{X}}_{y^{\prime}}
of
\hat{j}
. Recall that a general fiber
{\widehat{X}}^{0}_{y^{\prime}}
of
{\widehat{X}}^{0}\to\hat{J}
is exactly a general fiber of
X^{0}\to J
. Consequently, the restricted morphism
\hat{f}|_{{\widehat{X}}_{y^{\prime}}}:{\widehat{X}}_{y^{\prime}}\to A_{1}
is birational onto its image, where
\hat{f}:{\widehat{X}}\to A_{1}
is the natural morphism. This concludes the first assertion of the lemma.

Finally, we show that
\hat{j}:{\widehat{X}}\to\hat{J}
is the Iitaka fibration of
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})
. By
7.9
, the orbifold base
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})
of
\hat{c}:({\widehat{Y}},D_{{\widehat{Y}}})\to{\widehat{X}}
has the same Kodaira dimension as
(X,D_{X})
. It follows that a general fiber of
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})\to\hat{J}
has zero Kodaira dimension, and thus it is the Iitaka fibration of
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})
.
The lemma is proved.
∎

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
(X,D_{X})
be a smooth orbifold and let
A^{0}
be a semi-abelian variety. Let
A
be a toroidal compactification of
A^{0}
. Assume that
f:(X,D_{X})\to(A,D_{A})
is an orbifold morphism such that
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{A}
and

\lfloor D_{X}\rfloor\geq f^{-1}(D_{A})=:D.

If a general fiber of
f:(X,D_{X})\to A
is of general type, then after replacing
(X,D_{X})
by a birational model, the Iitaka fibration
j:(X,D_{X})\to J
of
(X,D_{X})
is regular, and for a very general fiber
X_{y}
of
j
, the restriction
f|_{X_{y}}:X_{y}\to A
is generically finite onto its image.

Démonstration.

By
7.3
, we have

\kappa(K_{X}+D_{X})\geq\dim X-\dim A>0.

Therefore, we consider the Iitaka fibration
j:(X,D_{X})\dashrightarrow J
of
(X,D_{X})
, where
\dim J=\kappa(K_{X}+D_{X})
. By
[
Cam11
, Théorème 3.9]
, after replacing
(X,D_{X})
by a birational model,
j
is a regular morphism, and a very general fiber
(X_{y},D_{X_{y}})
of
j
is a smooth orbifold of Kodaira dimension zero.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X,D_{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Let
h_{y}:\Gamma_{y}\to f(X_{y})
be a desingularization such that
D_{\Gamma_{y}}:=h_{y}^{-1}(D_{A})
is a simple normal crossing divisor. We replace
(X_{y},D_{X_{y}})
by a birational model such that the induced rational map
X_{y}\dashrightarrow\Gamma_{y}
becomes regular. Note that
f_{y}:(X_{y},D_{X_{y}})\to(\Gamma_{y},D_{\Gamma_{y}})
is an orbifold morphism.

Assume by contradiction that the lemma fails. For a general
a\in\Gamma_{y}
, we denote by
(X_{y,a},D_{X_{y,a}})
the fiber of
f_{y}=f|_{X_{y}}:(X_{y},D_{X_{y}})\to\Gamma_{y}
. Note that a general fiber
(X_{a},D_{X_{a}})
of
f:(X,D_{X})\to A
is of general type, and that
(X_{a},D_{X_{a}})
is covered by
\bigcup_{\{y\mid a\in f(X_{y})\}}(X_{y,a},D_{X_{y,a}})
. Then by
[
Cam11
, Théorème 9.12]
, for a fixed general
y\in J
, we have that
(X_{y,a},D_{X_{y,a}})
is of general type for a general
a\in\Gamma_{y}
.

Note that
\Gamma_{y}\backslash D_{\Gamma_{y}}
is birational to a subvariety of the semi-abelian variety
A^{0}
. It follows that

\kappa(K_{\Gamma_{y}},D_{\Gamma_{y}})\geq 0.

Applying
7.4
to
f_{y}:(X_{y},D_{X_{y}})\to(\Gamma_{y},D_{\Gamma_{y}})
, we conclude that

0=\kappa(X_{y},D_{X_{y}})\geq\dim X_{y,a}+\kappa(K_{\Gamma_{y}}+D_{\Gamma_{y}}),

and hence
\dim X_{y,a}=0
, so that
f_{y}:X_{y}\to f(X_{y})
is generically finite. The lemma is proved.
∎

7.6
Proof of
E

Theorem 7.15
.

Let
Y_{0}
be smooth quasi-projective variety. If
Y_{0}
is special, then a general fiber of its quasi-Albanese map is also special.

Démonstration.

We adapt the proof of
[
CC16
, Théorème 2.4]
, with suitable modifications to the quasi-projective setting. Let
a_{0}:Y_{0}\to A^{0}
be the quasi-Albanese map. By
proposition

2.13
,
a_{0}
is dominant with connected fibers.
We let
(A,D_{A})
be a toroidal compactification of
A^{0}
. We may also pick a smooth compactification
Y
with
D_{Y}:=Y\backslash Y_{0}
being simple normal crossing such that the induced map
a:Y\to A
is an algebraic fiber space and
D_{Y}=Y\backslash Y_{0}
is a reduced divisor with simple normal crossings. In particular
a:(Y,D_{Y})\to(A,D_{A})
is an orbifold morphism.

We proceed by contradiction and assume that general fibers of
a_{0}
are not special. Then for any surjective isogeny
\varphi:A_{1}^{0}\to A^{0}
from another semi-abelian variety, the general fibers of
Y_{0}\times_{A^{0}}A_{1}^{0}\to A_{1}^{0}
are also not special. Let
A_{1}
be a toroidal compactification for
A_{1}^{0}
and we take a smooth projective compactification
Y_{\varphi}
for
Y_{0}\times_{A^{0}}A_{1}^{0}\to A_{1}^{0}
such that the boundary
D_{Y_{\varphi}}:=Y_{\varphi}\backslash Y_{0}\times_{A^{0}}A_{1}^{0}
is a simple normal crossing divisor.

By Theorem
2.10
, we may consider the relative core map of
(Y_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}})
over
A_{1}
:

c_{\varphi}:Y_{\varphi}\dasharrow X_{\varphi}:=C(Y_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}}/A_{1}).

Replacing
c_{\varphi}
by an appropriate birational model, we may assume that
c_{\varphi}
is a regular, neat, and high fibration. Let
(X_{\varphi},D_{X_{\varphi}})
be the orbifold base of
c_{\varphi}:(Y_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}})\to X_{\varphi}
. In this case, by
2.7
, we have

\kappa(X_{\varphi},D_{X_{\varphi}})=\kappa(c_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}}).

(7.15.1)

Note that the equivalence class of the fibration
c_{\varphi}
is uniquely determined by
\varphi
up to birational equivalence.

We define a bimeromorphic invariant associated with
Y_{0}
by

\mathcal{K}(Y_{0}):=\inf_{\varphi:A_{1}^{0}\to A^{0}}\{\kappa(c_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}})\},

(7.15.2)

where
\kappa(c_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}})
is the canonical dimension of
c_{\varphi}
defined in
2.6
, and the infimum is taken over all surjective isogenies
\varphi:A_{1}^{0}\to A^{0}
from semi-abelian varieties to
A^{0}
.

Claim 7.16
.

We have
\mathcal{K}(Y_{0})>0
.

Démonstration.

By Theorem
2.10
and
7.12
, the general orbifold fibers
(X_{\varphi,y},D_{X_{\varphi,y}}):=(X_{\varphi},D_{X_{\varphi}})|_{X_{\varphi,y}}
of the natural morphism
(X_{\varphi},D_{X_{\varphi}})\to A_{1}
are of general type. Hence, by
7.3
and (
), we obtain

\kappa(c_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}})=\kappa(X_{\varphi},D_{X_{\varphi}})\geq\dim X_{\varphi}-\dim A_{1}.

By our assumption and construction, for each
\varphi
, the general fibers of
(Y_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}})\to A_{1}
are not special. Since
c_{\varphi}
is its relative core map, the dimension of its base must be strictly greater than the dimension of
A_{1}
, yielding

\dim X_{\varphi}-\dim A_{1}>0.

The claim is thus proved.
∎

Since
Y_{\varphi}\setminus D_{Y_{\varphi}}
is birational to a finite étale cover of
Y_{0}
via a birational morphism that is proper over a big open subset, the pair
(Y_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}})
remains special by
[
Cam11
, Proposition 10.11]
and
7.10
. Furthermore, the general fibers of the morphism
Y_{\varphi}\setminus D_{Y_{\varphi}}\to A_{1}^{0}
are birational to those of
Y_{0}\to A^{0}
, and this birational equivalence is also proper over a big open subset. Thus, by
7.11
, the proof of the theorem reduces to showing that the general fibers of
(Y_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}})\to A_{1}
are special. To this end, we choose a specific isogeny
\varphi:A_{1}^{0}\to A^{0}
that attains the infimum of
\mathcal{K}(Y_{0})
, that is, we have

\kappa(c_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}})=\mathcal{K}(Y_{0}).

To simplify notation, we replace the original
(Y,D_{Y})\to A
with this minimizing
(Y_{\varphi},D_{Y_{\varphi}})\to A_{1}
. Thus, we have

\kappa(c,D_{Y})=\mathcal{K}(Y_{0}).

(7.16.1)

Furthermore, by
proposition

2.8
, we may assume that :

(a)

c:(Y,D_{Y})\to X
is
neat and high
,

(b)

the orbifold base
(X,D_{X}:=\Delta(c,D_{Y}))
is smooth,

(c)

the Iitaka fibration of
(X,D_{X})
is a regular morphism
j:X\to J
.

By
2.7
and our minimizing assumption, we have

\kappa(X,D_{X})=\kappa(c,D_{Y})=\mathcal{K}(Y_{0}).

(7.16.2)

By Theorem
2.10
and
7.12
, the general orbifold fibers
(X_{y},D_{X_{y}}):=(X,D_{X})|_{X_{y}}
of
f:(X,D_{X})\to A
are of general type. By
7.14
, for a general fiber
X_{y}
of
j
, the restricted morphism
f|_{X_{y}}:X_{y}\to A
is generically finite onto its image.

We now apply
7.13
. There exists a new surjective isogeny
A_{1}^{0}\to A^{0}
of semi-abelian varieties such that if
X^{0}:=X\setminus f^{-1}(D_{A})
and
Y^{0}:=Y\setminus D_{Y}
,
{\widehat{X}}^{0}:=X^{0}\times_{A^{0}}A_{1}^{0}
,
{\widehat{X}}
a suitable smooth projective compactification of
{\widehat{X}}^{0}
such that if

{\widehat{X}}\setminus{\widehat{X}}^{0}
is a simple normal crossing divisor and
{\widehat{Y}}\to{\widehat{X}}\times_{X}Y
a strong desingularization, admitting natural morphisms
\nu:{\widehat{X}}\to X
,
\hat{c}:{\widehat{Y}}\to{\widehat{X}}
, and
\mu:{\widehat{Y}}\to Y
such that
D_{{\widehat{Y}}}:={\widehat{Y}}\setminus\mu^{-1}(Y^{0})
is a simple normal crossing divisor,
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})
the orbifold base of
\hat{c}:({\widehat{Y}},D_{{\widehat{Y}}})\to{\widehat{X}}
,
{\widehat{X}}\xrightarrow{\hat{j}}\hat{J}\to J
the Stein factorization of
j\circ\nu:{\widehat{X}}\to J
, then
\hat{j}:{\widehat{X}}\to\hat{J}
is the Iitaka fibration of
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})
, and for a general point
y\in\hat{J}
, the induced morphism
{\widehat{X}}_{y}\to A_{1}
is birational onto its image, where
A_{1}
is a suitable toroidal compactification of
A_{1}^{0}
.

Crucially, because
\mathcal{K}(Y_{0})
was defined as the absolute infimum over all isogenies, and by utilizing (
), we obtain :

\kappa(\hat{c},D_{{\widehat{Y}}})=\kappa(X,D_{X})=\kappa({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}}).

(7.16.3)

This fact is essential ; as we will see later (by
2.5.(ii)
and
2.6
), for any further high birational model of
({\widehat{Y}},D_{{\widehat{Y}}})\to{\widehat{X}}
, the Kodaira dimension of the resulting orbifold base remains equal to
\kappa(X,D_{X})
.

To simplify the presentation in what follows, we will drop the hats and rename
({\widehat{Y}},D_{{\widehat{Y}}})\to({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})\to\hat{J}
and
({\widehat{X}},D_{{\widehat{X}}})\to A_{1}
simply as
(Y,D_{Y})\to(X,D_{X})\to J
and
f:(X,D_{X})\to A
.

We also note that
f:(X,D_{X})\to(A,D_{A})
is an orbifold morphism. To see this, let
Q
be any prime divisor in the support of
D_{A}
and
P
a prime divisor in the support of
f^{*}Q
. If
E
is a prime divisor contained in the support of
c^{*}P
, then it is contained in the support of
a^{*}Q
, and so
m_{E}(D_{Y})=+\infty
. This implies that
m_{P}(D_{X})=+\infty
and so
(X,D_{X})\to(A,D_{A})
is an orbifold morphism.

Consider the following commutative diagram where each vertical map is a birational modification and
Y^{\prime}
,
X^{\prime}
and
J^{\prime}
are smooth,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

(7.16.4)

Set
D_{Y^{\prime}}:=\mu^{-1}(D_{Y})+{\rm Ex}(\mu)
. Then
\mu:(Y^{\prime},D_{Y^{\prime}})\to(Y,D_{Y})
is an elementary orbifold morphism. By
2.5.(ii)
, we have

\displaystyle\kappa(c,D_{Y})=\kappa(c^{\prime},D_{Y^{\prime}})\leq\kappa(X^{\prime},\Delta(c^{\prime},D_{Y^{\prime}}))\leq\kappa(X,\Delta(c,D_{Y}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq :achievemininal3}}}{{=}}\kappa(c,D_{Y})

(7.16.5)

This implies that the map
j^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\rightarrow J^{\prime}
is the Iitaka fibration of the orbifold base
\left(X^{\prime},D_{X^{\prime}}=\Delta\left(c^{\prime},D_{Y^{\prime}}\right)\right)
of
c^{\prime}
. Based on this remark, we are free to replace
J
by birational models and perform base changes as in (
).

We can therefore apply
proposition

7.6
to the morphism
f:(X,D_{X})\to A
, and we will use the notation introduced there without recalling it. We deduce that we may assume that there is a birational morphism
h:X\rightarrow\bar{X}
constructed as in the proof of
proposition

7.6
. In this case, we have a morphism
\bar{j}:\bar{X}\to J
together with a simple normal crossing divisor
\partial J
on
J
such that

\bar{j}
is a locally trivial fibration over
J^{0}:=J\backslash\partial J
. Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\bar{f}^{-1}(D_{A})\supset\bar{j}^{-1}(\partial J).

(7.16.6)

If we let if
D_{\bar{X}}=h_{*}(D_{X})
, and
D_{J}=\Delta(\bar{j},D_{\bar{X}})
, then we have

\displaystyle\kappa(K_{J}+D_{J})=\dim J.

Moreover,
f:X\to A
factors through
\bar{f}:\bar{X}\to A
.

To prove that
Y
is not special, we need to construct a neat model for
j\circ c
. By the Raynaud-Hironaka theorem, there exists a smooth birational modification
i:J^{\prime}\to J
such that the main component of
Y\times_{J}J^{\prime}
dominating
J^{\prime}
is flat over
J^{\prime}
. Let
\mu:\tilde{X}\to\bar{X}^{\prime}=\bar{X}\times_{J}J^{\prime}
be a strong resolution of singularities. Let
\tilde{Y}
be a desingularizaton of the main component
(Y\times_{J}J^{\prime})_{\rm main}
of
Y\times_{J}J^{\prime}
, such that the induced map
\tilde{Y}\to\tilde{X}
is regular.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y\times_{J}J^{\prime})_{\rm main}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{c}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{j}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{f}}

We remark that
\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c}
is neat. Indeed, for any
(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c})
-exceptional divisor
P
in
\tilde{Y}
, its image in
(Y\times_{J}J^{\prime})_{\rm main}
has codimension at least two since
(Y\times_{J}J^{\prime})_{\rm main}
of
Y\times_{J}J^{\prime}\to J^{\prime}
is flat. Hence
\nu(P)
has codimension at least two, and thus
\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c}
is neat.

We set

\displaystyle D_{\tilde{Y}}:=\nu^{-1}_{*}D_{Y}+{\rm Ex}(\nu).

(7.16.7)

and
D_{\tilde{X}}:=\Delta(\tilde{c},D_{\tilde{Y}})
the orbifold divisor of
\tilde{c}
. By
2.5.(ii)
and (
7.16.5
),
\tilde{j}
is the Iitaka fibration of
K_{\tilde{X}}+D_{\tilde{X}}
. Hence by the same arguments in the proof of
7.7
, if we let
D_{J^{\prime}}:=\Delta(\tilde{j},D_{\tilde{X}})
, then we have

\displaystyle\kappa(K_{J^{\prime}}+D_{J^{\prime}})=\dim J^{\prime}.

(7.16.8)

Claim 7.17
.

We have the following equality of orbifold base divisors

\Delta(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c},D_{\tilde{Y}})=D_{J^{\prime}}.

Démonstration.

Write
\partial J^{\prime}:=J^{\prime}\backslash i^{-1}(J^{0})
and let
\tilde{f}:\tilde{X}\to A
be the composition
\bar{f}\circ\mu^{\prime}\circ\mu
. By (
7.16.6
), we have

\displaystyle\tilde{D}:=\tilde{f}^{-1}(D_{A})\geq\tilde{j}^{-1}(\partial J^{\prime}).

(7.17.1)

Let
Q
be any prime divisor on
{J^{\prime}}
. We first assume that
Q
is not contained in
\partial J^{\prime}
.
Since
\bar{j}:\bar{X}\to J
is a smooth fibration over
J^{0}
, and
\mu:\tilde{X}\to\bar{X}^{\prime}=\bar{X}\times_{J}J^{\prime}
is a strong resolution of singularities, it follows that
\tilde{j}
is smooth over
J^{\prime}\backslash\partial J^{\prime}
. Then

\tilde{j}^{*}Q=P+R

for some prime divisor
P
on
\tilde{X}
and some
\tilde{j}
-exceptional divisor
R
contained in
\tilde{D}
by (
7.17.1
), such that
\tilde{j}(P)=Q
. Recall that

D_{Y}\geq a^{-1}(D_{A}).

Hence we have

\displaystyle D_{\tilde{Y}}\geq(a\circ\nu)^{-1}(D_{A})=\tilde{c}^{-1}(\tilde{D}).

(7.17.2)

This implies that

\displaystyle(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c})^{*}Q=\tilde{c}^{*}(P+R)=\tilde{c}^{*}(P)+\sum_{i}a_{i}E_{i}

(7.17.3)

where each
E_{i}
is supported in
D_{\tilde{Y}}
. Hence the multiplicity of
D_{\tilde{Y}}
along
E_{i}
is

m_{E_{i}}(D_{\tilde{Y}})=+\infty.

We remark that for any
\tilde{c}
-exceptional prime divisor
E
, one has

m_{E}(D_{\tilde{Y}})=+\infty.

Indeed, since
(Y\times_{J}J^{\prime})_{\rm main}\to\bar{X}^{\prime}
is flat and
(\mu\circ\tilde{c})(E)
has codimension at least two, it follows that
\nu_{0}(E)
has codimension at least two in
(Y\times_{J}J^{\prime})_{\rm main}
. Consequently,
\nu(E)
also has codimension at least two. Hence
m_{E}(D_{\tilde{Y}})=+\infty
by (
7.16.7
).

Therefore, by
2.1
, the multiplicity of the orbifold divisor
\Delta(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c},D_{\tilde{Y}})
along
Q
is

m_{Q}\left(\Delta(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c},D_{\tilde{Y}})\right)=\inf_{G_{i}}\{m_{i}m_{G_{i}}(D_{\tilde{Y}})\mid(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c})(G_{i})=Q\},

where
G_{i}
ranges over all prime divisors in
\tilde{Y}
such that
(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c})(G_{i})=Q
, and we set

m_{i}=m(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c},G_{i})

that is the
multiplicity
of
\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c}
along
G_{i}
defined in
2.1
.
Note that if
G_{i}
does not dominate
P
via
\tilde{c}
, we have already seen that
m_{G_{i}}(D_{\tilde{Y}})=+\infty
. It follows that

m_{Q}\left(\Delta(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c},D_{\tilde{Y}})\right)=\inf_{G_{i}}\{m_{i}m_{G_{i}}(D_{\tilde{Y}})\mid\tilde{c}(G_{i})=P\},

where
G_{i}
now ranges over all prime divisors in
\tilde{Y}
satisfying
\tilde{c}(G_{i})=P
. By (
7.17.3
), for such
G_{i}
, we have

m_{i}=m(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c},G_{i})=m(\tilde{c},G_{i}).

Consequently, we obtain

m_{Q}\left(\Delta(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c},D_{\tilde{Y}})\right)=m_{P}\left(\Delta(\tilde{c},D_{\tilde{Y}})\right).

We now assume that
Q
is contained in
\partial J^{\prime}
. By (
7.17.2
),
we have

\lfloor\Delta(\tilde{c},D_{\tilde{Y}})\rfloor\geq\tilde{D}:=\tilde{f}^{-1}(D_{A})\geq\tilde{j}^{-1}(\partial J^{\prime}).

Together with
D_{\tilde{Y}}\geq\tilde{c}^{-1}(\tilde{D})
, this implies that

m_{Q}\left(\Delta(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c},D_{\tilde{Y}})\right)=m_{Q}\left(\Delta(\tilde{j},D_{\tilde{X}})\right)=+\infty.

The claim is thus proved.
∎

Therefore, we have

\kappa(J^{\prime},\Delta(\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c},D_{\tilde{Y}}))=\kappa(J^{\prime},D_{J^{\prime}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq :canonicalbundle}}}{{=}}\dim J^{\prime}>0.

Since
\tilde{j}\circ\tilde{c}
is neat, by
2.9
,
(\tilde{Y},D_{\tilde{Y}})
is not special. This implies that,
Y_{0}
is not special, and this is a contradiction. Therefore the general fibers of
a_{0}
are special. The theorem is proved.
∎
